design can begin with the screenwriting process, although it is
relatively rare for this to happen. In many projects, it is not until postproduction that sonic mood begins to be properly considered. The
question of whether enough room has been left for the sound
designer to have a meaningful influence on the piece is often more a
case of luck than judgement. In the worst-case scenarios, a director
may come to the sound designer with a desperate desire to
transform a scene emotionally through sound, but constant dialogue
and visuals packed with information leave little room for manoeuvre.
If the audience is continually being asked to extract meaning from
words and pictures, they cannot use the full potential of their hearing,
due to the hierarchy of perception in the mind. It is only when we do
not get the full story from language and image that non-verbal
sounds come into focus, so space must be allowed for sound if the
director feels it is particularly important to a scene. Randy Thom
articulates the ideal situation, happening within the unconscious:
‘What I’m seeing isn’t giving me enough information. What I’m
hearing is ambiguous, too. But the combination of the two seems to
be pointing in the direction of a vaguely familiar container into which I
can pour my experience and make something I never before quite
imagined.’

#### **Narrative Tone**


One mood-related element that often comes up in sound design
conversations is _tone_ - the filmmakers’ feelings, or ‘angle’, on what
is happening. Should an event have a sense of comedy to it, or
should we play it straight? Is it more effective to be generous to an
audience in a given scene, or should we hold back? And do we really
mean a certain thing, or are we being ironic? Sound can help to
crystallize this perspective, just as tone of voice adds extra cues for
the listener whenever a person speaks.

Exploring the conventions of using rain as an image, we soon run
into a cinematic cliché, rhyming with the tears of a character as it
runs down the windowpane. If the tone of this scene is sincere, the
sound can be shaped to enhance the sense of sadness. If an ironic
effect is desired, we could use mismatched music, or comically
exaggerated streaking down the glass, to subvert a straight
performance. When thinking about tone, we keep in mind that the
elements of cinema are relational – none of them exists in isolation,
and each serves to shift and modulate the overall meaning and
effect. A director will often look to balance mood and tone through
the sound design, which, along with music, visual effects and colour
grade, offers a final chance for reshaping.

#### **Tonal Shifts**


Emotional changes in films often evolve slowly, but at times a
sudden change of approach is used. This is known as a _tonal shift_ . It
may be for dramatic effect – as in _Sorry to Bother You_ ’s
unexpectedly grotesque turn midway through – or for the purposes
of comedy, as, for example, in a serious countryside scene that is
undermined by an animal sound at just the right moment. By turning
a corner sharply, the resulting surprise can be a very effective
visceral tool, but we must take care to avoid doing this accidentally,
slipping from the sublime to the ridiculous through lack of control.
Sound can sometimes be responsible for these lapses. Any poorly
handled moments in sound design can bring the audience out of the
film, destroying the magic of the moment.


#### **Dramatic Irony and Suspense**

Counterpoint and deliberate anticlimax are both forms of irony,
working contrary to what is expected. A different mode is found in
_dramatic irony_, where the audience is allowed to know more than the
characters about what is unfolding. This creates a particular form of
tension, as the onlooker waits on tenterhooks for the penny to drop
in the mind of the character. Non-diegetic sounds play a particularly
important role here. For example, the iconic score of _Jaws_ warns the
audience of the hidden antagonist’s approach, whilst those within the
film remain oblivious.


What are they looking at? By controlling if, when and how unseen information is delivered
through sound, it is possible to create suspense.


This suspension of information, creating excitement or anxiety, is
a key element of mood. Alfred Hitchcock often allowed his characters
to know more than us, an aspect of his reputation as the master of
suspense. He used sound to create this effect during the concert hall
finale of _The Man Who Knew Too Much_, in which the orchestra


drowns out the lead characters’ on-screen plotting. Our innate
curiosity to know what is happening brings a sense of urgency,
propelling the scene forward. Likewise, if a character becomes
aware of something off screen, the director might choose to delay, or
withhold the revelation of the source, to keep an audience guessing.

### **WORKING WITH MUSIC**


Alongside sound design, music is a key maker of mood, often able to
express itself more freely when it comes to the image. On smaller
productions, a single person may be responsible for both areas, but
usually musical selections and creations are handled by a separate
department. Within this team, a composer writes new pieces, known
as _music cues_, whilst a music supervisor deals with commercial
tracks. A music editor works to fit both to the picture.

Ideally, the departments collaborate closely from pre-production
onwards, with ideas and drafts being shared along the way.
Composition and sound design are effectively painting in the same
frame, but the boundary can become especially blurred if the ideas
step beyond the naturalistic world of the action. Sometimes, due to
tight schedules, the first meeting of the teams does not occur until
the final mix, at which point any incompatibilities become
depressingly apparent, requiring quick and dirty solutions.

Some composer–sound designer pairings manage this process
more sensibly. One notable example is the collaboration of Skip
Lievsay and Carter Burwell, who often work within the same studio
complex on productions for the Coen Brothers. In the scenes of _No_
_Country for Old Men_, and _Barton Fink_ ’s ‘through the sinkhole’
sequence, it can be difficult to tell where the work of one ends, and
that of the other begins. Central to their process is early discussion
of where a particular sound effect or music cue should be present,
and which area of the frequency spectrum – low, mid or high – it will
occupy. Elements at similar pitches can clash, and so by first splitting
up the frequency spectrum scene-to-scene, sound and music can
dovetail more elegantly. Carter Burwell recalls the positive aspects of


working on _Barton Fink_ : ‘It was one of the most pleasant mixes I ever
did. Everything’s very clean, clear, it was a beautiful sound
environment that was created, and it was because of planning. And
there’s no reason why composers and sound effects people can’t do
this all the time.’

Conversations can also be had about rhythm. If the plan is to use
distinctive patterns of percussive sound effects, such as clocks or
artillery fire, in a scene, it would usually be wise to avoid beat-driven
music at the same time, and vice versa. And an early sense of the
score’s musical keys can also prove a helpful resource for designers,
enabling the re-pitching of sound elements to suit.

#### **Pre-Existing Tracks**


For scenes in which characters need to respond to music directly, as
in the sing-alongs of _American Honey_, the music may be played
back during the shoot. If the director is keen to use the on-set
recordings, it may be a good idea to capture the scene as a one-shot
or with multiple cameras, to avoid the potential minefield of continuity
issues. More often, pre-existing tracks are added during the early
stages of post, allowing editors to work their cuts accordingly.
Choices made after the shoot are subject to change during post, as
each track’s usage must be approved, with record labels providing a
_master licence_ to the specific recording, and songwriters agreeing to
a _publishing licence_ .

In some cases, the sound department may also offer options for
diegetic music, otherwise known as _source music_ . The sense of a
character’s personality might be enhanced by musically styling their
phone ring tone, doorbell or alarm clock. For textural elements –
radios, televisions, parties next door – a sound designer may delve
into their own creations, or rely on the public domain, to find a track
that helps create the right mood. Or we might experiment with
adding a musical instrument to an environment, courtesy of a busker,
wind chimes, or a music box (such a familiar cliché of the horror
genre).


#### **Worldizing**

Once the music selections have been made, the clean recordings
can be placed within their scenes by applying reverb and EQ,
simulating the acoustics of a diegetic space. This is a convenient
and effective option, although more interesting results may be
achieved using a process called _worldizing_, pioneered by Walter
Murch on _American Graffiti._ For this technique, the music is played
over a speaker into a physical space and the results are recorded; it
is similar to using an echo chamber in a studio. This is usually done
with a mono speaker and a stereo microphone, experimenting with
placement to achieve the desired result. Different set-ups can be
captured to give a variety of options, and the effect can later be
softened by mixing some of the clean signal back in. In order to
emulate a sound-colouring device – a phone, television or car radio,
for example – we can use a similar real-world machine for the
playback.

#### **Diegetic Switching**


One related effect that can bring a playful tone to a scene is a
_diegetic switch_ . It is a device beloved of many directors, where
source music transitions into score, or vice versa, in a wink to the
audience that acknowledges the artifice of cinema. This can either
be done by cutting or crossfading between clean and worldized
recordings or by employing different versions of the music itself.
During Spock’s funeral in _Star Trek II_, we hear Scottie playing
_Amazing Grace_ on his bagpipes. As the coffin shoots out of the USS
Enterprise, an orchestra picks up the melody. Rather than assuming
that there is a spacecraft just out of frame that contains an entire
symphonic ensemble, the audience readily accepts the melody’s
ascent through the layers of cinematic reality.

### **ACOUSMATIC SOUND**


Moving into the non-diegetic sphere, alongside narration and score
there is _acousmatic sound_ . These are effects that have no apparent
source within the scene. Genres that deal in supernatural forces, or
the workings of the mind, may use this kind of element to
communicate unseen events, or create ambiguous moods. The
_rumble_ also falls under this heading – not being tied to the image,
the sounds instead determine their own shape, in response to the
narrative beats of a scene. The overlaid sounds of classic cartoons
use a similar device, although in this case it is played for laughs –
the tick tock of the character’s thoughts, the ‘ting’ of the lightbulb
moment, the tweeting birds that appear after a bump on the head.

In the non-diegetic space, the boundary between sound design
and music can become particularly blurred. This creates a potentially
delicate political situation with the composer, but it also provides
fertile ground for magical transformations. One such case is _Stalker_ ’s
first foray into ‘The Zone’, a mysterious realm in which conventional
logic breaks down. Here the sound of a railroad cart becomes
increasingly melodic in feel, transporting the audience into dreamlike territory.

The sound designer can begin to explore emotions within the
different narrative layers once they have read a script. Just as
cinematographers create mood boards to explore visual
atmospheres, we can follow a similar process, offering our
collaborators a sense of what we will bring to the creative process.
This is often best done with elements that remain unseen –
ambience and non-diegetics – worlds that we must build from
scratch, regardless of when we are brought on to a project. Any
sounds we design during pre-production within these categories are
also simple for an editor to use during the cut, as they do not require
tight sync with the image.

If it suits the tone, the sound designer can be playful with the
form these materials take. For a horror script that contained
references to fictional films, for example, the designers invented
some of their own, creating imaginary scenarios, film posters and
sound tracks to illustrate the overall aesthetic they had in mind. The
sounds were later used by the picture editor as backgrounds to
enhance the emotion of certain scenes. A similar approach can be


used with any unseen elements that are imagined at script stage,
allowing us to create a palette of moods ahead of the shoot. These
sounds can be created by sculpting newly recorded or existing
elements, and by experimenting with audio processing. If we are
taking a psychological or supernatural approach, we are usually
looking for an otherworldly feel. Common techniques to achieve this
are extreme pitch manipulations, layering up sounds in interesting
ways, or adding reverbs that contrast with the diegetic space ( _see_
Chapter 14).


**MOOD ACTIVITY A**


Returning to the _Man vs Sand_ script in Appendix II, come up with places where you
might try using non-diegetic sound to communicate what is happening in the
character’s head.

If you can, make some audio recordings to experiment practically with different
moments, finding interesting sound sources that can create the desired mood, before
playing with software processing to push the sounds into more surreal territory.


For aesthetic reasons, a director may choose to avoid score and
other non-diegetic sounds altogether, but this should not necessarily
be viewed as an unhelpful restriction. The off-screen diegetic space
still allows for great creativity and such limitations can prove to be
the inspiration for really interesting designs. In fact, the opposite
case is more often a problem, where a director comes to rely too
heavily on underscoring elements. As Walter Murch suggests, ‘If a
film becomes too dependent on music to create the emotion, there’s
a kind of steroid-like artificiality that comes into play. The audience,
without knowing it, begins to feel manipulated. “They want me to feel
sad, so they play sad music.”’ Striking a delicate balance between
expression and restraint, the aim should be to confirm an emotion,
rather than transplant one that is not already present in the diegetic
elements.


In all its forms, mood allows us to express story without words, using
the primal territory of instinct and feeling. On top of this, we can
influence audiences using the lens of tone, creating a sense of our
attitude towards events. And by exploring the non-diegetic domain,


we can represent less concrete forms of experience, transcending
the constraints of cause and effect, in a space of boundless
possibility.


_[OceanofPDF.com](https://oceanofpdf.com/)_


# **5** **SYMBOL**
## **_SOUND WORLDS AND_** **_METAPHORS_**

All things are symbols.


_(Henry W. Longfellow)_


Delving deeper into meaning and message, we find increasingly
enigmatic channels, offering greater interactivity to the listener.
When we deliver information directly, the signs we use are universal

- for example, alarm clock means wake up, laughter follows humour

- but through symbols we create our own coded languages,
designed specifically for each film. This metaphorical approach,
using one thing to refer to another, opens hidden worlds of
significance for an audience to interpret, rather than being prodded
by the filmmakers towards a fixed result.


Hiroshige, U., _Eight Shadow Figures_ (1842). THE MINNEAPOLIS INSTITUTE OF ART


But why adopt metaphor, giving up some control over a film’s
message, rather than just ‘saying what we mean’?


The words of director Wim Wenders provide one answer to this
question:


A film only exists when it’s reflected in someone’s heart. That’s why I often sit in a
cinema and watch some mindless thing on the screen and see people stare at it just
as mindlessly, and I realize: this film does not even exist. It provokes no
repercussions, no encounters. It’s a waste, not only of time. But if a film leaves the
space for the spectator to dream himself or herself into it, to read ‘between the lines,’
to fill it with his or her own experiences and thereby make it come to life, _then_ and
only then it exists.


This view suggests that the true stuff of cinema is the interaction
between image, sound and audience. A film comes into being only
when it is experienced. If the material does not respect those to
whom it will play, spoon-feeding meaning with no gaps or intrigue,
boredom is the likely result. But if room is allowed for multiple
interpretations, a work’s meaning becomes richer, expanding within
the imagination of the audience.

Another perspective comes through in the collaborations of
director David Lynch and sound designer Alan Splet, which make
distinctive use of metaphor in films such as _Eraserhead_ and _The_
_Elephant Man_ . These works contain a high degree of ambiguity, with
much left open to interpretation. Certain metaphors are repeating
themes – industrial images and sounds form symbolic threads,
commonly interpreted along the lines of dreams and the unconscious
mind. The machines can be heard as rhymes for the mechanics of
the brain, with the recordings often pitched down, giving them a
strange, otherworldly feel and transporting us to places that defy
straightforward readings. In this way, cinematic symbols can be used
to explore mysterious regions whose truths cannot be articulated in
more literal terms.

### **SOUND WORLDS**


For social satire _Parasite_, designer Ralph Tae-Young Choi was
tasked with developing distinctive aesthetics for the spaces of two
families – one rich, the other poor. The hard-up family’s home is
subjected to a constant stream of sounds – aggressive dogs,


passing cars and noisy neighbours – breaking in from the outside.
By contrast, only birdsong and a gentle breeze flow into the wealthy
household, while the interior sounds are given lavish, immersive
reverb, lending a sense of scale and openness. The doors and gates
of the property were also given particular attention in the design,
acting as a source of ‘metaphorical sound representing the
difference between the haves and have-nots’.

This kind of approach makes use of _sound worlds_  - sets of
audible qualities that mark out the different territories of a story. Just
as a production designer might use colour themes to represent the
essence of a character, a sound designer can evoke the underlying
nature of things with sound.

A sound world can serve any aspect of story, including:










the domain of a **character, group, time** or **place** (wealthy elite,
future, hell);
a **story theme** (social structure, technology, religion);
a side of a **narrative conflict** (rich vs poor, reality vs fantasy, life
vs death).



The aim is to fill each world with sonic ideas that connect with its
subject. These might be aesthetic qualities:










pitch (low, high);
rhythm (regular, irregular);
timbre (bassy, bright);
style (realist, hyperrealist, surrealist).



Or we can choose apt sound sources, or individual effects that
resonate with the theme.

For example, worlds of _good_ and _evil_ could be designed by
imagining sounds that chime with each side of the conflict. In the
world of the good we might choose mainly rounded, organic sounds,
of natural origin. The evil world might be filled with bassy and
piercing sounds, of synthetic and industrial sources. We then need to
test these ideas against the environment and aesthetic of the film.
For example, the plan might work well for humans battling robots in a


science fiction, but might be less successful for factory workers
fighting the greed of their bosses in a social realist drama. Ideas
should flow out of the story _content_ (characters, events), as well as
the _context_ (setting, genre).

Once the worlds have been populated with sonic features, we
need to think about how we will apply them. Our chosen qualities of
sound might relate to voice, foley and atmos, or perhaps reveal
themselves in non-diegetic elements, representing the sound world
_acousmatically_ .

If we wanted to develop worlds of power and vulnerability, for
instance, we might apply a guiding principle around soundscape
categories. When the protagonist is in command of their own destiny,
the backgrounds could be filled with the sounds of humanity. But
when the character’s fate is being shaped by forces outside of their
control, the atmos could instead be dominated by the sounds of
Earth.

Whilst demanding a certain level of analysis, this developmental
phase is best seen in a playful light. It is a place to experiment,
where there is still plenty of time to fail and re-imagine. Once postproduction begins, we spend a lot of time reacting – to the picture
edit, to the director’s feedback, to practical constraints – but at this
earlier stage there is a chance to be proactive, led by instinct, story
and creative testing. The aim is to internalize a big picture design
that lays out possible routes to explore, providing a platform upon
which we can pivot. Once we start putting ideas into practice, we will
often discover more interesting avenues, but by making a plan first,
we can better improvise later.


**SYMBOL ACTIVITY A**


Returning to the _Man vs Sand_ script in Appendix II, come up with at least three
appropriate sound worlds, and list as many characteristics as you can for each one.

An expansive list of ideas for dynamic qualities can be found in Appendix III.
As you build your list, think about how your chosen characteristics will apply to
specific sounds within your design.


#### **Sound World Interaction**

The conflicting worlds within a story must provide tension, but
ultimately be reconcilable, as Walter Murch explains: ‘It’s a boldly
stated version of the goal of any film: to present compelling
contradictions and resolve them inventively...If they’re too close to
begin with, they produce a flat, uninteresting metaphor; if they’re so
far apart that you can never resolve them, the whole thing doesn’t
make any sense.’ The peculiar twists of the aforementioned _Parasite_
provide an imaginative example of resolution, with the two clashing
social worlds ultimately becoming fused within the same space.

_Force Majeure_ serves up another case of sparring sound worlds,
in a story about a middle-class family at a ski resort, and the brittle
foundations on which they have built a veneer of perfect living. This
facade is depicted by the predictable, manicured sounds of the
resort interior, with its soft edges and plastic pop music. In stark
contrast are the bassy, angular sounds of storms, mechanics and
booming snow-making machines, along with an equally fierce
musical theme, representing the incendiary truths that are about to
emerge. In a particularly striking scene, the two worlds creep closer
together through intercutting – in matching pyjamas, the family face
the bathroom mirror as the gentle uniform buzz of their toothbrushes
plays off against a wild snowstorm, topped with explosions, as the
scene cuts back and forth between interior and exterior. The
aesthetic contrast builds a strong sense of two worlds that are about
to collide, with only the audience having any idea of what might
happen. It is another case of dramatic irony in action.

#### **Illustrating Ideas**


Thematic relationships can be explored during pre-production using
sound and text, but sometimes it helps to visualize our ideas. We
can chart the chosen sound worlds against one another, to compare
their qualities and test whether we have designed enough separation
between them. One technique is to plot the worlds in quadrants,


made up of two sets of opposing properties ( _see_ the example on the
next page).


_Force Majeure_ sound world comparison, created with the online tool soundmaps.app.


Quadrant illustrations that relate the three sound worlds of _One Last Dance._


To create these illustrations, we can start by looking at the lists of
characteristics we have made to describe each world, spotting any
opposing pairs that crop up between them. It might be bassy on one,
bright on another, realist on one, surrealist on another, and so on.
We can then experiment by combining different sets of these
pairings in quadrants, searching for an interesting spread of worlds,
until we have run out of useful properties to compare. If we find that
there is not enough contrast, it is a good idea to rethink either the
worlds we have chosen, or the properties they have been given.

By creating a good set of relationships, with identities that
connect to the themes and allow for distinctive shifts between
worlds, the design is given a powerful foundation. It is not necessary
to be too literal with how the plans are translated – the most precious
creative concept in the designer’s imagination may fail miserably
when projected into reality, and director, designer or mixer instincts
might reasonably override the framework at any point. However,
boundaries can help guide us towards a result that is dynamic and
meaningful. Ultimately, we will judge each moment on whether it
feels right in context, but by first looking to encode some wide-angle
intelligence into our designs, we can ensure that what emerges in
the end will be greater than the sum of its parts.


**SYMBOL ACTIVITY B**


Create a series of illustrations to compare your chosen _Man vs Sand_ sound worlds
from Activity A, using the quadrant format.


Start by mapping out opposing qualities on each axis, with one pair on the
horizontal and another on the vertical. The two pairs do not need to be related to
each other – you can start by choosing them at random to discover what they might
reveal.

Next, plot your sound worlds, thinking about where they should fall on the fourway spectra. The aim is to make sure we have given each world a distinct aesthetic
identity to help delineate the story. If you find you are plotting your worlds in similar
positions to each other, it may be time for a creative rethink.

Repeat this process until you have experimented with a range of different
qualities, fully exploring the potential contrasts between your sound worlds.

#### **Sound Sketches**


Audio experiments are another way to test ideas during
development, exploring the essence of each sound world through
creative expression. With a focus on mood and feel, the results can
also be useful for communicating our ideas to others.

Making collages using existing sounds is a quick way to cover a
lot of ground, but it is a good idea to do some recording too. The real
world often throws up more interesting possibilities than can be
found in sound libraries, or imaginations. Discoveries of new sounds
can be tested against the themes, to see how they might enhance
our plans. If they do not seem to work in a particular instance, they
may be saved for another time.

A number of years ago, I was tasked with designing a sound
world to represent the delusions of a character traumatized by
childhood memories. The flashbacks contained the visual motif of a
wind chime, which gave me the idea of experimenting with large
sections of chime-like scaffold tubing that we had picked up from a
scrapyard for a previous project. We discovered that by suspending
the tubes and swinging them back and forth, and then layering up
recordings at different pitches, we could create strange undulating
drones to give a sense of being pulled into a warped reality. These
sketches were later refined and used throughout the timeline,
underscoring moments where delusional thoughts were forming in
the character’s mind, whilst hinting at the chimes of the early trauma.


Like many of the symbolic expressions that emerge in the work of
a sound designer, this example was a happy accident that came
about through a process of play. The most creative moments tend to
have a sense of levity to them – if ideas are not coming, it is not
usually worth trying to force them. Instead, move on to something
else, and pick back up once enthusiasm and flow have returned. If
you find visual references helpful, there may be mood boards or
storyboards available for the film, which you could work to. One
option is to use an _animatic,_ a sort of moving storyboard formed by
cutting together a sequence of storyboard frames along a timeline.
This gives a sense of a scene’s pacing and can provide an
instructive visual context if you want to push your sound sketches
into more detailed areas. You could even create a _mood film_,
building an image sequence from the cinematography and
production design mood boards, to which you can add your thematic
sound ideas.

#### **Sound Libraries**


One related technique is the creation of thematic sound libraries for
a film. These are categorized audio collections that provide a rich
resource for the edit. These could be classified according to sound
worlds or using any other system that makes sense. Frank Warner,
designer of _Taxi Driver_ and _Raging Bull_, favoured an abstract
approach: ‘I established a library for each picture, and at the end of
every picture I threw everything away – everything was destroyed...I
gave them musical definitions, colors – it wasn’t only words. I used a
lot of red sounds, blue sounds, green sounds, which meant
something only to me...Red to me was more of a hard, mean sound;
blue could be more passive. Green could be pastoral, very light or
airy. It often depended on what my mood was when I started writing
about my combination of sounds for the picture. I always used
colors.’

These sound libraries can then be given to the film’s editor, who
is responsible for the shaping of both picture and audio in their work,
before they hand over the audible element to the sound team. If the


editor can draw on a sound palette that the sound designer has
come up with specifically for the project, the thematic threads that
they imagine are given an early chance to take root.

#### **Communicating with the Director**


Whilst much of a creative process need only make sense to the
creator, it is always sensible to bounce ideas off the director before
getting too far in. The format to use, and the level of detail to include,
is something you need to judge based on the context, and the
personalities involved. The director’s approach may be very
analytical or more intuitive; they may have a greater interest in
character or tone, and so on. Many filmmakers find it difficult to
conceptualize around sound, so keeping things brief, and focusing
on themes and mood, is often a sensible approach. And rather than
presenting your complete thoughts for approval, the process is best
seen as a discussion, with your materials as a jumping-off point. The
director may also bring their own poetic sound ideas for the sound
designer to work in – for example, Andrei Tarkovsky requested
enigmatic water atmospheres for almost every project he directed.

Timing is also important, with early pre-production usually a good
moment to discuss broader ideas, before the director’s head is too
deep into the details of the shoot. This assumes that the sound
designer has been brought on to the project by this point. As the
majority of the practical work happens in post-production, sound
design can end up being treated as something of an afterthought; it
is important always to push to be part of a film’s growth from its early
stages.

One advantage of laying a creative foundation ahead of the shoot
is that our designs can influence the on-set sound, informing
conversations around how certain moments should be captured and
giving ideas for additional recorded elements. For example, whilst in
pre-production on a psychological horror, we discussed how to mic a
scene of a train entering a tunnel. Two key sound worlds for the film
were _psyche_ and the _external_, and as the moment provided the
transition out of a dream, we decided to foreground the internal,


micing up the inside of the tunnel to play against the exterior camera
perspective. And our idea that the film world’s objects would have
shadow sounds, lurking beneath everyday perception and breaking
through in key moments, was something that later figured in
conversations between director and lead actor.


By finding elements of a story that can be served by metaphoric
sound – often the psychological, philosophical or otherwise unseen –
a sound designer can give life to deeper themes, enriching elements
of subtext. And by linking auditory meanings and qualities to other
aspects of narrative, we help form the cipher with which a film’s
events can be interpreted. This poetic approach of embedding
symbolic patterns into our work can lead to the most profoundly
personal encounters with cinema, both for us and, most importantly,
for the audience.


_[OceanofPDF.com](https://oceanofpdf.com/)_


# **6** **SHAPE**
## **_SOUND MAPS, CONTRAST AND_** **_MOTIF_**

I’m simply interested in what is going to happen next.


_(Kurt Vonnegut)_


So far we have largely looked at elements in isolation, but we also
need to be in control of how things connect through time – the shifts
and evolutions of ideas, themes and feelings that guide an audience
through a story. In this chapter we will be exploring these temporal
dynamics through the lens of _shape_ .


Hiroshige, U., _Fireworks at Ryogoku Bridge_ (1858). JOSEPH PULITZER BEQUEST, 1918


In film, the fundamental expressions of shape are _story_ and
_narrative_, terms that are often used interchangeably, but we should
be aware of how their meanings differ. Whilst story describes the
events that happen to the characters within the film world, narrative


refers to the way in which those events are portrayed – the _telling_ of
the story.


Top-down illustrations of common story shapes, created by designer Maya Eilam.


In the original version of revenge thriller _Irreversible_, the plot is
shown in reverse chronology, opening with a brutal murder, without
any sense of motive. The next scene jumps back in time, giving us
some idea of what is happening, before continuing to work its way
backwards through the plot, scene by scene, until we reach the
story’s set-up. This unusual assembly forces the audience to first
confront the violence, and then get to know the characters whilst
they are still processing it.

The film was later re-released in a new version, which instead
assembled the scenes in chronological order, allowing the audience
to build a sense of empathy with the characters before the violent
turn. The story is the same in both versions, but the narrative unfolds
in a radically different way, making a huge impact on how the events
are experienced and interpreted.

From such fundamental decisions of structure, down to the most
detailed of judgements, every aspect of filmmaking craft combines to
form narrative. It is the job of each department, and ultimately the


director, to ensure that these choices add up to something that works
for the story.

With a top-down perspective, we start by describing an overall
vision, before zooming in to deal with each moment in turn. By
gaining a bird’s-eye view on the timeline, a story’s structure becomes
easier to digest, helping us to explore different patterns within our
design.

### **MAPPING THE SOUND WORLDS**


A system for charting narratives graphically was devised by writer
Kurt Vonnegut, with a view to studying cultures through the shapes
of their stories. On the vertical axis, he positioned the spectrum of
good and ill fortune that befalls the characters, and along the
horizontal axis he plotted time. Applying this technique to a large
number of stories, he identified a set of common patterns, and was
fascinated to find that the story of Cinderella and the Bible shared
the same shape. Each saw incremental gifts delivered by a
supernatural force, then a spectacular fall from grace, before
ultimately resolving in infinite bliss ( _see_ below).


The story shape of the Bible (left) and Cinderella (right), which share an uncanny similarity.


This approach was adapted for sound by designer David
Sonnenschein, who saw that a similar vantage point could be gained
through charting our chosen sound worlds. Which world is the focus


of each scene? Are there shifts that happen within scenes? And
which world ends up on top?

To map these dynamics, a script should be distilled down into a
series of key moments – snapshots of structure that form the
skeleton of a story. There is no need to be too detailed at this stage

- a good set of moments would often be smaller than the number of
scenes. Each moment is given a meaningful label: a dialogue
phrase, an action, an image, or whatever gets to the heart of its
significance for the story. These labels are then written out as a
sequence, moving horizontally along the page, forming a timeline.
For each moment, we can think about which sound worlds should be
present in our design, and how their intensity rises and falls through
time. At the end of this process, we emerge with a map of our initial
design schema, showing how each world’s sonic qualities and
sources might be heard across the film.


Illustrating the sound worlds of _One Last Dance_ using connected dots, with the size of each
circle indicating a world’s strength at that time (using the online tool soundmaps.app).


The sound worlds of _One Last Dance_, illustrated using waves, with the height of each point
communicating the world’s power over the story in each moment.


This process of mapping allows us to externalize the story
dynamics that we have in our head, seeing how they feel on the
page. New ideas will often emerge during this dry run. If we want to
push the approach further, we can animate the charts, to give a
sense of our compressed timeline in action, and overlay our sound
sketches to bring an audible sense of themes at work.

Sound mapping is best done from script, developing ideas and
exploring the story during the more relaxed period before production
begins. The final edit is likely to have a different structure, perhaps
drastically so, and at that stage the sound designer can decide
whether or not to adapt their maps to the final shape. This may not
be the best use of energy – once the edit is ready for us, there may
be serious time pressures, and we are likely to have already
absorbed most of the benefits by this point. However, if there is time
to play with, there can be value in finalizing the chart. Online
mapping tools will provide efficient ways to reorder a sequence.


**SHAPE ACTIVITY A**


Picking up the _Man vs Sand_ script in Appendix II, draw a sound world map, using the
sound worlds you chose in the previous chapter’s activities.

Think about each world in turn – where should its sounds be most prominent, and
how should it develop over the course of the story?

You can do this on paper. Alternatively, there are sound mapping tools online,
which may include options for animating the charts.

### **CONNECTING TIME**


Sound also offers some powerful devices for performing narrative
operations on a timeline. By using rhythms of variation and similarity
to make or break connections, it is possible to move moments
together or apart in terms of meaning and emotion. _Motif_,
_foreshadowing_ and _contrast_ are three such tools.

#### **Motif**


The _sound motif_ is a recurring sonic symbol that weaves its way
through a timeline. To create a motif, we first form an initial meaning
by attaching a sound to a particular experience, idea or other
narrative element. When we want to echo that meaning, reminding
the audience of the initial moment, the sound is reintroduced. With
this technique it is possible to tunnel through time to link different
parts of a story together.

The power of this technique on the mind is well illustrated by the
curious case of Pavlov’s dogs. Ivan Pavlov was a scientist best
known for his research on the reflex system, which included canine
conditioning experiments. The dogs were given meals whilst hearing
a particular sound. Different studies used bells, whistles and tuning
forks, producing their sound in sync with the appearance of food.
The researchers found that initially the dogs would salivate only
when the food was revealed. However, as time went on and the
association between sounds and meals grew, they began to salivate
when hearing the sounds alone. The striking of a bell, the shriek of a
whistle or the ‘ting’ of a tuning fork came to mean the same thing as


food for the dogs, and they reacted accordingly. The scientists were
effectively creating metaphoric sound relationships to trigger
physiological, perhaps even emotional, responses in their fourlegged audience.

The example of Pavlov’s dogs demonstrates the manipulative
power of motif, but within our minds the symbols can give rise to
much more complex meanings. In _A Man Escaped_, the striking of a
bell is associated with the attempt of a prisoner to escape his
captors. In this case, it recalls the bell of a tram that he tries to
board, before being recaptured. Throughout the rest of the film, we
hear the same chime whenever the idea of escape is in the
character’s mind, a continual reminder of his desire for freedom. And
whilst the meaning is always an echo of the original escape, each
refrain feels different, responding to what the character, and
audience, have experienced since. In this way we can think of motif
as a tool of development, as much as retrospection – each repetition
brings a new chance for interpretation and reflection on how things
have changed.

The chiming of a bell is again used as motif in _Akira_, impressively
creating a more abstract meaning. The character of Akira appears in
the film as a disembodied consciousness. He was once an ordinary
human, but is now an entity whose unlimited psychic powers have
seen him outgrow the need for physical form. The film’s events
centre around the initially more earthly character Tetsuo, who begins
to develop strange powers of his own. In Tetsuo’s clairvoyant
moments we initially hear an almost musical exclamation of a name

- _Akira!_ - alongside a bell, somehow connecting the two characters.
In later scenes we hear the chime alone, by which time, as with
Pavlov’s food, the name is no longer necessary to trigger the
response. What is interesting here is the complexity of the meaning;
is the motif a communication from another dimension, a foretelling of
later events, a metaphor for psychic powers in general, or all of the
above? The audience have to figure out for themselves what it all
means, and this mystery is a big part of the film’s enduring appeal.

Figuring in all three examples, the bell is perhaps an obvious
candidate for motif, given its rich symbolic history – used for dividing
time, calling towards and warning against – adding metaphoric to


literal resonance. It is also possible to create motifs with any
conceivable sound source – a garden sprinkler in _We Need to Talk_
_About Kevin_, magpies in _Lady Macbeth_ - and their effects range
from subliminal to unmissable. _Star Wars_ is famed for its use of
sonic symbols. In a scene from _The Empire Strikes Back_, we see
imperial officer Captain Needa being asphyxiated by an off-screen
presence, whose identity we immediately read as Darth Vader via
the iconic motif of his breath. The scene itself has just been set up
by a musical theme, or _leitmotif_ . This is an idea closely aligned with
the operas of Richard Wagner, in which a distinctive musical phrase
becomes associated with a character, and then serves as a signpost
for their imminent arrival on stage. John Williams used this device
extensively in the _Star Wars_ score, writing music to symbolize the
two main narrative worlds of the story: rebels and Empire. The motifs
prepare the audience for whichever world they are entering. They act
rather like an establishing exterior shot before the director cuts to
interior, modulating the viewer’s emotions to suit.

#### **Foreshadowing**


If motif is about connecting moments in time by echoing meaning,
_foreshadowing_ does the inverse, giving a taste of things to come.
This might be a piece of information that serves as a clue to later
events, or something in the mood or tone, to hint at future emotions.

Dialogue is often used to foreshadow plot points, for either
dramatic or comic effect. _Hot Fuzz_ uses this device to set up a twist
at the village fête, pre-empting a gruesome sight gag with a piece of
wordplay. A troublesome reporter’s name is drawn in a raffle, with the
announcement ‘Tim, your number’s up!’ drawing maniacal laughter
from the crowd. As ominous music builds, the clock strikes three and
the fate of the journalist is sealed in violently absurd fashion.

A deeper-rooted example is found in _Force Majeure_, whose
destructive revelations are foreshadowed by a bombastic musical
theme – functioning in a similar way to _Jaws_ - and the explosive
sounds of piste control. After the initial exposé, these sounds return


as a combined foreshadow-motif, calling back to the unleashed
truths, whilst also taking aim at other lying dogs to awaken.

These are instances that draw attention to themselves, but other
examples may go under the radar on first viewing. In _Scream_, when
the teenage killer is introduced during an innocuous domestic scene,
the song _Don’t Fear the Reaper_ can be heard in the background, in a
whimsical anticipation of later events. This kind of approach offers a
chance to be playful with an audience, scattering hints along the
path. Repeat viewings then offer the added satisfaction of picking up
clues as they emerge.

#### **Contrast and Turning Points**


If we want to pull narrative elements apart, instead of connecting
them, we can make use of _contrast_ - in order to say something
strongly, making the opposite point immediately before is a way to
underline the intended meaning. In the opening to _Ivan’s Childhood_,
for example, a dreamy sequence sees Ivan playing in a forest,
amidst the sound of cuckoos and a romantic score. Suddenly he
awakes, and we are thrust into his reality as a war-time spy, in a
world filled with gunfire and ominous music. The sharp contrast
between these two sequences communicates the gap between what
we feel childhood should be about, and the character’s actual
situation. By first showing us the idealized dream, the reality is all the
more shocking.

Other moments of contrast come in dramatic story developments,
where there is a clear before and after, with no going back. Such
extreme moments of change – a realization, a birth, a death – are
known as _turning points_ . Special attention should be given to these
events, as we explore what they mean for the characters, and often
make sharp folds in the design to reflect the shift. As David
Sonnenschein advises, ‘Ask yourself about the predominant feeling
before and after this transition...How would the audience participate
more in the world of a character with respect to a shift of sounds?’

Contrast is again at work in the opposing musical themes of _Star_
_Wars_, and in our use of sound worlds to delineate a story. Contrast


may also be unleashed within a sound motif itself, through
subversion, or inversion, to show how things have moved on. For
example, if the song of a caged bird is used as a symbol for a
character feeling trapped, a newfound sense of freedom could later
be underscored with the sounds of a dawn chorus streaming through
an open window.

#### **Connected Time Charts**


The visual mapping technique can be used to explore contrast and
motif. Using different icons for each narrative device, we can
experiment with different patterns and configurations, as we work our
way through a timeline ( _see_ below).


Connected time chart example for _One Last Dance_, using diamonds for motifs, triangles for
foreshadows and arrows for turning points.


As is the case with our explorations of symbolism ( _see_ Chapter
5), we need to decide whether to share this material with our
collaborators. Some directors will be willing to engage with ideas in
this form, whilst others may prefer a simple list of the scenes in
which we imagine our motifs will figure. When working alongside a
sound team, depending on the individuals involved, the maps might
serve as helpful guides, or it may prove more beneficial to allow the
editors to work intuitively within their process, shepherding them
towards our desired result through regular review sessions.


**SHAPE ACTIVITY B**


Draw a timeline map to explore how you might use motif, foreshadowing and contrast
for _Man vs Sand_ .

Could you use sound to set up later elements of the story, or call back to previous
ones? Are there any dramatic turning points in which the sound should
instantaneously shift?

You can draw your chart on paper, or make use of online sound mapping tools.


Through each thematic thread in our design, we support and
enhance the clash of forces, shifts and resolutions of a story. And
using patterns of likeness and difference, we paint within the time
domain, embedding joins and disconnects into the storytelling. By
first exploring these contours from a wider perspective, we can map
out the territories we wish to create, in preparation for the detailed
work to come.


_[OceanofPDF.com](https://oceanofpdf.com/)_


# **7** **VOICE (PRODUCTION)**
## **_THE ART OF ON-SET RECORDING_**

There are voices, and then everything else.


_(Michel Chion)_


When the brain processes sound, it magnifies the expressive details
of speech, demanding great attention in the work of the sound
designer. This sensitivity can make dialogue an unforgiving zone at
times, but it also offers rich possibilities for storytelling. In addition,
unlike other areas of sound designed to speak to audiences
subconsciously, voice provides the most direct route into the
awareness of the listener.


Lequeu, J.J., _Le Grand Bailleur_ (before 1825). BIBLIOTHÈQUE NATIONALE DE FRANCE


### **FROM SCRIPT TO MIX**

To understand the sound designer’s role in shaping a film’s voices,
we should first look at the long and circuitous journey of dialogue
through the filmmaking process. Every project runs slightly
differently, but the standard model goes like this:



1.


2.


3.


4.


5.



**Script development:** the process of drafting and rewriting a
script, usually with the support of the film’s producers. The sound
designer is not usually involved at this point, but a regular writerdirector collaborator may choose to send early versions their way
for sound-related feedback. The designer might suggest a
character is given mysterious power by their voice being heard
before they are seen; they might offer thoughts on where the
image could help set up a sound motif, or recommend dialogue
breaks in scenes that would benefit from more visceral design.
**Pre-production:** once the script has taken shape, the director
may rework scenes with actors in rehearsals, and production
issues may cause other changes, resulting in the _shooting script._
Detailed plans for capturing the performances can then be
finalized, in preparation for the shoot.
**Production:** dialogue may continue to evolve on set, with
performers ad-libbing, or exploring line variations across different
takes. The sound team works together to capture each line as
cleanly as possible, whilst gathering as much additional material
as they can.
**Picture edit:** in post, the editor creates the sequence of images,
whilst working closely with dialogue to shape character and plot.
Additional off-screen lines may also be written in at this stage, as
a way to experiment with the flow of information through the film
and to fix any plot issues that may become apparent once the
edit is assembled.
**Dialogue edit:** once the sound post team picks up the baton, the
dialogue editor works extensively with the existing material, and
oversees additional recording sessions for creative and technical


6.



enhancements. The eventual aim is for a seamless edit of voices
that best tell the story.
**Re-recording mix:** in this final stage, all of the edited tracks are
combined, balanced and bedded into the film. High-quality sound
effects and music are of huge importance to a great mix, but,
where present, voice is almost always the focus.


#### **Dialogue Variation**

The director’s approach to dialogue performance can have a big
impact on the post-production _workflow –_ the way we choose to map
out our technical process – so it is a good idea to find out early on
whether lines might be varied on set. This point is illustrated by the
example of the _One Last Dance_ script:

```
  JOHN gazes into her eyes. In a whisper:
          JOHN
  I miss you.

```

‘I miss you’ medium shot audio.


Whispered lines can be difficult to capture, due to their relatively low
level compared to the background noise. This issue is compounded
if the picture editor uses a medium or wide shot, meaning the sound
team may be positioned much further from the actor than desired.

If the sync sound for a shot is unusable, there are three potential
solutions in the sound team’s toolbox: alts, wild tracks or ADR.

#### _ALTS_


The line may also have been covered in closer setups, which would
usually have cleaner sync sound. The timing will not be identical, but
it is often close enough and can be made to work through careful
editing.


‘I miss you’ close-up shot audio.


This process of substituting alternative lines from the _rushes_ (all
of the material gathered on set) is known as using _alts_ .

However, if the actors are changing up their on-screen dialogue,
the approach becomes less viable, as fitting the alt to the image
requires a closely matching line.

#### _WILD TRACKS_

Extra lines, or re-dos of problem takes, can be recorded on set,
without the camera team being involved. These are known as _wild_
_tracks_ . Whereas sync sound is shackled to the visual requirements,
a wild track is freed from the leash of the image. This offers certain
advantages: they can be recorded in a quiet area of the location,
with crew noise eliminated, and mics can be held as close to the
actors as needed. If they are performed soon after a scene’s
shooting, actors tend to repeat their timings closely enough for use
as alts.


‘I’ve missed you’ wild track.


Returning to the _One Last Dance_ example, if the actor delivers
the line as written in the close-up but changes it to ‘I’ve missed you’
for the medium, that variation would be an important element to wild
track.

#### _ADR_

The most radical, and expensive, option is to re-record the line in
post, using a process known as _automated dialogue replacement_, or
_ADR_ . This technique requires a certain level of experience from ADR
supervisor and performer to achieve a convincing result. Movement,
pitch, breathiness and projection must all closely match the original
performance to ensure the re-recorded line does not stick out as
ADR.


‘I’ve missed you’ ADR.

#### **Improvisation**


On some projects, dialogue may be ad-libbed, with each take having
its own interpretation of the scene. This can rule out any plan to use
alts, as there may not be much similarity between lines in the rushes,
and wild tracking every variation is not usually practical. Any
unusable recordings must instead be resolved by the editor finding a
different visual take with usable dialogue, or through ADR. This
means that, in the case of improvised films, it is advisable to allow
for additional budget and actor availability for dialogue replacement
in post, to place a greater focus on _radio mics_ (worn on the actors’
bodies, ensuring close proximity and so a louder signal compared to
any background noise), and to give greater significance to sound
department concerns about shooting locations.

### **PLANNING FOR THE SHOOT**


Whilst sound designers have a hand in all creative aspects of audio,
they are not usually involved directly in the shoot. The production
sound department runs their own show, but designers do need a
basic grasp of the process for pre-production discussions, and to get
the most out of the tracks in post. It is also becoming increasingly
common for freelance designers to begin their career with a balance
of production and post work. A shoot tends to offer better rates
initially, especially if you can rent your own sound kit to the
production, and it also provides an opportunity to make industry
connections on set.


An illustration of how a recorder interface might have looked for the van scene from _One_
_Last Dance_ . The display includes the 2-track mix (LR) and iso tracks (1–8), with meters
indicating the audio level for each track.


The person in charge of sound capture during a shoot is the
_production sound mixer_, who determines the choice and placement
of mics for each shot, and balances levels to create a 2-track mix of
each take. These mixes will later be synced up with the picture
rushes, helping the editor decide which takes to use during the cut.
Modern recording devices also allow for additional tracks to be
captured separately, allowing for mics to be isolated down the line.
These _iso tracks_ give the post team far greater control than a 2-track
mix would allow.

On bigger productions, the mixer works alongside a _sound_
_recordist_ who operates the recording devices. On smaller
productions, the same person may fulfil both roles, with the
mixer/recordist labels used somewhat interchangeably, depending
on the conventions of the specific industry sector. The department
also includes a _first assistant sound_ ( _1AS_ ), who acts as the boom
operator ( _boom op_ ), ‘swinging’ a lightweight, extendable pole to
control the position of the _boom mic_, sets up other mics, ensuring
they cannot be seen by the camera, alongside other responsibilities.
Larger teams may add a _second assistant sound_ ( _2AS_ ) and a
trainee, who take on less sensitive roles, such as looking after the kit
and providing general assistance.

#### **Recording Strategies**


During pre-production, the mixer uses their experience to consider
the best approaches for recording the production. Each real-world
situation is different, and it is not an exact science, but there are
some basics that apply in most cases.


In wide shots it is rare that the boom can get close enough to capture clean dialogue. In this
case, it might be necessary to rely upon the lavs, or to plant a mic near the actors.
Alternatively, where the mouth of the actors cannot be seen clearly, it would be easy to cut
in wild tracks, or the sync sound from tighter shots.


The boom is often considered the best sounding option, where it
can get close enough to the action, without being seen in the shot or
blocking lights to create visible shadows. A distance of one to four
feet from an actor is ideal, although certain camera setups will make
this impossible, most commonly in a wide, _master shot_, or in spaces
where a reflective surface would reveal the boom from any angle.

In these cases, there may be a greater reliance on radio mics,
also known as _lavalier mics_ ( _lavs_ ), after a type of necklace, as they
are generally hidden on the chest or collar. If the actors are
stationary, and cables can be hidden from the frame, wired lavs are
sometimes used. These may provide a higher quality signal than
their radio equivalent, free from any potential interference. However,
wireless lavs are much more common. Both wired and wireless lavs
have the advantage of always being close to the actor, minimizing
background noise and reverb, but they can sound ‘chesty’ due to
their placement on the body, and may also pick up clothing rustle
caused by actors’ movements.

A third option is to use _plant mics_, hidden in the set or just out of
frame to pick up dialogue the boom cannot reach. An additional


boom op may also be brought in for _double booming_, enabling the
simultaneous capture of actors in different parts of the frame.

#### **Preparation**


The production sound team prepares by familiarizing themselves
with the script, planning the recording of each setup and building a
list of the kit they will need. Deciding the best way to record a scene
can begin once the associated shot list, storyboards and floor plans
are provided by the camera department. The shot list gives each
camera setup ( _slate_ ) a number and specifies frame size, angle and
camera movement. The boards visualize this information, whilst also
adding a sense of composition within the frame. These details can
be combined with the location layout and positions of actors, lights
and camera shown on the floor plans, to get a clear picture of where
mics can be placed.


Floor plans created by recordist Alfred Wales, giving an overview of the setups, showing
how camera, actors and mics will move around the space.


Boom and plant mics can then be marked up on the floor plans,
alongside room mics if the space has an interesting acoustic that the
mixer wishes to capture. For studio shoots it may also be possible to
work with the set designer to build in more ideal places for planting.


A stereo pair of overhead room mics can capture more reverb to blend with the closer mics.
This is especially useful if a musical performance is being recorded for a scene.


Not every shot will have associated floor plans. Movement may
instead be worked out on the day of the shoot, or scenes may be
designated as _run-and-gun_, in which the crew responds instinctively.

If a shot list marks a setup as _MOS_ (an acronym with disputed
origins), this means that someone has decided it should be shot


mute. Unless there is an obvious reason for this, it should be
queried, as it is nearly always worth picking up sync sound, even if
only as a guide. We never know what we might end up needing –
ideas always evolve during the cut – and sync can be painstaking to
recreate in post.

#### **Locations**


Someone from the sound team should ideally attend the initial
location scouts, flagging any that are unsuited to sound recording
before they are confirmed. This might be due to a nearby busy road,
crowded area or flight path that will slow down a shoot or make
things unworkable. During later tech scouts, or _recces_, full
investigations can then be made into eliminating background noise –
turning off fridges, covering air gaps – and for interiors, acoustics
should be assessed. Excessive or unpleasant reverb can sometimes
be tamed by hanging blankets or duvets out of frame, or the art
department could be asked to include carpets, rugs and soft
furnishings in their plans for dressing a space. If it is not possible to
scout a location in person, photos and videos can help with spotting
any issues, whilst also making it easier to visualize the setups and
action.

Even if the location itself is quiet, background noise may be
generated by the crew, or sometimes by extras in the frame. It is
worth having some rolls of carpet on hand to lay along the line of a
camera team’s movement for a shot, and asking them to remove
their shoes. This can also be helpful for the actors, where the floor is
not visible. If it is in shot, pads may be affixed to the soles of the
actors’ shoes, to minimize footsteps in the on-set sound and allow for
greater foley flexibility later. For crowd scenes, it is also worth
ensuring that the extras mime under any dialogue, in order to
capture the lines cleanly. This kind of performance issue should be
discussed with the director and 1st assistant director ( _1st AD_ or _first_ )
ahead of the shoot.


**VOICE ACTIVITY A**


As the mixer on a shoot, how might you plan for the following?









A location where background noise will make clean sync impossible.
A scene that is only being covered in a wide shot.
A crowd scene, with actors delivering crucial dialogue in the foreground.


#### **Kit Lists**

Once the recording plans have been made, a kit list is drawn up to
ensure the sound team will have everything they need on set. A
typical inventory of production sound equipment might include the
following:
























boom mics (shotgun, hypercardioid);
boom poles (short, long);
wireless boom transmitters and receivers;
shock mounts, wind protection;
plant/room/atmos mics (cardioid, omnidirectional, stereo);
mic stands, magic arms;
wired lavalier mics;
wireless lavalier mics;
stickies, undercovers, overcovers;
mixer, recorder;
high-quality headphones;
monitoring systems;
camera hop unit;
wireless timecode system;
cables;
batteries;
recording media;
blankets, duvets, carpets.



Boom mics are either supercardioid shotguns or hypercardioid
condensers, both of which have highly directional pick-up patterns.
This gives the best chance for minimizing background noise, but also
leaves little margin for error when lining them up with the actors.


When a mic is properly aligned with the sound source, this is known
as _on-mic_ or _on-axis._ This axis can be visualized as a straight line
drawn down the microphone, extending out beyond it, which should
end up at the actor’s lips when recording dialogue.

Shotguns are more directional than hypercardioid mics, making
them the best choice for exteriors, where noise is usually more of an
issue. Within interior spaces, alongside the direct mic pick-up, actors’
voices also bounce off the walls, ceiling and floor, with reflections
entering the mic at all angles. Due to the _interference tube_, that
increases a shotgun’s directionality, any reflected sound entering offaxis – through the back and sides – can have an unnatural quality,
which is more noticeable with speech. Hypercardioids are a little less
directional, and must be positioned closer to the source, but they do
not suffer from the same issue of off-axis colouration. This means
that for interiors, if you have both options, you must decide whether
to prioritize gaining the most on-mic signal versus background noise
(shotgun), or avoiding unnatural-sounding reflections
(hypercardioid). If you have the option of taking only one type of
boom microphone to a location, then the shotgun is a safer bet and
also more commonly available for hire.


but a more flexible option is to use a _radio boom_ system, which
allows for wireless sending of the boom mic signal to the recorder,
and also for the 1AS to wirelessly monitor the boom signal. This
wireless monitoring line also allows the 1AS to tune into the overall
sound mix – _production mix_ - to check for other issues such as
poorly placed lav mics. On this private production mix radio channel,
the mixer can also communicate instructions to the 1AS, either
during or between shots.

The boom mic is mounted inside a system to absorb any shocks
from handling, and to eliminate wind noise. This usually takes the
form of a _zeppelin_ in which the mic is suspended, with the whole
thing covered by a furry _wind sock_ during exterior scenes. You may
also need to use shock mounts on any planted mics, to avoid
vibrations of footsteps and touches entering the plant mics. Plant
mics can be rigged on stands out of frame, or on _magic arms_ smaller and more versatile rigging devices, which can be clamped
and angled precisely.

A lav mic may be attached to a costume with a clip, but a more
flexible option is to use _stickies_, double-sided adhesive pads that
allow for durable and residue-free fastening. Certain kinds of
costume will lead to major rustling issues, which _undercovers_
attempt to minimize by providing an absorbent layer on top. Lavs are
often hidden under a layer of clothing but, if they are exposed to the
elements, _overcovers_ provide a layer of wind protection.

All of the mics are fed into a _location mixer_ connected to the
recording device. Alternatively, a combined mixer-recorder may be
used – more convenient for a smaller crew but with less mixing
flexibility. Monitoring systems are run from the mix outputs, providing
headphone feeds to anyone who needs a close ear on the dialogue.

The final piece of the technical jigsaw is the ensuring of sync
between sound and image. In its most basic form, this happens via
the _slating_ process, with a _clapperboard,_ or _slate_, producing an
audio-visual ‘clap’ at the start or end of a take. If the budget allows,
wireless timecode systems may also be used to embed an identical


rolling timestamp into both audio and visual footage, for automated
syncing in post. Lastly, a _camera hop_, or _cam hop_, can wirelessly
send a copy of the mix to the camera, if it needs to be embedded
into the footage as a reference.

This is a large amount of kit for a small team to move around on
set. In suitable conditions, they may have access to a _sound cart_,
which houses everything neatly in a portable recording studio of
sorts.

### **PLANS IN ACTION**


Completing these preparations puts the sound team in good shape
for the shoot. However, even with intensive planning, production can
be a gruelling and unpredictable affair, best crewed by resilient
problem solvers – as Mike Tyson said, ‘Everyone has a plan until
they get punched in the face.’ Expect reality to land a few solid blows
and learn to roll with those punches, leaning on your designs, but
being flexible when the ground shifts. This uncertainty makes
preparation all the more important – without a foundation that has
been carefully thought through, things can quickly unravel on set.

When arriving at an interior location, begin by dealing with any
background noise. Heaters, vents, fans, air con, fridges and alarms
are common sources, and, even when agreements have been made
during the recce, it may not be easy to find the person who can
switch these facilities off. If a power generator is being used, discuss
with the lighting crew’s electricians ( _sparks_ ) where this would best be
installed to minimize impact on recording. As lights start to be turned
on, check for buzzing heads or power ballasts – if you can hear any
issues, see if the sparks can do anything about them.

If smoke machines or hazers are being used, make friends with
the sparks operating them, as you will want these to be turned off
during takes. Certain equipment, such as motion-controlled camera
kit ( _moco_ ) and pyrotechnics ( _pyro_ ), may produce noise that cannot
be controlled, and instead must be worked around through mic
placement. All forms of cardioid mic (including hyper and super)


have a _null point_ at the back – the opposite end of their axis – which
picks up significantly less sound. If it is possible without sacrificing
proximity to the actors, angle the boom so it points directly away
from the noise’s origin. This will not eliminate it entirely but is the
best you can do to limit the problem when the noise cannot be
reduced at source.

#### **Setting up the Kit**


Whilst the mixer deals with any initial noise concerns, the rest of the
team can prepare the kit. The recorder should be configured with the
correct settings – a sample rate of 48KHz, and a bit rate of 24 is
usually used. If timecode is being used, the correct frame rate must
also be set. _Phantom power_ is enabled for any channels that need it

- a 48-volt power source usually delivered to a mic via its signal
cable. Boom mics and wired lavs are usually powered in this way,
whilst wireless mics require batteries.

If a continuous wireless timecode link is not being used, an
alternative is to _jam sync_ . This involves connecting the camera and
recorder at the start of the day to align their timecode generators, as
in the synchronized watches of _Mission: Impossible_ . One device is
designated as _master_ - usually the audio recorder – and the other as
_slave_, which echoes the master’s timecode. Once disconnected,
they continue to run on their own internal clocks. There will inevitably
be some drift over the course of a day, so another jam sync usually
happens at lunch, and again at dinner if the shoot will go late.

Radio mics should also be set up, testing for any interference on
frequencies used by the units. Each transmitter-receiver pair has a
limited number to choose from, according to their design and local
laws, and will not work properly if any other devices are set to the
same value. Once configured, you can then find a good moment to
mount the transmitters and lavs on to the actors.

If monitoring feeds are being used, these are distributed to the
director, script supervisor and producers, usually via wireless units
known as _IFB_ s (interruptible foldback). If a radio boom is not being
used, the 1AS may use a personal monitor amp – a _preamp_ with a


headphone monitor and signal pass-through – which boosts the
boom mic signal to a good level for headphones, whilst also sending
an unboosted copy to be recorded. Higher-quality preamps in the
mixer or recorder raise the level of each mic to capture it cleanly.
These need to be pushed harder when recording quieter material, so
it is worth being aware that cheaper ones can create a significant
amount of hiss or have an unnatural sound. Some preamps are
designed to change the tonal quality of their input, in a process
known as colouring or warming, but the ‘transparent’ variety is used
in production sound, for a more faithful result.

#### **Planting Mics**


Mics can be rigged in place using stands, arms or clamps to capture
dialogue where a boom cannot reach. These can be placed behind
larger props or hidden just out of frame, with the exact scenario
influencing the type of mic chosen. To capture a number of actors
surrounding a particular point, or a moving actor who maintains a
distance from a specific spot, an omnidirectional mic can be used,
which captures sound evenly from all directions. Cardioid mics – the
slightly less directional kind that are used in foley work – are more
suited to stationary actors. If an actor is precisely hitting marks, and
keeping a similar head position, perfectly aligned shotguns or
hypercardioids may pick up dialogue best.

There may also be places where wired or wireless lavs can be
hidden around the set – attached to walls, tucked into the pot of a
houseplant, or behind props on a table. These should not be rigged
directly on to a surface or prop, as any physical contact will be
conducted straight through the mic. Instead, make a small loop of
tape for the mic to sit in, which holds it just above the surface.

#### **Wiring Up the Talent**


An actor’s lav should be rigged as close to the mouth as possible –
attached to, or just under, the collar, within hair or a hat, or anywhere
else the costume allows. Whilst they must generally be hidden from


view, they will always sound better mounted on the outside of a
costume. For wide shots and setups with the actor off screen, or with
their back to the camera, they should be remounted if time allows.
Cables are rigged with some slack, so that any tugs – along a wire
running to the mixer, or to the radio transmitter in the actor’s pocket –
will not dislodge the mic.

If you are able to discuss options with the wardrobe department
in advance, you might be able to give a sonic perspective on
planned costume materials. Natural fibres such as cotton are much
less prone to lav noise issues than synthetics such as polyester. You
might also be able to arrange for miniature ‘holsters’ to be sewn into
the costumes to house the lavs. Alternatively, you can use alligator
clips, pins or adhesive stickies to secure them in place. Stickies are
often the simplest and most durable option, attaching to clothing or
skin without leaving a mark, and coming with undercovers for rustle
prevention and overcovers for wind protection. The covers should
also prevent any popping with words containing _plosives_ - blasts of
air that most commonly happen with the pronunciation of ‘p’s.


**VOICE ACTIVITY B**


As head of the on-set sound team, think about how you might handle the following
scenarios:










You arrive on location. What are some of the first things you should check?
You need to plant a mic to record actors standing around a small table at a bar.
What kind of mic would you choose?
Your wireless timecode system is not functioning correctly. What methods can
you fall back on to ensure sync between sound and image?


#### **Liaising with the Director and 1st AD**

A particularly tricky issue for production recording is how to handle
scenes with overlapping speech. These can easily create issues for
the dialogue edit, where any elements that cannot be separated may
cause continuity issues. For shots where an actor’s mouth is not
visible, you could ask the director if the unseen actor can mime their


side of the conversation, or later wild track the exchange without
overlaps.

Other issues for post can arise where directors choose to give
verbal instructions during takes, which commonly happens when
working with child actors or improvised material. Extraneous voices
need to be cut around in post and filled with foley, which comes with
associated time and budget implications. The mixer might make a
polite request for instructions to be kept as brief as possible or
suggest the use of an earpiece where feasible.

Background music and crowds should also be silent on set during
dialogue scenes. Where characters need to move to a rhythm, this
can be cued visually instead, by people or screens in each actor’s
eyeline. Over this artificial hush, the performers will need to project
their voices a little more than feels natural. When a person speaks
more loudly, the voice takes on a different quality, which cannot be
simulated in post by boosting the volume or by applying EQ. If the
performance lacks energy, the result can seem fake when mixed
with the background layer. Any issue in this respect should be
flagged up with the director or 1st AD.

The crowd sounds themselves may then be done as an
additional take, or wild track, without the dialogue. This method can
also be applied to any action that would be painstaking to recreate
from scratch in post, such as the movement in a group dance scene.
These kinds of additional recordings might impact on the schedule,
and so need to be run past the 1st AD in advance, to see if time can
be found.

The 1st AD is also responsible for crew discipline and for
announcing ‘quiet on set’ ahead of takes. If these instructions are not
being followed, the sound team should raise a concern, as any
unnecessary background noise will harm their recordings. The AD
should also be alerted immediately if a shot needs to be retaken for
sound – once the crew has started to move on to the next setup, the
opportunity will have gone.

#### **Unit Rhythms**


Each film crew involved in a shoot is known as a _unit_, and there is
often a _second unit_ in operation, shooting anything that does not
involve the main actors. This might include cutaways, visual effects
elements and sequences involving stunt performers.


Information on the clapperboard allows each shot to be easily identified in post.


For each unit, every shot begins in the same way. After the 1st
AD has asked for quiet, they will announce ‘Roll sound!’, at which the
recordist will begin recording and reply ‘Sound speed’. The AD then
calls ‘Roll camera!’, and the camera operator answers ‘Camera
speed’. Usually the AD will then say ‘Slate!’, and the second camera
assistant ( _2nd_ _AC_ ) will bring the clapperboard into frame with details
of the setup written on it, whilst verbally announcing the scene, shot
