and take numbers: for example, ‘Scene X, Slate Y, Take Z’. When
the AD calls ‘Mark!’, the 2nd AC claps the board. The sound team
must ensure that the clapperboard sound is picked up clearly on at
least one of the mic channels.

An alternative sequence comes if the shot is being _end boarded_ .
Sometimes it is impractical to bring a slate in front of the lens at the
start of a shot – if it is an extreme close-up or similar, for example. In
this case, the AD will call for the slating to happen once the action is
complete and the camera can pull away.


In either case, once the scene is ready to begin, the director or
1st AD will announce ‘Action’. Once it is over, the director will call
‘Cut’, which cues the recordist and camera op to stop rolling. The
director will often wait a while at the end of the action, gaining
additional material of reaction or stillness, before cutting.

#### **Swinging the Boom**


When moving to a new scene, the director will usually begin by
_blocking_ . This is the choreography of how the camera and the actors
move around a space, which the 1AS must follow. Learning precise
control of the boom mic comes with practice, and operators learn to
use their ears, as well as their eyes, to ensure that dialogue is
always on-mic. A distant sound, or lack of crispness, results from offmic recordings and there is no real remedy in post, beyond alts and
ADR.

For each setup, extend the pole to the desired length and get into
position by holding it above your head, with arms pointed directly
upwards. This gives you the most amount of control, whilst limiting
the strain on your muscles. If you can hear handling noise as you
move the pole around, check that the shock mount is properly
suspending the mic, or try wearing cotton gloves and moving more
carefully.

When getting near the action, a novice operator will tend to play it
safe, giving themselves plenty of room, to avoid accidentally dipping
into frame. The confidence to push closer, and get cleaner sound,
comes with experience, and you will have to ruin the odd take to get
there. If you are able to see a video monitor, you can judge the edge
of the frame yourself; if not, you will need the camera department to
help you. Designer and author Jay Rose has this advice: ‘You could
start with the mic high and gradually lower it until someone says “any
lower and you’ll be in the shot”. But that almost always results in too
much safety margin, and more mic distance than is necessary. A foot
of extra distance to protect the frame doesn’t look any different than
an inch or two, but sounds a lot worse. It’s better to start with the mic
in the shot and gradually raise it until it can’t be seen any more.’


For shots involving dialogue, the aim is to get the speech as
cleanly as you can, with as little of everything else – movement,
background – as possible. The most effective way to boom will
depend on the framing, composition and blocking of each setup. For
examples of conventionally covered exchanges between two actors,
_see_ the previous page.


**1 Wide:** the first setup is the master wide. The whole scene is run, but is often only used by
the editor to establish the scene ahead of the dialogue. The boom operator gets as close as
possible, but is not able to pick the sound up crisply. The lavs pick up the sound cleanly, and
could be used if the director particularly likes this initial performance.


**2 Single:** a setup where only one actor is visible. Often the actor has the other lines fed to
them by a crew member, but here the second actor remains in place and the conversation
runs as normal. You attempt to capture everything by re-angling the mic between lines, but
with a main focus on getting clean sound for the actor in shot.


**3 Dirty single:** a medium close-up of the second actor, which also includes the first actor’s
shoulder, out of focus in the foreground, ‘dirtying’ the edge of frame. This time, a stand-in is
used in place of the first actor, giving you just one set of lines to worry about capturing. You
follow the actor’s head movements, ensuring he is always on-axis.


**4 Two-shot:** a two-shot frames both actors in a medium, requiring everything to be cleanly
captured. Learning a script allows you to instinctively reposition the mic between lines – you
align the pole so that rolling it with your fingers swivels the mic between the ideal positions
for each performer. If the actors are too far apart for this technique, a 2nd boom op may be
used.


**5 Close-up:** the next setup is a close-up detail of hands. The editor is given a shot of the
actor that is free from dialogue sync, allowing them to cut away from the main action and
back again, to transition between different takes. If there is no dialogue, you should focus
on the most significant actor movements; in a quiet moment, you might also capture some
useful breath.


**6 Cutaway:** the final shot is another cutaway, this time of the environment. Without any
actors present, the setup may be carried out by a second unit. Where the frame is still, you
aim for a balanced recording of the soundscape, potentially recording in the mid-side
format, which is both mono and stereo compatible.


It is important for the 1AS to know the script, especially for shots
involving multiple actors, as the boom must often be moved very
quickly between lines. The most informative version of the script to
use is the _tramlined_ or _lined script_, which indicates the dialogue and
action to be run for each shot.

#### **Riding Levels**


For the mixer, it is crucial to ensure that all channels are free from
distortion during each take. This issue can happen when the
microphone, preamp, or wireless link is overloaded, or if a digital
recorder is fed a level beyond _digital peak_, leading to a horrendous
type of distortion known as _clipping._ Wireless systems usually have a
_limiter_ feature, which reduces the level of loud sounds to prevent
distortion, although you might hear this as a ‘pumping’ effect as it
sucks down the volume in extreme cases. Recorders and mixers


also offer this option, but you may be able to achieve a more natural
result by manually riding the levels.

Start by setting the preamp volume, or _trim_, so that dialogue
reads around −18db (European standards) or −20db (US and Japan
standards) on the meters for a normal level of speaking within the
current mic setup. If you know that a character will shout midway
through a take, you may need to smoothly lower the level just before,
to give yourself more _headroom_ and avoid distortion. This change
should not happen on active dialogue, as it will be difficult to reverse
in post.

The trim setting affects both the 2-track mix and isos, but there
are also controls that only alter the mix. These can be used to shape
tracks and bring up quieter dialogue, giving a more balanced result
for monitoring and cutting, whilst leaving the isos untouched. This is
the preferred approach for post.

During each take, the sound team must work to block out all
distractions and focus solely on getting the best mix of the available
mics. Documentary mixing brings the added diversion of continually
new material. With a drama, the same lines are repeated over and
again, but with docs each interview moment can bring a new sense
of intrigue. ‘I actually focus on everything but the dialogue,’ sound
recordist Don Nelson ( _Amy_, _The Great Hack_ ) notes. ‘You’re listening
round it, but the whole thing with docs is that they’re usually taking
you in, you’re usually learning something, so that’s even harder to
do.’ It takes discipline and practice to focus on the sound itself,
rather than on the meaning and emotion of the material.

#### **Recording Wild Tracks**


Capturing clean audio away from a noisy set can prove even more
valuable than the sync at times. These recordings not only help to
avoid the need for ADR, but in some cases have saved a film. In _The_
_Dark Knight_, for example, mixer Ed Novick had the foresight to wild
track all of Heath Ledger’s lines, due to the extensive moco and pyro
on set, which rendered the sync unusable. As it would turn out, ADR
was not an option, due to the actor’s untimely passing before the edit


was complete. As the wild tracks (also known as _wild lines_ ) were so
well performed and recorded, they could be seamlessly woven into
the finished film. Crucially, mixer and actor achieved a sufficient
energy level in the tracks, avoiding a common issue with recording
away from the intensity of a camera take. Wild tracking immediately
after a scene, whilst still in the flow, can help to prevent flat reads,
and result in closely matching timings. Actors must also repeat any
movements they might have made as part of the on-screen action,
as this affects vocal delivery. Without this, the wild lines may not
match up to the picture very convincingly.

Tight schedules may mean that requests for wild track time are
dismissed, so it is a good idea to find ways to avoid impact on the
overall schedule. An opportunity may present itself, for example,
when lighting is being adjusted for the next setup, or whilst kit is
being packed up between locations.

If there is no quiet place on a location, one option is to try wild
tracking in a car. This can result in a slightly boxy sound, which is not
ideal for exterior lines, but draping blankets and duvets may help.
Almost anything you can get is better than nothing, and slightly
compromised results can often be made to work in the final mix.

#### **Room Tone and Atmos**


Wild sound effects are another crucial part of the production sound
process, providing a valuable resource to the post team. A _room tone_
(not to be confused with the post-production flavour of room tone,
recorded for its creative possibilities) is a recording of a location, with
the same mic perspective as a particular camera setup, which
captures the ‘dead air’ of the location, known as a space’s _noise_
_floor_ . This helps the dialogue editor fill any gaps that need to be cut
into the sync sound – chopping out verbal directions, crew noise and
the like. Room tones, sometimes known as _buzz tracks_, are normally
recorded at the end of a setup, and require the crew to be quiet for
thirty seconds or so.


Multi-channel recordings of evocative locations can be a great creative resource for post,
rendering environments with much more spatial detail than mono allows.


A more challenging situation arises if the space’s noise floor
includes intermittent sounds, potentially leading to continuity issues
in the dialogue edit. In one film I was designing, a railway line ran
alongside one of the locations, and every so often the clanking of
passing carriages filled the room. As railways were already an
aspect of the story, this could be neatly worked into the creative
design, but there was a risk that, when the scene was assembled,
trains would be heard jumping in and out on the cuts. The remedy
was to ensure enough time was allowed in the schedule to record a
clean train pass from each main setup, which could then be
introduced to lead in or out of a train heard under dialogue.

In the case of that particular film, the issue was deemed
important enough to allow time for it. However, in most situations the
schedule will not accommodate the recording of a full complement of
room tones, and it will be necessary to choose a handful of the more
crucial ones. In a space with a very noticeable noise floor, where
filling of edit holes is likely to be needed, try to imagine which setup


is most likely to be used in the dialogue edit, and capture room tone
for that. Tracks of a consistent noise floor can also be made in post
by looping small gaps where the frame is still within a take or
synthesized by specialist software from a sample of the recording,
but a well-judged track from the set may be particularly appreciated
by the dialogue editor.

Atmos wild tracks, on the other hand, are something a mixer
decides to take if they encounter anything outside of the main shoot
that they feel is worth capturing. This process is similar to
environmental _field recording_, judged within the context of the story.
It might be the dawn chorus of a countryside setting, the striking
bells of a clock tower or waves crashing on to rocks at a seaside
location. Specific elements may also be requested by the sound
designer, if the shoot is happening in a location that is particularly
significant, or hard to reach – in a cave or on an iceberg, in an
unusual industrial setting, and so on. Atmos tracks are normally
recorded in stereo or surround, with a view to capturing a good
sense of space, and most kit lists will include either a designated
stereo mic, or two of the same cardioid or omnidirectional mics, to
form a stereo pair. These background recordings can potentially be
made before the start, or after the end of the shoot day, when there
will be no crew noise to contend with. Action wild tracks may also be
recorded on set to capture specific features of those present, such
as a musical performance or groups of people talking in a particular
language. Key props may also be captured. One example is the
iconic sound of a spoon hypnotically stirring tea in a cup in _Get Out_,
which was in fact an on-set wild track.

### **LOGGING AND BACKING UP**


On-set administration is another important part of the production
sound team’s work. For every take, the mapping of mics to iso
channels, and notes about any problems, or particularly good
moments, are logged in the _sound report_, helping the post team to
navigate the rushes. Mixer Mick Duffield’s report of the shoot of the


_One Last Dance_ script ( _see_ Appendix I) is a good example of what
this document might look like.


A section of the sound report sheet for _One Last Dance_ .


The ‘Ch’ column documents how many channels were recorded
for each take, which corresponds to the number of isos. The ‘WT’
column indicates a wild track, and ‘Notes’ include details of any
falsely started takes, alongside judgements of quality and whether
an end board (‘EB’) was used instead of a standard slate at the start.
The final columns detail the mic on each iso track.

The filenames are auto-generated by the recorder, combining a
user-specified prefix – here an acronym of the production name, and
the shoot day – along with an ascending number. Additional
information can also be added to each take as file _metadata,_ via a
keyboard attached to the recorder.

During breaks in shooting, recorded media should be delivered to
the _digital imaging technician_ ( _DIT_ ), who is responsible for ensuring
that all material is successfully transferred and backed up to multiple
drives. The sound team usually brings enough blank media to
ensure that no formatting is required during the day and can make
their own back-ups off site. The DIT may often begin to sync up the
sound and picture rushes during the shoot day, allowing the post
department to get a head start.


_[OceanofPDF.com](https://oceanofpdf.com/)_


# **8** **VOICE (POST)**
## **_DIALOGUE EDITING AND ADR_**

Dialogue is the tractor the characters use to haul their heavy load.


_(Sarah Kozloff)_

### **DIALOGUE EDITING**


Once the rushes start to come in, the assembly process can begin.
This first pass is usually quite rough, without much attention paid to
the dialogue recordings. In subsequent passes, picture editors
usually start to explore the sound a little more, getting a sense of
which dialogue takes are usable, and cutting in alts to sculpt
performances or solve technical issues.


Hunter, M.Y., _Joy and the Labourer_ (before 1825). W.A. CADBURY COLLECTION


Once everything is assembled, the editor works on the _rough cut_,
initially ordering scenes according to the script, allowing
assessments to be made of how well the story is working as written.
Experiments with removing or re-ordering scenes can then be made,


with reshoots and pick-ups planned if additional material is required.
Once a shape has been found that the editor, director and producer
agree is working, the sequence is called the _first cut_ . The dialogue
editor will often become more involved at this point, attending a
_spotting session_, in which the film is watched through to enable
discussions about which dialogue should be replaced or added, to
resolve technical issues and enhance the storytelling. The picture
editor’s final stage is to produce the _fine cut_, the final picture edit in
which the sequence of frames in the film is finalized. Once this is
ready, the project has reached _picture lock_ .

In some workflows, the sound department may begin their work
alongside the edit, responding to picture changes by adjusting their
timeline to match. Alternatively, the sound post team waits for picture
lock. The advantage of this is that there is less to-ing and fro-ing with
re-timing, but the disadvantage is that it limits the sound designer’s
input during the cut. If the sound team are involved earlier on, they
may be able to help steer the cut in new directions, or provide
elements that help with the fine-tuning of timings towards lock.

In either case, once the sound department receives the editor’s
tracks – via an interchange format such as _OMF_ or its successor
_AAF_ - the first job is to organize them. The edit department may
have provided all recorded channels, but in other workflows, the
editor uses only the 2-track mixes, and the isos need to be synced
up with the timeline, in a procedure known as _conforming_ . If image
and sound were embedded with synced timecodes during the shoot,
it will be possible to auto-sync the isos for each shot. Specialist
software is fed an _EDL_ ( _edit decision list_ ) file, which details the
timecodes of every shot used in the edit. If reliable timecode was
unavailable during the shoot, then the editor must sync the isos
before handing over to the sound department, as this needs to be
done with reference to the clapperboards at the start or end of each
shot.

If the cut continues to change whilst the sound edit is under way,
a similar software process uses ‘before and after’ EDL files to work
out the differences and move the audio clips to match. This
automatic _reconforming_ process does not always work perfectly. For
example, if an editor has used _prelapped_ dialogue to lead into a


scene ahead of the picture, this may be sliced in line with the scene
change instead of coming in early. Some manual work is usually
needed to neaten things up, but the automated part of the process
can save a huge amount of time.

#### **Timeline Layout**


Dialogue edits can be very fiddly affairs, so keeping things organized
is important. There is no single standard for laying out the tracks but
having a system that you can stick to will make it easier to navigate
the session. Your choices will also have a big impact on the
efficiency of the _re-recording mix_ - the process of recording a
balanced mixdown of all the tracks in the sound edit. If someone else
is mixing, check how they like things laid out. They may even ask
you to edit within their mix template project, to speed things up later.


The recorded tracks of a two-shot in which both actors speak might look something like this.
The boom captures each line at a similar level, while each actor’s lav picks up their own
lines cleanly, and the other actor’s lines faintly.


The first layout task is to label your tracks with sensible names,
for example:










‘dx1’, ‘dx2’, ‘dx3’ for the sync sound;
‘pfx1’, ‘pfx2’ for production sound fx (sfx grabbed from the sync,
or wild tracks);
‘adr1’, ‘adr2’ for the post-recorded lines.



You can then start spreading the iso channels across the tracks,
according to your system, whilst deleting any instances of the 2-track
mixes. The recordist usually maintains a consistent channel order, as
indicated by the sound report – boom before radios is the standard –
and you should take care to maintain the same pattern through the
edit.


Splitting boom recordings on to separate tracks for each character is not a good idea.
Continuous dialogue recordings should be kept as continuous edits, otherwise the rerecording mix may take longer, requiring the mixer to copy their processing settings to
multiple parts of the same recording.


The approach of cutting up takes is sometimes used. Ask the re-recording mixer in advance
how they would like the production fx tracks to be handled.


Laying out dialogue tracks to respond to the picture editor cutting between camera set-ups
or scenes.


It is a common instinct for trainee sound editors to split boom
recordings on to separate tracks for each character, but this is not
recommended. The exception to this rule is where special
processing needs to be applied to different parts of the same
recording, for example, in the case of a phone conversation that


involves image cuts between talker and listener. During shots of the
latter, the former’s clean recording will be distorted, or _futzed_, to
emulate the effect of the technology.

Cutting up takes to split the spoken lines on to dialogue tracks,
with the unspoken moments on the production fx tracks, may also
prove to be unwise. However, where foley is being recorded for the
entire film, it may be useful for the dialogue editor to split all wordless
sync sound moments of a reasonable length on to the pfx track. One
such moment could be a sequence of an actor setting a suitcase
down, opening it and examining its contents. If this action has also
been recorded by the foley team, it is useful for the re-recording
mixer to be able to choose easily between the sync and the foley.
Having the sync split on to the pfx tracks allows for this option. It is
usually best to ask the re-recording mixer in advance about their
preference across the film.

The only cuts that you would definitely want to make in this
example would be to mute the sections of the lavs where the actor is
not talking – avoiding clothing rustle and the off-mic sound of the
other actor’s lines – and to remove and fill any other unwanted
sounds. Splitting of pfx should be considered within the context of
the film.

Moving on within the example scene, the image has now cut to
the single. At this point, the associated sync should be moved on to
a new set of tracks. This is because the two-shot and the single are
likely to sound different, requiring distinct processing settings in the
mix. Laying them out on different tracks makes this easier.

For the shot that follows the single, you can then return to tracks
dial 1–dial 3, then back to 4–6 and so on.

#### **Alts**


If you encounter performance or recording issues with a line of
dialogue, you can start exploring the alts. When searching through
takes, the recordist’s sound report will come in handy, making it
easier to navigate the rushes and ensure you have located any wild
tracks. Listen to everything that might match the line you are looking


to replace, getting a full sense of what is available and choosing a
few good options if possible. Once you have made your selections,
you will need to re-time them, in order to mirror the actor’s lips. This
can be done using a combination of cutting and moving parts of the
line, or using subtle _time stretching_ - 20 per cent either way is a
good rule of thumb for how much you can stretch or squash before
digital artifice starts to become noticeable.


This alternating system of audio and blank spaces is known as checkerboarding.


The alt that works best for performance and visual match should
be chosen in consultation with the director. Anything that changes a
performance will be a sensitive issue for the director and picture
editor, who have both put a lot of thought into shaping the characters
during the cut and will probably have made their own substitutions.
The sound designer needs to find out what has happened in the
decision-making process prior to their involvement, whilst fully
embracing the creative possibilities of their own storytelling skills.
The transformative potential of sculpting character and plot through
alts and additions can be the most rewarding part of the process; as
dialogue editor Adele Fletcher ( _Control_, _Mary Queen of Scots_ ) puts
it, ‘I love working with actors and directors as much as sound. To me,
communicating the story is as important as delivering the sound
track.’


One particularly ingenious use of alts lies at the heart of the
mind-bending plot twist in _The Conversation_, an early collaboration
between editor-designer Walter Murch and director Francis Ford
Coppola. At the beginning of the film, surveillance expert Harry Caul
records an exchange of dialogue between a couple and comes to
realize that he has become part of a wider story, perhaps even a
conspirator. The recorded conversation includes the line, ‘He’d _kill_ us
if he had the chance’, leading Harry to worry for the safety of the
couple. He is later a witness to events that prove that the couple are
in fact the culprits. Within a montage of memories, the original
conversation is replayed, but this time the line runs, ‘He’d kill _us_ if he
had the chance.’ The change in emphasis alters the meaning, and
therefore leads to a different interpretation of who is the guilty party
in the equation, shifting the perspective of the audience in sync with
that of the protagonist.

#### **Working with Isos**


Once everything is in place and synced up, you can start digging into
the detail of the different tracks. Every mic should be listened to, as
you consider which to use, and why. If multiple tracks are to be used
together – lav to bring a sense of intimacy, with some boom to bring
a sense of space – you will need to ensure they are correctly
aligned. As boom and lav are at different physical distances from the
actor, sound enters each mic at a slightly different time. This results
in the _comb-filter effect_ or _phasing,_ where certain frequencies build
up, or cancel each other out, causing an unnatural sound. Whilst it is
possible to resolve this manually by moving one of the clips by tiny
amounts, it is far more accurate and efficient to resolve any phase
issues using an auto-alignment plugin within your editing software.

Once you have corrected any phase issues, experiment with the
relative level of each mic. This will be refined, or rethought, during
the mix stage, but getting a good blend during the edit will help you
make a judgement about how well your choices are working. In most
software, you can change levels either using _track automation_ or _clip_
_gain_ ; you should be consistent about which you use. Check with the


re-recording mixer about their preference. Any track automation will
be connected to their mixing desk controls, and they often prefer to
start with flat faders and perform level changes from scratch
themselves. Clip gain instead works on one audio region at a time,
providing a quick way to pull a line up or down, or draw in a volume
curve to even out a dynamic delivery, without having an impact on
the mixer’s controls.

Other forms of processing, such as EQ, noise reduction and
reverb, are usually left until the mix, but you may choose to run some
temporary effects if it helps you to cut. If you are also mixing the film
yourself, you can take any approach you like. However, it is usually
wise to avoid too much mix work early on, as the director is likely to
want to chop and change things until the sound edit is complete, and
your detailed mixes may end up on the cutting room floor.

#### **Smoothing Joins**


The next stage of the dialogue edit involves the removal of any
unwanted sounds as you work towards a seamless edit of the
disparate dialogue sources. If you need to cut out part of a recording

- crew noise, directions or any other extraneous sound – this will
leave a gap to be filled. Ideally, a room tone will have been captured
for the setup, otherwise you will need to make something equivalent.
You can try using a digital tool to extract the noise floor of a
recording, although this only works for consistent backgrounds, and
it can easily sound synthetic. Alternatively, creating _fill_ manually may
be the best way to go, using the following method:



1.


2.


3.



After cutting out the offending section, use the logs and clip name
to identify which camera setup the recording is from.
Locate all of the takes from this setup, which should all share a
similar noise floor, and offer the closest match for fill.
Any small movement sounds, breaths or background variations
can make the loop very noticeable, so hunt for a moment of
genuine stillness. A very short clean moment will make a better
loop than a longer section with recognizable detail.


4. If the background contains wall-to-wall detail, such as voices in a

bar, a good loop may not be possible. Instead, try joining together
different snippets of noise to make the fill.


Sections of dialogue tracks often need to be removed due to crew noise and other
undesirable background sounds.


Look for a moment of clean background within these takes, cut it out, and loop it to the
desired length.


If you hear a click on each loop, you can try putting in a tiny crossfade between the joins. If
you then hear a volume change during the fade, try making the crossfade smaller, or
change the shape – open the fade editor, and try toggling between ‘equal power’ and ‘equal
gain’ to fix this ‘pumping’ effect.


An alternative looping method is to make a forward-reverse loop – reversing every other
instance of the loop. This eliminates the need for crossfades and can feel less ‘loopy’,
although any details present may sound obviously reversed.


In addition to removing and filling any unwanted elements, you also
need to look at the joins between different sound takes and sources
as you work your way through the timeline. Giving the edit a good
sense of flow is an iterative process. It involves making small
changes on each pass to improve things, until the splices become
unnoticeable. This ability to create a seamless edit from thousands


of tiny decisions is what gives dialogue editing its status as a ‘dark
art’. There are no magic bullets to make things flow, but there are a
few commonly used techniques that will serve you well.

#### **Temporal Masking**


Due to the way the brain works, if two simultaneous changes happen
in what we hear, we tend to notice only the larger change. This effect
is known as _temporal masking_ . This means that if the dialogue editor
needs to hide any joins, or cover up any other momentary issues,
they can try to distract the listener, just as a magician uses
misdirection to hide their trickery.

This technique can help deal with changes in background noise
between setups. If the picture cuts from one actor to another during
a break in dialogue, this can cause a noticeable jump in background
noise as the angles change. If instead the sound is cut at the precise
moment when the second actor begins speaking, the background
join can be hidden underneath the start of the dialogue. The
audience member’s brain will latch on to this instead of the noise
change.

If the issue is not solved through temporal masking, there are
other ways to smooth over any lack of continuity in background
noise. If the noise difference is quite subtle, it can work to simply
_crossfade_ between the two clips over a couple of seconds or more,
experimenting to find the smoothest fade start, length and shape.

For noise that changes level through the day – a growing crowd,
crickets beginning to chirp and so on – add some background from
the noisier take under the quieter one. Experiment with how much to
blend in, aiming to match the two takes closely enough to hide the
join.

Planes flying overhead or trains passing by are another common
issue. Although they are not necessarily too objectionable in
themselves, they often wreak havoc with sound continuity. If you
encounter a background sound that suddenly cuts in or out, see if
you can roll the take earlier or later and use a fade to ease the
transition. If you run into other dialogue when you try extending the


clip, you may need to find an alt, or use a matching piece of noise
from elsewhere to smooth the join.

Whilst these techniques are good guides for what to try first,
there are no hard and fast rules about what will work. It will usually
be necessary to experiment to find the best solution in each case.

### **ANALYSING VOCAL PERFORMANCE**


Another skill a dialogue editor must develop is the dissection of vocal
delivery. This is partly about sculpting emotion and characterization,
in close collaboration with the director, but there are also more
technical elements to master. The ability to dissect vocal delivery will
allow an editor to quickly locate good edit points between takes, help
actors match previous deliveries in ADR, and communicate with
directors using common performance lingo.


Each phoneme in language corresponds to a particular mouth shape; syllables are formed
by transitioning between these.


To begin the analysis, spoken language can be broken down into
a hierarchy of four elements:


sentence;
word;
syllable;
phoneme.



The difference between a sentence and a word is obvious, but
syllable and phoneme are slightly more difficult to distinguish.
Syllables are uninterrupted sounds made up of a vowel, often with
one or more consonants added. Phonemes are even smaller units –
the set of distinct sounds that make up a language. They can be
considered as the atoms of speech.

For example, the word _together_ can split into syllables ( **to | geth |**
**er** ) or into phonemes ( **t | o | g | e | th | er** ).

#### **Phonemes**


Being able to pick out the different phonemes in an audio clip makes
it much easier to cut. There is one particular sub-category that is
especially useful for edits: the _plosives_ . In English, these are p, b, t,
d, k, g. The reason they are helpful is because they involve a brief
stopping of the previous phoneme before they sound. This provides
a tiny gap, which often makes an ideal edit point. If a line you are
looking to splice contains any plosives, this should be the first place
to try as your moment of transition.

Another set of phonemes that deserve special attention is the
_sibilants_ : s, z, sh, and zh. These are often picked up excessively by
mics. This can usually be resolved in the mix, but sometimes a
particularly problematic sibilant is worth dealing with through recutting. It is usually easy to find another instance from the same
performer elsewhere in the rushes, to replace a particularly harsh or
whistly instance.

National and regional accents also vary the way that phonemes
sound, with the most obvious differences being found in the vowels.
When an actor has put on a particular accent, the dialogue editor will
need to work to ensure that the pronunciation remains consistent
across the film.


#### **Paralanguage**

Strings of phonemes allow the listener to understand what is being
said, but just as important is the way in which a performer uses
expression in their delivery, evocatively summed up by David Lynch:
‘Voice is like music. Everything, every word, has got to be said in a
certain way. It has to do with the timing of a line and how the line
strikes you.’ These non-verbal elements of speech are known as
_paralanguage_ or _vocalics_ .

Paralanguage can be divided into three areas:



1.
2.
3.



Volume: energy, emphasis.
Pitch: register, intonation.
Time: tempo, rhythm, pause.



_Energy_ refers to the amount of force a person uses when speaking.
A higher energy produces a higher volume, but also tends to
increase pitch and change the tonal quality of speech, whereas a
whisper can remove the pitched element of speech completely.
Whilst performers may vary their energy within a line for dramatic
effect, a mismatch when we cut between takes can be quite jarring.

_Emphasis_ is the way in which stress is placed on different
syllables in a sentence. It can change the meaning of the sentence,
as in the example from _The Conversation_ . If an actor is varying the
emphasis between takes, it is vital to consider which delivery works
best for the story. You also need to take care not to splice takes in a
way that removes meaningful stress, or accidentally doubles it up.

The _register_ of a line is the overall pitch – where it sits in an
actor’s vocal range. If this does not match when the editor cuts
between lines, then the edit does not usually work. If there are no
better places, or alts, to splice, you can try to remedy a slight
mismatch with _pitch shifting_ . Most algorithms offer a _formant-_
_preserving_ mode. This maintains the frequency of consonants whilst
changing the pitch of vowels, avoiding the ‘chipmunk effect’ when
pitching up, or the ‘monster effect’ when going the other way. Even
with this setting, pitch shifting will still sound artificial when pushed
too far – one or two semitones is usually the natural limit.


The degrees of intonation across an ‘a’ phoneme. Rising intonation in English usually
indicates a question, whereas a falling cadence can add force, or indicate negative emotion.


_Intonation_, or _cadence_, refers to the rise and fall of a voice during
speech. This has a big impact on the emotion transferred to the
listener and can change the meaning by indicating a question or
statement. Some performances have a wide intonation range, whilst
others will be flatter. The editor needs to be mindful of cadence when
cutting. A new cadence might purposely be created from two
different takes for emotional effect, but the adjoining phonemes at
the edit point must be compatible in pitch. In tonal languages, such
as Thai and Vietnamese, intonation plays a more radical role,
fundamentally modifying meaning; for example, in Vietnam, ‘pho’
pronounced with a falling pitch is a soup, whereas with a rising
intonation (‘phó’), it means deputy.

_Tempo_ is the speed of delivery. If this varies between the takes
that you are trying to splice, subtle time stretching can give a better
match. _Rhythm_ refers to the pattern in time that the sequence of
syllables forms, which may be regular or irregular. William Shatner’s
delivery as Captain Kirk is a famous example of the latter, and also
well known for using _pause_ in an unusual way. More conventional
performances tend to reserve it for hesitation, or a dramatic _beat,_
which is a slight gap to lend gravitas or help the sense of flow.

### **POST-RECORDING**


When they are usable, the materials from the shoot are generally
preferred, but it is often necessary to replace or add dialogue in post.


Some directors intensely dislike this idea, due to the potential for
inauthenticity, but others choose to embrace it fully, _post-syncing_
entire films for creative effect. Most projects fall somewhere in
between.

#### **Shooting ADR**


Another name for automated dialogue replacement is _looping_ . Each
line to be replaced is often listened to on a loop, as the actor learns
its rhythm and cadence, before repeating it back. This is a
challenging operation, both technically and in terms of performance,
with many factors needing to hit the mark if a convincing result is to
be achieved.


Example ADR script.


In preparation for the recording, the dialogue editor, or _ADR_
_supervisor_ on bigger projects, first creates an ADR list. This details
all the _cues_, or _loops_, to be captured, where they fall in the film, and
the reason for re-recording. Some dialogue may need to be replaced
due to noise, clarity or performance issues, some might be newly
written off-screen lines, whilst other cues may be added for


continuity. For example, if a character delivers a series of lines in
quick succession, and one needs to be replaced, it may be better to
replace its near neighbours too, to avoid a noticeable jump from ADR
to sync sound.

Once the list has been finalized, a schedule can be drawn up,
usually allowing for ten to twelve loops per hour. The actor cueing
system must also be configured, often using specialist software that
displays each line, along with visual streamers and beeps, also
known as _pips_, to prompt the actors. Each beep is a 1kHz tone,
lasting for 1 frame, and they are ordered as follows:


**beep** | _gap_ | **beep** | _gap_ | **beep** | _gap_ | **line**


This allows the actor to feel when the line should begin – in the spot
where a fourth beep would fall. If cueing software is not available,
then the beeps can simply be placed on an audio track in the correct
place for each line, with the actor reading the lines from a script. The
length of each gap is usually 19 frames, but if a faster read is
required for a line, you might use a spacing of 15 frames to inject a
little pace, or 23 frames for a slower delivery.

A session file is also prepped with the required materials:









Video reference of the performance.
Audio reference of the original line.
Atmos bed to play under the ADR.



In the absence of cueing software, the session must also contain:








Beeps.
Loop markers for each cue.



When setting up loop markers, ensure the in-point is a second or so
before the first beep, and that the out-point is a couple of seconds
after the end of the line. If a longer line contains a gap, it may be
worth splitting it into two markers, to make it easier for the actor,
although sometimes this can disrupt the sense of delivery flow when
cut together. For each loop, the actor will listen round a number of
times, until they feel comfortable with the rhythm. They then begin


performing the line, often doing multiple takes in a row as the loop
goes around. Some actors prefer to keep the original dialogue in
their headphones, whilst others will ask for it to be muted once they
have the timing in their head.

An alternative strategy is _listen and repeat._ In this case, the actor
listens to the guide once, and then repeats it back three or so times
in a row. The best performance is then checked against the picture,
to make an assessment of sync. Many actors prefer this method, as
it can feel less mechanical than looping.

If there are any off-screen lines in the script, there is no
requirement to match specific timings, although often the speed of
the line must be controlled to fit neatly between a gap, which can be
checked after a set of takes.

The _ADR mixer_, also sometimes known as the _ADR recordist_,
runs the session, alongside the director and dialogue editor, who
work together to ensure that each newly recorded performance will
work for the film. The mixer ensures the actors are comfortable, and
that they are ‘on side’ for the process; this is especially important, as
it can be an emotionally draining experience, with the actor needing
to match their original timings and get back into the right headspace
in the absence of the rest of the cast, costumes or locations.
Because of these challenges, an actor should not be expected to do
more than a half-day of ADR at a time.

One related problem is that the lower energy of the studio
environment can easily lead to a lower energy performance. This
can affect both pitch and level, leading to an unconvincing effect
when the new recording is cut into the film. Actors can be directed to
give more energy, but the recordist can also help by turning down
the live sound in the actor’s headphones and increasing the level of
the scene’s background track. This often results in the actor
increasing their energy to project over the background. This is
particularly important for exterior scenes. Sometimes, it may also
help to have the actor be physically active before a take, perhaps
running around the studio, or on the spot, to liven themselves up. As
with wild tracks, performers must also match their on-screen


movements during delivery, as this will affect the sound of their
voice.

The mics for the session should be selected by the mixer, with a
view to matching the way in which the production was originally
recorded. This usually involves a supercardioid mic on an overhead
stand, above the actor’s head but pointed at their lips, and a lav mic
worn by the actor. One final aspect of the mixer’s job is to operate
the studio equipment – cueing and recording, maintaining tracks of
director selects, editing takes to combine different parts, adjusting
timings to check sync, and running temporary processing to test how
well the ADR will match the sound of the scene. ADR mixer Nick
Baldock ( _Black Mirror_, _Sex & Drugs & Rock & Roll_ ) likes this on-thefly temp mixing, ‘if not for the benefit of the clients in the room, then
to satisfy myself that ADR will work within a scene and that I’m
effectively not signing off on something that will have to be dropped

[in later stages of the process]’.

After the session, the dialogue editor, ADR supervisor, or
sometimes a specialist ADR editor, will be responsible for _ADR_
_fitting_ . This involves cutting the replaced lines into tight sync with the
picture. During the recording, they will watch carefully for anything
that will make this tricky – common issues are actors reading at the
wrong speed or rhythm, joining up words in ADR that had a small
pause in the original, or vice versa. They also keep a careful ear on
how well the pitch, pace, rhythm and emphasis match the original
performance, noting down the workable takes and the director’s
preferred performances, to help with the ADR edit.

Another technical consideration stems from the fact that only the
problematic lines are re-recorded, rather than ADRing entire scenes.
This means that care must be taken during the mix to match the
original sound as closely as possible. This can be technically very
challenging, so, if lines are delivered very close together, it may be
worth re-doing the lines on either side of the unusable one too,
moving the sync–ADR transition to somewhere less noticeable.

To help with the matching of ADR to production sound, a similar
boom mic and lav are used, ideally with a similar placement and
distance, although this requires a room with a very dead acoustic, to


avoid unwanted reverb. As with foley, if you are in a non-ideal room,
you may need to go in much closer with the mic and compensate in
the mix. Acoustic issues with the studio are particularly problematic
for exterior ADR, where interior reverb on the recordings may render
it unconvincing. If you are unable to access a good room, you might
try recording in a quiet exterior location instead, where the noise
floor would be less of an issue than it would be for foley, as voice
provides a much higher level of signal.

If any actors are working far away from the location of the ADR
studio, a local facility may be booked, with the director and dialogue
editor joining the session remotely. It has even been known on major
films for a few lines to be recorded on a smartphone, held away from
the actor’s mouth at a specific distance, in a room with a reasonably
dry acoustic. Sometimes, due to availability issues or for creative
reasons, and where contracts allow, characters may be entirely revoiced by different actors.

#### **Cutting ADR**


During the ADR sessions, the recordist will usually create _selects_
tracks, to house takes that the director has indicated as being
preferable. This gives a good starting point for editing the best
performances, although directors will often want to dive back into
other takes, to explore other delivery options. Lines will often be
assembled from different takes – the start of one, the end of another

- in a composite approach known as _comping_ .

Once the selects are finalized, they need to be tightly fitted to the
image. For each line, it is a good idea to start by comparing the
waveforms of the original take and ADR visually, to see where they
go out of sync. Listening to the two together can also help to reveal
any places where they are misaligned. However, attempting to fix
sync by comparing the ADR to the actor’s mouth on screen is not a
great method to use whilst cutting. Once you start consciously
comparing audio and video for sync, _everything_ can start to feel out.
This issue, sometimes called _syncitis_, can also rear its head if you
encounter sync problems with the main dialogue recordings during


the cut – once a seed of doubt about sync has been sown, you can
easily become convinced that all of the material is misaligned. The
best remedy is to locate a version of the edit that is known to be in
sync, and cross-reference directly with that. Automated alignment
tools are available to aid with this process, although they tend to
yield mixed results.

Sometimes, ADR and sync can be made to work together to
solve technical issues, whilst preserving most of the original
performance. For _The King’s Speech_, which involved a large amount
of exterior scenes, the sync sound was well recorded and mixed, but
the background noise could still be heard noticeably coming in with
each line. To solve the problem, the beginnings and ends of every
line were re-recorded, to enable more controlled use of temporal
masking, hiding the joins under the loudest phonemes near the line’s
top and tail.

Once a line has been replaced with ADR, this leaves a hole in the
background noise, whilst also removing any sounds of movement
that were present in the original sound. These gaps need to be
closed with fill, using a method similar to that described earlier in this
chapter. In these cases, however, some actor movement detail, also
known as _shash_, is desirable in the fill to help bed the ADR into the
mix. Foley must also recreate any missing action sounds, requiring
more time to record and edit, due to the added exposure where no
sync sound is present.

#### **Background Voices**


For scenes involving larger groups of people, a crowd session, or
_loop group_, may be run. This involves a collection of performers
recording in the same space, providing context-specific texture, and
ambience, known as _walla_ . They will also pick out on-screen
reactions from extras and improvise lines to add energy to a scene.

ADR supervisor Juno J. Ellis ( _Lethal Weapon_, _Million Dollar_
_Baby_ ) describes the process thus: ‘A couple of the actors from the
group watch it two or three times, but the whole group is in with you,
so you might have fifteen pairs of eyes trying to figure out dialogue


that might fit. We lip-read or just find something that might fit a
gesture. The group ADR sets a mood and a tone, but you don’t really
want to be aware of it. It has to really be subliminal. You’re aware
that you’re in a restaurant and there are fifty other people yakking in
the back, but you really only want to be hearing what your main
actors are saying.’


Loop groups can add richness and texture to crowd scenes, through improvised reactions
and general background.

#### **Breath Tracks and Vocalizations**


During ADR sessions, it is also common to record word-less
elements, particularly for the main character. These might be gasps
of surprise, and grunts of physical effort at particular moments, or the
sounds of breath to cover more intimate scenes. This kind of
vocalization is a critical element of sound design. As well as giving
the audience a sense of leaning into the characters, it can also help
communicate their inner world through the nuances of performance.


The sound of breathing can also imply an unseen voyeur behind the frame, a common
feature of horror films.


For example, in _The Elephant Man_, the protagonist’s face
remains unseen for large parts of the film, with sackcloth shrouding
his unusual features. Although his eyes cannot be seen, the sounds
of his laboured breath bring a sense of intimacy and empathy, both
humanizing the ‘monster’ and communicating the physical pain of his
experience.

Breath tracks can be difficult to record, due to their low level
compared with any background noise, so a close mic is usually
required. A _pop filter_ or wind protection should also be used, to avoid
a ‘blowing into the mic’ effect. Sometimes, it helps to position the mic
so that the actor is exhaling across the mic, rather than directly into
it, to further reduce the problem.

#### **Voiceover**


A character’s internal monologue, and other forms of narration, are
another way to communicate a character’s internal world. This can
manifest as a rather uncinematic approach – telling instead of
showing – but, when used with sensitivity ( _Apocalypse Now_ ),
coupled with a hint of irony ( _Barry Lyndon_, _Badlands_ ), or in allusion
to film noir ( _Taxi Driver_ ), it can be a very effective technique.

Unlike ADR, when capturing _VO_ the aim is not to match the
aesthetic of the production sound, but instead to create a separate
space for it to live within the film. Large diaphragm condenser
microphones are often used for a full, rich sound, with very close
micing to enhance the lower end through the proximity effect. A pop
filter is also used, to avoid any issues with plosive phonemes.

Actors do not usually need an audio reference in VO sessions,
but there may be a creative advantage in having them respond to
their on-screen performance. Takes are usually run using longer
sections of the script, which gives a good sense of flow, but
sometimes things are taken line by line, which allows for more
surgical direction. The completed recordings are then edited from the
best takes, whilst the relative timings of word and image are adjusted
to achieve the most effective telling of the story in each moment.


As the most immediate carrier of story, dialogue remains the primary
narrative device in modern filmmaking. This fact, alongside the
technical challenges of voice work, means that the sound team must
use every trick at their disposal, and a lion’s share of their budget, to
deliver a pristine result. On the more creative side of things, voice
also offers a wealth of possibilities to reimagine a story during post,
adding information, reshaping performance, and opening up internal
worlds for the audience.


_[OceanofPDF.com](https://oceanofpdf.com/)_


# **9** **IMAGE**
## **_SPOTTING AND SOUND–IMAGE_** **_RELATIONSHIPS_**

The better the sound, the better the image.


_(Walter Murch)_


As a sound designer, your imagination may be most engaged when
reading the script, but, once the sound edit is under way, you will
need to enter into a deep relationship with the image. In some
moments, your work supports the picture, lending a sense of weight
and solidity, but in others you play against the visual, creating
contrast for dramatic effect. In the end result, the sounds are in many
ways absorbed by the image – for _viewers_ who _watch_ a film, the two
are seamlessly entwined, and experienced together as a cohesive
whole.


Lequeu, J.J., _Portrait of a Girl and Her Dog_ (ca. 1796–7). THE METROPOLITAN MUSEUM OF ART

### **FRAME-BY-FRAME**


The basic unit of moving image is the _frame_ . Sequences of frames
played back at sufficient speed give the illusion of unbroken
movement, especially when accompanied by continuous audio. The
standard cinema format is 24 frames per second ( _FPS_ ). There has
been a growing trend for films made to be projected at higher frame
rates, but audiences have given these a mixed reception, with some
finding an overly clinical effect to 48 FPS and beyond, compared to
the ‘dreamier’ feel of 24.


In both analogue and digital film, audio is stored as a separate stream, seen here running
above the 35mm frames, within the blue sound track strip.


Each frame in a film is given a timecode, using the format
**HH:MM:SS:FF** (hours:minutes:seconds:frames). The first frame of
an edited film is usually denoted as 01:00:00:00. This is to allow a
_leader_, or _count-in clock_, to appear beforehand, so, for example, a
ten second count-in would begin at 00:59:50:00.

Digital sound is not made up of frames, but of _samples_, with each
sample being a document of a sound wave’s state at a particular
moment. The standard rate for film sound is 48,000 samples per
second, otherwise known as a sample rate of 48KHz.

When timecode is stored in an audio recording, it is captured as a
separate channel, which has no effect on the sound itself but can
later be used to determine the correct sync point with the image.
Once image and sound are correctly aligned, they should remain in
sync, so long as both are played back at their native rates.


Likewise, when creating a new project for sound editing, you
should ensure that your session frame rate matches the FPS value
used by the picture editor. This will not only avoid the potential issue
of playing material back at the wrong speed, but also give you a
correctly aligned display grid to help you pick out the precise
moments where one shot changes to another. This is particularly
important for scene changes – if the moment calls for a hard
transition, you would usually ensure that the sounds cut at exactly
the same time as the image. By setting the software’s grid to
timecode mode, it is possible to nudge the cursor one frame at a
time, to find the exact place where one scene becomes another.

### **SPOTTING**


The copy of the cut that the sound designer receives from the editor
will usually include _burnt-in timecode_ (or _BITC_, pronounced ‘bit-sea’).
This visually stamps every frame in the film with its timecode, to
allow for easy identification of frames throughout the post process.
This video file will often be at a lower resolution than the finished
film, rendered using a _codec_ (encoding/decoding format) that does
not require too much processing power to play back. Both of these
factors will improve video performance within the sound-editing
software, allowing more computational resources to be used for
audio processing. A feature film may also be split into multiple _reels_
of twenty minutes or less, which can be easier to work with, and offer
the advantage of isolating future edit changes; if a scene is re-cut,
this will have an impact only on a single reel, leaving the others
untouched.

During the sound editorial phase, it is the job of the _sound effects_
_editor_ to source all of the _sfx_ required to bring the image to life and
best tell the story. As with the dialogue edit, this process usually
begins with a _spotting session_, which is a meeting with the director in
which each scene is broken down in detail. The sfx team notes down
every _spot effect_ - the sonic equivalent of spotlights, picking out a
visual element on screen – alongside thoughts on ambience and any


creative notes to be passed to the foley team. The line between spot
effects and foley can be blurry at times, but in general foley only
handles character movement and interactions, with spots covering
everything else. Sometimes dialogue, sfx and music may all be
spotted during the same session, to allow greater interaction
between the disciplines.

The sfx editor documents all the required sound effects in a table,
also known as a _cue sheet_ .


_**One Last Dance**_ **Cue Sheet (Excerpt)**


During the spotting session, the sfx editor should note down any
sounds that the director requests, as well as putting forward their
own perspective. These notes can then be used to complete the cue
sheet at a later date, adding in as much detail as desired. The
spotting sessions may also be documented as sound recordings as
a good reference for later. During the session, spot placement
information and other details may also be added within the soundediting software as _markers_ .

Each sfx cue must then be sourced, either from sound libraries,
or ideally through tailor-made recordings. Whilst capturing your own
sounds requires more time, it gives you a much greater degree of
creative control and ensures that your work will sound different from
everyone else’s.

Another issue with library sounds is that the more unusual
moments are often cut out in favour of the most recognizable and
generic ones. The eccentricities thrown up during real-world


recordings are often what brings character to a scene. When it is
impractical to record every sound yourself, there are many good
commercial libraries available, as well as free online resources.
Careful layering of multiple library sounds can also create novel
combinations that will be perceived by the audience as single
elements, with their constituent parts rendered unrecognizable.

Maintaining these sonic catalogues, alongside your own sets of
recordings, requires good organization. Sound files tend to be given
long, descriptive names, and tagged with more detailed information
as _metadata_ . This allows for quick searching and efficient testing of
multiple options against the image.


Keep your ears open for interesting real-world sounds to record. Building your own library
will give your designs a distinctive flair. Carry a portable recorder around with you or whip
out the mic on your smartphone if you hear something that intrigues you. If you own a set,
keep some wired lavs in your recording kit. They can easily be suspended or placed in
nooks to quickly capture noises you encounter.


Spot effects are usually recorded in mono, which allows them to
be aligned with the relevant part of the frame through speaker
panning. Library sounds often come in stereo as a standard and may
need to be converted in order to sit properly with the image. You can
either split stereo files into two mono channels and delete one of
them, or merge the two together into a mono signal. While spot
effects are usually cut in exact sync with the image, at times you
may need to diverge by a frame or two, for practical reasons, such


as getting out of the way of dialogue. In these cases, effects editor
Marvin Kerner ( _Ben Hur_, _Dr. Zhivago_ ) offers this advice: ‘If you must
make a choice, always cut an effect early rather than late. An effect
out of sync early is less obvious than one out of sync late.’

The decisions around which elements to spot will have a big
impact on how the audience views the image. Accenting the
appearance of a visual detail can draw attention to it, focusing the
eye on a particular part of the frame. Alternatively, purposely leaving
an element unheard can help to channel it more subconsciously into
the audience’s experience.


**IMAGE ACTIVITY A**


Find a film scene to watch online, pick a thirty-second section and note down all the
sound effects that you can hear within each cue sheet category. Review it multiple
times to see how many layers reveal themselves. It may be easiest to do this on
headphones.


**IMAGE ACTIVITY B**


Using your smartphone, try recording a short video clip of an image that contains
some movement within the frame. Once you’ve found something you like, experiment
with capturing a few different alternatives for sounds that could sync with the
movement.

Bring them together within your editing software and play with different
combinations, exploring how sound and image can affect each other.

### **SOUND–IMAGE RELATIONSHIP**


In Philip K. Dick’s novel _VALIS_, a group of friends watch a
mysterious sci-fi film that contains a dense array of subliminal
messages. Images of a satellite are glimpsed momentarily in the
movie as flash frames, or in one case as a small metal object that
bounces off a speeding taxi. The impact sound heard by the
audience in this moment is that of an empty beer can, tricking them
into seeing only a discarded piece of trash in place of the satellite: ‘It


_sounded_ like a beer can; that’s what fooled me...Hear it, so you see
it.’ This fictional example deftly illustrates the power of the ear to
influence the eye.

#### **Asynchrony**


Any divergence between what we see and what we hear is called
_asynchrony_ . This could be a movement away from a realistic
association of sound and image, for example, we might see and hear
an action coinciding in time, but the two components may not seem
to emerge from the same natural cause. _VALIS_ uses this device to
encode a hidden message, but often the effect is used more
explicitly. In the opening of _Apocalypse Now_, images of jungle
warfare are superimposed over shots of a man in a hotel room, with
helicopter blades cutting through the doom-laden sounds of 1960s
band The Doors. The music and visual overlays fade away, leaving
the chopper to dominate, as the man opens his eyes and the scene
cuts to a ceiling fan. This image lines up with the helicopter sound in
an elegant rhyme, but we cannot interpret the connection literally. It
underlines what the entire sequence has been telling us: this is a
character who may be in an everyday place physically, but
psychologically remains locked in his memories of war.


A slightly fanciful chart showing the increasing effects of asynchrony. In reality, the effects
are too complex to boil down into a graph, but the illustration may help you find a way into
thinking about the area.


‘We experience moments of asynchronous sound throughout all
types of filmmaking and accept it without question,’ observes
designer Larry Sider ( _Street of Crocodiles_, _The Piano Tuner of_
_Earthquakes_ ). ‘For example, when a reaction shot is cut into a
dialogue sequence, we sense something different is happening
because we must look and listen to two separate strands of
information – the voice of the person talking and the expression of
the person listening – rather than taking the sound and image of the
speaker together as a single, realistic event. We pay attention in a
different way. If we want to deviate from naturalism or ‘real’ life, we
must break the connection between image and sound. We pay
attention because it makes us see the film differently; it changes our
role as spectators. I find that enjoyable and I think audiences do,
too.’

Breaking this connection might involve seeing an action that has
no associated sound, or hearing elements that do not appear to
connect to the image. These inconsistencies force the audience to
instinctively resolve the disparity narratively, adding an extra
dimension to the filmmakers’ storytelling. As Walter Murch puts it, ‘By


choosing carefully what to eliminate, and then reassociating different
sounds that seem at first hearing to be somewhat at odds with the
accompanying image, the filmmaker can open up a perceptual
vacuum into which the mind of the audience must inevitably rush.’

Asynchrony is by no means an exact science, but it can be useful
to map out in a chart or graph the kinds of effects that can be
achieved with different degrees of disconnect.

At the most consistent end of the scale, where there is no
perceived tension between image and sound, the observer tends to
experience a scene in an observational mode. This appearance of
naturalism is usually the result of a great deal of design work, with
layers of sound added or removed to achieve a more elegant result,
and care taken so that no element appears missing, or out of place.

Moving up the scale, we can achieve increasingly complex
psychological effects. **Hyperrealism** is a device used in many styles
of film, where visual elements are accompanied by exaggerated
foley or spot effects to emphasize an action or amplify a moment. In
**shifted perspective**, the camera’s point of view is contrasted with a
different _point of audition_ . It is usually used to make the audience
feel emotionally closer to a character, or to introduce a sense of
subjectivity. One famous example of this approach is the spacewalk
scene in _2001: A Space Odyssey_ . In a wide shot of a spacecraft,
from which a tiny figure emerges, we hear the astronaut’s breathing
as though we are inside his spacesuit.

**Conscious experience or thought** can be implied by pulling
sound and image further apart, either removing a scene’s sounds to
suggest a character’s attention is turned inwards, or through the
addition of audible elements that appear to be present only in the
internal world of a character. They might represent memory,
reflection or imagination, or other features in the life of the mind.

Pulling further away from the scene and characters, dream-like
collages can be created, where image and sound have less and less
connection to each other. Depending on the context, this might be
interpreted as evoking a character’s **subconscious mind** - internal
activity of which they are not aware, which is a staple of Lynchian
films – or a **metaphor**, which might speak to the film’s overarching


themes, rather than suggesting anything about a particular
individual. One previously encountered example of metaphor is the
score of _Force Majeure_ ( _see_ Chapter 5), whose incendiary musical
motif is completely at odds with the manicured scenery, creating a rift
in the film world through which the truth can enter.

Director Andrei Tarkovsky speaks particularly highly of these
kinds of effects: ‘As soon as the sounds of the visible world...are
removed from it, or that world is filled...with extraneous sounds that
don’t exist literally, or if the real sounds are distorted so that they no
longer correspond with the image – then the film acquires a
resonance.’

In order to open our imagination to the possibilities of
asynchrony, it can be useful to initially watch the picture edit with the
guide sound turned off, to see if any interesting relationships come to
mind. It is also important not to fall into the trap of assuming that a
repetition of the same image, or location, necessitates a repetition of
the same sonic approach. The need to develop a strong sense of
emotion through the story far outweighs any issues of consistency,
even within the most naturalistic of films. For example, in _Sus_, a
story of police brutality that largely takes place within a single room,
the sound of the space changes radically from scene to scene to
track the shape of the story. This is not how spaces tend to behave
in everyday reality, but, if the emotion is nailed in each moment, the
audience will accept almost anything that is thrown at them within
their experience of the film.

Likewise, there is no requirement for the sound to slavishly follow
camera movement. This generally happens in video games, where
sound is used to give important cues for navigation, but in films the
point of audition may be kept anchored whilst the camera drifts
away, or vice versa. Alternatively, if the film is looking to create a
highly subjective first-person perspective, as found in the
collaborations of designer Tamás Zányi, director László Melis and
cinematographer Mátyás Erdély (particularly prominently in _Son of_
_Saul_ and _Sunset_ ), camera and sound may move together as one.
This creates a feeling of being closely tied to the protagonist as they
journey through the drama.


Last but not least, there is one very common area of sound–
image divergence that offers designers a huge array of possibilities.
This is the _off-screen space_ . Inexperienced directors may be wary of
hearing elements that they do not see within the frame – I have often
novice filmmakers doubting that an audience will accept a ticking
clock if they do not see one – but this fear is unfounded. Everyday
sounds tend to go unnoticed by viewers, but it can dramatically
change the overall feeling of a scene.

The off-screen category may also be extended to include any
elements that are sufficiently small or defocused to avoid being seen
clearly, any character speaking with their back to camera, and any
sound reproduction devices, such as radios, laptops and the like.
Each of these cases provides a great deal of leeway in terms of the
sounds that a director might wish to associate with them, without
creating the kind of narrative tension that comes with more obvious
forms of asynchrony.

#### **Acousmêtre**


Michel Chion describes a certain off-screen effect as the
_acousm_ ê _tre._ This is where a character is given a strange power by
being heard before an audience sees them, lending something of a
‘voice of god’ feel.

One example comes in _Two & Two_, a political allegory of control
and rebellion set within a classroom. The headmaster’s voice is
broadcast from a speaker, introducing new draconian rules for the
school. The disembodied voice has a mysterious quality, carrying
with it a sense of the following:











**being everywhere** (not being able to see where it comes from
lends a ubiquitous feel);
**seeing everything** (if it addresses us and we cannot see it, we
assume it is probably watching us);
**knowing everything** (if it watches our every move, it must know
everything about us).


_The Wizard of Oz_ commands great power through an artificially projected voice hidden
behind a mechanical facade, but loses this sense when the true source of the voice is
revealed. (Illustrated by W.W. Denslow.)


The second phase of this device comes when the _acousmatic_
subject is revealed visually, stripping away its mysterious aura. In
_2001: A Space Odyssey_, the ship’s computer HAL serves as an allpowerful antagonist for much of the film. Once lead character Dave
reaches the power source – an array of electronic modules – he
removes them one by one, slowly pulling back the wizard’s curtain
as HAL regresses into a child-like state of helplessness.


By choosing certain visual elements to pick out with sound, leaving
others unheard and adding their own unseen forces, the sound
designer can manipulate the sound–image relationship across the
timeline. It is possible to draw attention to or away from things within
the frame, build an off-screen world around it, and create poetic or
psychological effects through asynchrony. This collaborative sensory
process works towards a seamless fusion of sound and vision –


whether in harmony or contrast, both must come together to best tell
the story in each moment.


_[OceanofPDF.com](https://oceanofpdf.com/)_


# **10** **TIME**
## **_TRANSITIONS, RHYTHM AND_** **_TIME PERCEPTION_**

What is the essence of the director’s work? We could define it as sculpting in time.


_(Andrei Tarkovsky)_


One of the most fascinating powers of film is to represent,
manipulate and eclipse time. Connecting narrative points through
difference and repetition, it is possible to distort the flow of time
through a moment, or weave rhythmical patterns into the fabric of a
scene. Transitions can be sculpted for dramatic effect, multiple
timelines overlaid or time manifested in contrasting ways across
sound and image, creating complex effects that take the audience
beyond the world of their everyday experience.


Phelan, A.B., _Lunge to Sideward_ (1917).

### **TRANSITIONS**


Sound plays a key role in how audiences read the flow of time
through a scene. Where sound remains consistent across a picture
cut, a sense of continuation is created – the audience may be
observing or experiencing this new moment from a different visual
perspective, but it has followed straight on from the previous one. As
film theorist Tomlinson Holman puts it, ‘Presence and ambience help
to “sell” the continuity of a scene to the audience.’

By the same logic, it is also possible to do the opposite and
create a discontinuity of sound on a cut that makes it clear that time
has passed. This might be a small change in atmos, to show that the
action has moved forward slightly within the same scene, or a larger
one implying a more significant passage of time. For example, the
introduction of the sound of cicadas can tell the audience that
afternoon has become evening; similarly, birdsong may signify a
transition from night into morning. Changes in lighting may also
provide clear temporal cues, but at other times sound alone needs to
carry more of the narrative load.

Time jumps can also take the form of _flashbacks_ and
_flashforwards_, transporting the audience into parallel narrative
threads, and usually providing some insight into the events of the
primary timeline. This might be a memory that adds context to a
character’s current state of mind, or a future projection to show the
after-effects of a key moment. There are no hard and fast rules for
communicating these shifts, and image and sound usually work
together to make things clear to the viewer/listener. Production
design, colour grading, music and atmos can all help an audience to
distinguish between different timelines, with characterful elements
often underlining the moment we move from one to the other. During
such transitions, sounds of movement are often used as a _prelap_ - a
sound edit that comes in ahead of the picture cut. This might be a
passing train, a plane overhead, or the lapping waves, campfire
dances and desert convoys of _The English Patient_ ’s numerous
backward leaps.

These kinds of pre-emptive sound transitions may also be used
to more poetic effect, as Walter Murch advises: ‘There could be an
element of fruitful confusion for the audience, as they would not be
able to relate the sound to the visual except perhaps metaphorically.


They can only fully relate to it, in a realistic sense, after they have
seen the visual of the next scene.’ The order of the transition may
also be reversed, with the visual changing to a new scene whilst the
sound lingers in the previous one. This might be called a _postlap_ .
Where prelaps propel an audience towards the next scene, postlaps
tend to prolong the emotion of the outgoing scene a little longer. A
related effect involves applying long reverberation ( _see_ Chapter 11)
to the outgoing sounds of a scene, producing a tail that fades
gracefully into the following moment.

Overlapping sound transitions can also be used to create a sense
of continuous thread through a montage of different spaces. German
thriller movie _M_ famously made early use of this device, also known
as a _sound bridge_, where each transition involves a slow sound
dissolve, as we move through different strata of a society responding
to the news of an active serial killer. The sound bridges bring a
sense of connection, helping to communicate the idea of a city in
collective panic over a shared threat.

Whilst they provide powerful storytelling options, it is important to
avoid any over-use of these softer transitions. Often a scene change
calls for a jolting of the audience with a hard transition, which can be
slightly more challenging to achieve – a crossfade is more forgiving
than a cut – but some experimentation around the precise edit point
will usually result in a workable join. For a completely hard transition
with picture, the outgoing and incoming sets of atmos should both be
cut on the first frame of the new scene. As this can create an audible
click, the atmos should ideally be cut on a _zero crossing_, where the
sound wave has returned to the digital zero line.

These zero crossing moments can be placed on a boundary
between the two scenes, but if you simply shift the whole atmos
region along, you may break the alignment with the previous
transition. The solution is to make a cut somewhere within the scene,
creating two independently movable pieces with an internal
crossfade to hide the join. If your schedule does not allow this level
of detail around transition crafting, then a compromise is to put a
very small fade – of a few milliseconds – on the start or end of the
region to avoid a click.


### **COMPOUND TIME**

Sound also has the power to simultaneously overlay different
timelines on top of one other. One commonly used device is a piece
of dialogue that returns as a memory, as in the line ‘He’d kill us if he
had the chance’, from _The Conversation_ ( _see_ Chapter 8). We can
also use the functions of everyday technology to superimpose time,
with voicemails and other forms of audio recordings bringing the past
into the present. This can be used to great narrative effect, as in the
