finale of the Orson Welles classic _A Touch of Evil_, where there is a
subversion of the classic film noir ending. Hero Vargas hunts down
the corrupt and villainous cop Quinlan with a microphone in place of
a gun, as he tries to capture a confession on tape. Once the
incriminating words have been recorded, Quinlan is mortally
wounded, and as he dies his final words are repeated through the
small speaker of the tape recorder. In the original studio-controlled
version, the playback of the tape recording was cut, a decision that
the director addressed in a memo: ‘The funny little voice of
condemnation was meant to be a general comment on the story
itself. Let’s not lose it, please.’ For Welles this moment seems to be
an inversion of the Wizard of Oz effect ( _see_ Chapter 9). By assigning
the corrupt cop’s dismissive statements of guilt to a small distorted
speaker, he is stripped of his power.

Another evocative example comes in _Apocalypse Now_, during
Private Clean’s untimely demise. In this poignant scene, his squad
sails along a river from Vietnam to Cambodia, having just received a
postal delivery from the USA. Clean is listening to a tape of his
mother relaying news from home when their boat is attacked, and he
suffers a mortal wound. His mother’s voice, and the loving tone it
brings, then continues to run over Clean’s final moments; by
overlaying this safe familial mood on to a world of war and death, the
sense of tragedy is poetically underlined.

### **TIME PERCEPTION**


#### **Rhythm and Tempo**

Composition of sound can clearly be considered in terms of pace,
but it can also work as a form of music-like punctuation, adding
shape, pause, bracketing or accentuation to the journey of meaning
and emotion through a scene. A sound effect at just the right moment
might remind the audience of a key story element, draw attention to
significant events, or help lift an actor’s delivery where the energy is
not quite there. A brief break in the sound, known as a _beat_, can also
work to accent the instant that follows.


In the human experience of life, time rarely flows in a linear fashion, but bends, jerks,
compresses and expands. This phenomenon can be harnessed in cinema, influencing
audience perception in the service of story.


‘Rhythm is a great tool to play with tension in a scene or to drive
it forward,’ sound effects editor Dayo James ( _Yesterday_, _Devs_ )
explains. ‘Any time there is an opportunity to play with a rhythmical
element it’s always fun – a clock, or waves, heart rate monitors,
trains, idling engines, washing machines, a barn door banging open


and closed, a dripping tap, a squeaky swing – the world is so full of
rhythm. Often the way a scene is cut will imply a rhythm and I will
use a rhythmic element to help highlight that.’ Transitions can also
be made more satisfying when we think of sound effects in terms of
musical bars and phrases, ‘so that the dripping tap or crashing wave
coincides with the cut, or so the cut implies the next beat of the bar’.

These kinds of sound patterns can help to anchor a scene,
playing a similar role to the rhythm section of a musical ensemble. In
the disorientating opening of _Saving Private Ryan_, sound effects
editor Teresa Eckton describes how she ‘came up with a certain
pattern, or rhythm for cutting these machine guns for the background
battle, so there was some form’, helping the audience make sense of
the chaos. This idea of structure through organized sound can also
be used as a stylistic device, as it was in _Delicatessen_ ’s famously
rhythmic scene, which intercuts between the activities of tenants in a
dilapidated apartment building, as they move in unison to an everaccelerating pulse. The sounds of bed springs, paint rollers, and
bicycle pumps form repeating rhyming patterns that draw the
characters together in a highly satisfying and humorous way.

Sounds may also create causal chains, as in the dog woof that
sets off a domino effect of other barking dogs in a quiet suburban
scene. This device might be used to create an explicit sense of
drama. Call and response might also be used as a guiding principle
in a sound edit, choreographing an interconnected world of audible
agents – a bird in conversation with a distant pneumatic drill, for
example – bedded into a background layer that the audience takes
for granted. In a more explicit example from comedy-drama
_Blindspotting_, a rhythmic car alarm underscores a traumatic event,
returning as a memory later that night, which audibly _match cuts_ into
a pulsing alarm clock the next morning. This regular pulse is then
used throughout the film, and as a crucial callback in the finale,
setting up a dramatic realization. Throughout the film, this rhythmic
pattern functions as a motif that endlessly reminds the character, and
the audience, of the original trauma.

All of these examples involve rhythm as a tool of connection and
cohesion; the cadence of dialogue, the beat of music, the pattern of
effects, and the stillness of absence are all strands in the temporal


thread that guides the viewer through a scene. When finalizing the
sound effects, ideally the sound team will have access to the
completed dialogue edit and reference music timings to play off,
otherwise some degree of fine-tuning may need to happen once
everything is brought together during the final mix. Audible rhythms
should not be overly slavish to the image in terms of sync – often, a
more elegant and musical pattern can be found that is not
completely frame accurate to the on-screen action, but close enough
to appear synced. A common example comes in footsteps across a
cut, where the best visual edit between two shots may not
necessarily maintain a rhythm of movement that sounds completely
natural. Here the foley may be performed in such a way as to
smooth out any inconsistencies, ramping up or down in pace to
avoid a jolt.

#### **Phrasing**


Just as in music, different phrasings of rhythmic sound can affect the
listener in emotionally distinct ways. Groupings of three can give a
sense of pulsing rotation, always returning to the same point, whilst
fours can be more driving, pushing ever forwards. Fives on the other
hand tend to bring a sense of irregularity, making the listener wait
slightly longer than feels natural for the next refrain.

As the human brain is naturally drawn to symmetric patterns, a
pattern that cannot be equally divided into two tends to draw more
attention. This is the reason why alarm and phone sounds tend to
repeat in fives, sevens, and other odd numbers: **Ring** _**beat beat**_
_**beat beat**_ **| Ring** _**beat beat beat beat**_ is more likely to attract the
attention than a pattern in four or eight time. When cutting attentiongrabbing sounds like these, you need to ensure that you mirror this
real-world design if you want to achieve a convincing result.


The bracketing of repeating elements – visualized here in options of fives or threes –
changes the way a sequence strikes the listener.


The ways in which sounds are clustered together can also
change our interpretation of their underlying source: to paraphrase a
certain Bond villain, once is chance, twice is coincidence, the third
time is a pattern. With each repetition comes a greater sense of
intention and, as a result, a particular impact on the scene.
Groupings of three also seem to have some kind of special
resonance. The _rule of threes_ as it applies to story structure
(beginning, middle, end), or visual composition’s _rule of thirds_, have
a mirror phenomenon in sound. Often, a series of three dog barks or
car horns just seems to be more satisfying in some primal way that
any other number.

Alongside different phrasing patterns, changing the rate at which
sounds enter, disappear, rise and fall can also have a big impact on
how a scene lands, working to modulate how fast or slow the
passing of time will seem to an audience. Chase scenes typically
use up-tempo music and effects to bring a sense of urgency, whilst
dream sequences may rely on drawn-out sound patterns to pull the
audience into their surreal space. Sounds may even be stretched out
indefinitely, with no obvious sense of tempo. Known as the _drone_,


this device is usually used to create tension or an unsettling mood.
Something about the relentless absence of pattern provokes anxiety
in the observer, so it is an especially useful trick for thrillers and
horror films.

These examples occupy the extreme ends of the spectrum, but
most scenes take a dynamic approach within this range, enhancing
the dramatic shape with a deftness of touch. Designer Ai-Ling Lee
( _Watchmen_, _Jojo Rabbit_ ) offers an example of how to approach an
escalating confrontation: ‘It might start off quiet, then gradually you
hear a whispering wind, then building into low, buffeting wind, and
the whole scene ends with one of the actors yelling out, and all of
that wind cuts out when the argument hits a peak.’ In this way, a
sound effects editor will often work as an inconspicuous composer,
providing an underscore that supports the dramatic shape of the
scene, whilst flying under the conscious radar of the audience.

#### **Aligning Character and Audience**


Rhythm and tempo may also be expressed through a character’s
breath. The rate and pattern of their breathing can say a lot about
how a character is feeling, from the slow regular cycles of
contentment to the fast erratic respiration of the fearful.

The effect on the audience may even be more direct. According
to Michel Chion, ‘breathing noises in a film can directly affect our
own respiration’. Walter Murch makes a similar physiological
assertion, suggesting that audiences might blink ‘more or less in
unison’ if a film’s edit is aligned with a character’s internal rhythms.

Sound may also influence audience psychology in relation to time
perception. Some sounds encourage relaxation, whilst others
increase anxiety. When a person is relaxed, time appears to pass
more slowly, whilst the opposite is true during heightened moments.
Thinking about time also seems to slow it down, as in the proverb ‘a
watched pot never boils’.

Making use of these principles, a sound editor might work with
unpredictable rhythms and variations in volume to put the audience
on edge, speeding up perceived time. Emphasized synchronicity


between sound and image can also charge a scene with pace and
impact. Both these factors are in play during the dinosaur conflict of
_Fantasia_, which uses Igor Stravinsky’s _Rite of Spring_ as its score.
This piece is famous for its rhythmic irregularity, with jagged stabs
falling in unpredictable ways, keeping the audience on its toes. This
effect is coupled with a picture edit that cuts on the strongest beats,
propelling the action forwards.

On the other hand, in order to slow the perception of time, regular
rhythms and volumes can be used to lull the audience into a sense
of certainty. A sparse mix, which under-stimulates the sensory
faculties, or the sound of clocks, which combine regularity with a
literal reminder of time, will also increase the sense of how long a
moment takes. By way of example, the lead-up to a royal execution
in _To Kill A King_ is drawn out by the regular striking of a bell, playing
over shots of a hushed crowd. Sound and image work together to
stretch out the monarch’s final moments. The slowed sense of time
creates a contemplative mood, giving the audience a chance to
reflect on what has led them to this point.


**TIME ACTIVITY A**


Capture a series of short video clips on your smartphone, steering some towards
stillness and creating movement in others.

Bring the videos into your editing software and try overlaying sounds with different
densities and rhythms to see how this changes the sense of time flow through the
shot.


Using patterns, superimpositions and transitions, it is possible to
sculpt a sense of time as it flows through a film. Additionally, when
we listen back to our designs, we quickly become aware of how time
affects our own perception; repeatedly listening to a moment in
isolation gives a completely different experience from hearing an
idea in a run of the film. Given that the vast majority of the audience
will hear our work only once, we must work hard to stay as close as
possible to the first-time listener perspective throughout the design
process.

# **11** **SPACE**
## **_ACOUSTICS, SILENCE AND THE_** **_FREQUENCY SPECTRUM_**

The sound of nothing – that’s the strongest sound in the world.


_(Frank Warner)_


Sound is a powerful tool for creating a sense of expanse around, and
within, the dimensions of a story. This process may be additive, to
embellish environments and simulate realistic effects, or it may be
used to subtract layers from the mix, to sculpt a more artful result.
Building on previously explored ideas ( _see_ Chapter 3), this chapter
will dig deeper into three spatial features: acoustic space, the
frequency spectrum and negative space.


Harrison, T., _Media Library at Azkuna Zentroa_ (2019).

### **ACOUSTICS**


Soundscapes are characterized not only by the audible sources
within them, but also by the way their physical environment shapes


what is heard. When sound waves hit a surface, they are partially
reflected and partially absorbed. This creates various acoustic
effects, including _echo, reverberation_ and _resonance_ . These are the
three that perhaps offer the most interesting possibilities for sound
design.


Surfaces can act as acoustic mirrors, echoing back sound as distinct repeats.


**Echo** is a familiar concept to most; if we shout towards a
mountain, or a distant building, we might hear a repetition of the
sound, as it bounces off the surface and comes back to us. The
effect takes its name from the Greek myth of Echo and Narcissus, in


which Echo uses her silver tongue to deceive a goddess and is
punished with a curse that forces her to voice only the last words
said to her, reflecting them back into the world. After falling for the
self-loving Narcissus and being rejected, she fades away, leaving
only the echoes of her voice behind.

**Reverberation**, or ‘reverb’ for short, happens when individual
echoes bounce back and forth between surfaces, forming a _tail_ of
decaying sound, which continues after the original sound stops. The
intensity and length of the tail is dependent on the distance between
the surfaces, and how reflective they are. Small sound booths with
padded walls tend to have very short and muted reverb, whilst stone
churches produce very obvious, long tails. We tend to think of reverb
as an interior phenomenon, but it also occurs in spaces such as
forests, where trees act as reflective surfaces, and in streets, where
we often hear one obvious echo, or _slapback_, alongside a reverb tail.

**Resonance** is the effect of certain frequencies, or pitches,
building up due to reflections that add together, a phenomenon
known as _wave interference_ . This happens most obviously in
regularly shaped rooms and is one reason why people enjoy singing
in the shower – resonance works to enhance the bass of the voice,
leading to a fuller sound. It can cause problems in interior dialogue
recordings, where certain syllables jump out, as the actor happens to
deliver them at the precise pitch that causes the room to resonate.

In addition to simulating these effects in the service of realism, or
dealing with them on problematic recordings, we can also put them
to evocative effect in our designs. Walter Murch’s reflections on
space are interesting: ‘I am very emotionally moved by the space
around a sound. I almost think that sometimes I am recording space
with a sound in it, rather than sound in a space.’

In _Citizen Kane_ ’s jigsaw puzzle scene, the protagonist and his
wife have a conversation in a large hall of their palatial home. They
begin in opposite corners of the room, and their voices reverberate
out as they project their words to each other. However, the effect
continues just as strongly once they are standing next to each other.
In this way, the distance in their relationship is effectively
communicated through sound.


Echoes build up in more enclosed spaces, bouncing off the walls, ceiling and floor to create
the diffuse effect of reverb.


Alvin Lucier’s sound piece _I Am Sitting In A Room_ takes an even
more unusual approach to worldizing ( _see_ Chapter 4). During the
performance, a few sentences of text are read into a microphone
connected to a recorder. The recording is then played back into the


space, and re-recorded, taking on more of the acoustic properties of
the room. This re-recording process is repeated numerous times,
until the intelligible words have turned into a droning mush of reverb
and resonance – a fingerprint of the room’s acoustics.

Acoustic effects happen in these particular ways due to the
behaviour of sound as a wave. It is not necessary to understand all
the underlying physics in order to be effective sound designers, but it
can help to have a basic grasp of how things work.

The image of the sound wave in water ( _see_ below) shows how
individual sounds have different properties:










**amplitude:** _from loud to quiet_ (seen in the height of the water
ripples);
**pitch:** _from high to low_ (seen in how spread out the ripples are);
**length:** _from short to_ _sustained_ (the length of time between the
first ripples forming, and the water surface returning to a state of
stillness).



The sound editor is continually considering these properties when
constructing scenes – quiet sounds can become lost under loud
ones, sounds at a similar pitch can clash – and tends to use a
combination of momentary and continuous sounds in order to create
a balanced mix.


A low-pitched sound wave rippling the surface of water.


**SPACE ACTIVITY A**


Using your smartphone, or a portable recorder, capture some loud noises in a variety
of different rooms. These could be shouts, hand claps, or any other kind of
percussive sound, allowing you to hear clearly echoes and reverb tails, and any
resonant effects of the room. The louder the sound you make, the more obvious the
acoustic phenomena will be.

In a small space such as a cupboard or shower, you may also be able to play with
resonance directly, varying the pitch of your voice to find frequencies that are
amplified or diminished due to wave interference.

#### **Recording Spaces**


Just as a cinematographer can vary the frame size, from establishing
shots to close-ups to wide shots, the sound team can focus their
recordings on small details, or opt for a wider perspective. Close-mic
technique results in less reverb compared with the dry signal, while
pulling back will capture more sound from the space. It can also be
useful to make multiple recordings from different perspectives, giving
different acoustic flavours as options for the edit.


Recordings of spaces are usually made in stereo or surround, to
give width and depth information for the mix. There are many
different permutations of mic configurations, but the basic choice is
between a _coincident set-up_, or a _spaced array_ .

#### _STEREO SET-UPS_

Coincident mic technique involves getting the mics as close to each
other as possible, pointed in different directions. You may even have
access to _single-point_ stereo or surround mics, which have this
arrangement hard-wired into them. Coincident set-ups are easy to rig
and produce an accurate sound image but can feel less open and
spacious than the alternatives.


In the X–Y coincident pair set-up, two matching mono directional mics are used, placed as
close together as possible, without touching each other. In a narrow space they should be
angled at 90 degrees to each other, increasing to a maximum of 140 degrees for wider
spaces.


Hanging a stereo spaced array over a coat hanger is an old location recording trick, with the
rig easily being suspended from ledges or branches.


Spaced arrays involve physical distance between the mics, which
can be varied to increase or decrease the perceived width.
Directional mics may be used, although omnidirectional mics can
produce a more faithful result.


The spaced-array technique can be taken into even more faithful
territory using a _binaural_ set-up, which closely emulates the way
sound travels into the ears. This is achieved either by using a
dummy human head, with omni mic capsules embedded into the
moulded lug holes, or by using special omni mics that sit in the
recordist’s ears. This system models how air flows around the head,
reaching the ears at slightly different times to give front-rear
localization abilities, alongside a sense of height. When listening
back on headphones, this perceived three-dimensional effect can be
remarkably realistic, transporting us right into the recording
environment. As the effect does not work so well over speakers,
binaural has not traditionally been a cinematic sound tool, but, as
films are increasingly being streamed from home, this format is
becoming more of a creative consideration.


A binaural dummy head, capturing the experience of standing directly behind a double bass
trio.

#### _SURROUND SET-UPS_


Surround formats take these stereo principles and add a controllable
dimension of depth, offering much more flexibility than can be
achieved with the ‘baked-in’ binaural recording effect. Four equally
spaced omni mics can produce a good surround image, or a _double_
_M-S (mid-side)_ configuration can be put together. The M-S technique
involves a cardioid (or super/hypercardioid) mic pointing forwards to
capture the centre of a soundscape and a figure-of-eight rotated 90
degrees, which rejects sound from the front and back, but captures
the sides. The figure-of-eight mic is mono, but, as sounds coming
from each side are exactly out of phase with each other, a stereo
image can be constructed using a clever phase cancellation
technique, separating left from right. This can be done manually, or
by using an M-S decoder plugin. M-S has the advantage of allowing
the stereo width to be controlled by changing the balance of mid
versus side, and also offers mono compatibility; the mid channel can
be used on its own as a mono atmos track, or a spot effect. A double
M-S set-up can be creating by adding an extra rear-facing cardioid
mic to a standard M-S set-up, with front and rear able to share the
same figure-of-eight mic for the side information.


A double M-S set-up, rigged within a shock mount. This is using a forward-facing singlepoint M-S mic at the bottom of the rig, and a rear-facing mono hypercardioid on top.


stereo, giving a great degree of flexibility in post-production.

#### **Psychoacoustics**


In addition to the physical world shaping what we hear, the brain also
has a big part to play in processing the sound waves that strike the
eardrums. Our _psychoacoustic_ _processes_ include the ability to
localize sound, using the minute differences between the signals in
each ear to extrapolate a three-dimensional environment, and
temporal masking ( _see_ Chapter 8), which allows us to hide
troublesome audio issues under a louder sonic event.

These examples are of great practical use in the work of the
sound designer, but other psychoacoustic effects can create a
challenge, as they function in the real world but break down in the
cinema. Rick Altman gives one example: ‘When we listen for a crying
child, we are marvellously effective at cutting out extraneous sounds
and concentrating on the cries that we recognize as those of our
child. Dubbed the “cocktail party effect” by Collin Cherry, the process
of selective audio attention is far more difficult when we are listening
to recorded material.’ A recording can often seem less sharp
compared with an in-the-moment listening experience, which is one
reason why it is a good idea to build up sound edits from individual
layers of isolated sound, giving precise control over the balance of
elements. In this way, you can focus attention on particular sounds
and achieve the desired levels of clarity and density. You can also
mirror this process as you think about a scene, adding only the
elements in your imagination that the story is asking us for.


Often, the foreground elements must be exaggerated to ensure they stand out from the
background; this is known as the ‘cocktail party effect’.


The brain also has a tendency to group together similar elements
and interpret them as a single entity. If sounds begin and end at the
same time and are located at the same point within acoustic space,
they appear to the listener as a single element. This allows a sound
to be injected with a different character by adding additional layers,
whilst still retaining the details of the original source. When editing
sound effects for _Top Gun_, Cece Hall spent a week recording jets,
but found the results ‘kind of wimpy’ on their own. She had the idea
of combining wild animal sounds in sync with the jet engines, a
hugely labour-intensive process, but one which brought just the right
flavour, lending the planes a ‘cutting, sharp feeling’.

The opposite effect of treating sounds very differently to clearly
divide them into multiple spaces is another common technique of
sound design. This might be the muffling of an argument, which
indicates it is happening next door, or the device can be used as a
central narrative sound concept that runs through the film. In _The_
_Guilty_, much of the action takes place at the other end of a phone
line, whilst the camera remains within the police call centre where
the protagonist works. The treatment of the phoned-in audio creates
an entirely separate sense of space, with the majority of characters
and their environments known only through sound. This give the
audience creative licence over their imagined image. In other
moments, the sound cuts out almost entirely, bringing them into the
character’s inner world and prompting them to imagine the thoughts
racing through his mind. Delineating techniques such as these can
create distinctive narrative sound spaces for the story to flow
through.

### **THE FREQUENCY SPECTRUM**


There is another kind of space within sound that is determined by the
range of pitches we are able to perceive. The lowest frequency we
can hear is around 20Hz – this pitch involves molecules in the air
moving back and forth at a rate of twenty times a second. The upper
limit of a person’s hearing runs to 20kHz (twenty thousand cycles per


second), although this value tends to decrease with age. Sounds
above this cut-off go unnoticed by a human, although pets can hear
far higher – dogs up to 45kHz, and cats to 79kHz. At the lower end of
the spectrum, geological events such as earthquakes, and some
subwoofers, can produce sound below 20Hz, which can be felt in the
body at sufficient amplitudes, but not heard directly through the ears.


Just as light sources can be broken down into their component visible frequencies, it is also
possible to analyse sounds in terms of the frequencies they contain.


If elements are to be clearly audible, they must not be clashing
with other sounds in the same area of the frequency spectrum.
Where two elements play at similar pitches, they may obscure each
other, in a phenomenon known as _spectral masking._ This effect can
appear particularly bad at lower frequencies, and one key task for
the re-recording mixer is to avoid such clashes between different
tracks within sound and music. This masking effect may be turned to
our advantage when dealing with noisy production sound; by adding
atmos or music that has strong activity in a similar frequency area to
the noise, we can cover it up if mixed in at a sufficient level.


#### **Layering**

When using layers in a sound edit, we often look to find sounds that
complement each other in terms of their frequency spread. Impact
elements might be created using a combination of a crisp highfrequency recording that tells the audience what the sound is, and a
low, rounded element that brings a sense of weight and character.
Atmos is often tracklayed in a similar way, with higher elements
bringing a sense of ‘air’, and bassier layers creating warmth and
solidity, although we sometimes find that a single recording alone
can communicate this full-spectrum spread. Either way, a rich, fullsounding background bed is a big part of giving scenes a cinematic
feel.

#### **Pitch Layering and Shifting**


The same sound can also be used simultaneously at different
pitches to create a bigger, more complex result, in a process Ric
Viers calls _pitch layering_ . A sound is copied to multiple tracks, and
then experiments are made with applying different amounts of pitch
shifting and time offsetting between the layers. This is a particularly
useful aesthetic for sci-fi: ‘Extreme amounts of this technique can
create explosions that trail away into infinity; small amounts can
suggest movement or pass-bys.’


A frequency display chart of the sound of a large steel sheet being bowed. Time runs along
the horizontal axis and frequency on the vertical. The brightness of colour represents how
loud each frequency is during each moment.


Pitch shifting may also be used to create interesting relationships
between tonal sound effects and music. Something might be pitched
into key with the underlying score, or pitched differently to cause
dissonance. When making use of pitch shifting, a more naturalsounding result will be achieved when it is applied without _time_
_correction_, so that pitch and speed change equally, as in the slowing
down of a tape or a record. This is known in the digital domain as
_resampling_, and does not usually add any noticeable artifacts, other
than the loss of high frequencies when pitching down, or low ones
when pitching up. On the other hand, whilst time-corrected pitch
shifting has the advantage of keeping things lined up in time, its
more complex digital algorithms can easily introduce syntheticsounding artifacts, especially when pushed harder.

#### **EQ**


In addition to using layering and pitch shifting to control the spread of
frequencies, we can also utilize EQ ( _see_ Chapter 3) to shape
sounds, helping them sit better together. It is important not to go too


far, as this can make things sound unnatural, but, when used with
care, EQ can improve a mix dramatically. In speech, consonants
tend to fall from 2kHz up, and are crucial for intelligibility, so it may
be necessary to boost the top end of dialogue to help it cut through
the mix, for example. EQ can be a little daunting at first, but we can
quickly learn specific tricks, and in time train our ears to quickly
pinpoint the area of the frequency spectrum where a problem is
occurring.

### **NEGATIVE SPACE**


In addition to considering sound as frequencies that are present, it
can sometimes be helpful to think about what is absent – the
_negative space_ of the sound track. This might be a pregnant pause
before a crucial line is delivered, or the moments that realistic
sounds are drained away from a scene to draw the audience into a
character’s internal world.


Just as the blacks of an image can be visualized as white in a negative, it is useful to think
about about how a lack of certain sounds may have a positive effect on a story.


When working to establish narrative elements, it is vital to ensure
that there is enough space around them, giving the symbols room to
breathe. This is particularly important with language. ‘Silence
remains, inescapably, a form of speech (in many instances, of
complaint or indictment).’ Susan Sontag wrote, ‘and an element in a


dialogue…as the prestige in language falls, that of silence rises.’ By
creating space around things, both in terms of time and frequency,
they will come across more strongly to the audience. Going even
further, an old mixing trick uses a similar principle for extreme sound
effects; an instant of pure silence inserted just before a very loud
sound, such as an explosion, will make it sound even louder.

Legendary director Alfred Hitchcock was one of the first to truly
explore the potential of silence within sound film. As Elizabeth Weis
observes of his work, ‘One central motif is silence, which confers
(negative) moral values on a character associated with it. A
character’s avoidance of speech may be a symptom of ruthless
efficiency, emotional immaturity, or moral paralysis. Thus, villains
who murder noiselessly, heroes afraid to express their love, and
heroines who conceal evidence of a crime are all related in the
Hitchcockian scheme.’

More extended moments of absolute silence can also be
employed to great effect. They may be used in an end credit
sequence to create a strange, reflective atmosphere, or at dramatic
moments such as in the _2001: A Space Odyssey_ airlock scene, in
which the protagonist forces himself back on to the ship, despite
antagonist HAL’s best efforts. Removing the sonic comfort blanket
can be shocking for the audience, creating a palpable sense of
discomfort, much as when the music accidentally cuts out at a party.

In a related, but more subtle technique, silence can be used to
build tension, creating a void that the audience expects to be filled at
any moment. In these cases, the sound designer will rarely use
literal silence to communicate the mood, but rather find cinematic
forms of emptiness to do the work. It could be a symbolic sound,
such as wind blowing through an interior space, or the smallest of
sounds, such as a ticking clock or breath, heard very clearly, which
serves to highlight the absent sounds of dialogue or action. This
effect can be used in domestic situations, communicating distanced
family relationships through the awkward atmosphere around a
dinner table, or employed in high-stakes scenarios. In Werner
Herzog’s _Aguirre, Wrath of God_, one of the characters describes the
moment when ‘one of the men panicked because of the silence
which always comes just before someone gets killed’.


Lastly, as in the example of _The Guilty_, silence can also work to
draw the audience into the internal world of a character. Bong Hoon
Jo’s directed segment of _Tokyo!_ uses a similar effect, when the
protagonist sees the object of his desire through a glass door. The
cicadas in the environment gradually fade away, and when the
moment is shown to be an illusion, they cut back in. This creates a
momentarily enchanted and intimate effect for the audience, pulling
them closer in, before snapping back into reality.


Whether applying acoustic effects, stripping elements away or
layering up across the spectrum, the sound designer’s use of space
is a key consideration. We often work with a huge number of
disparate layers to build a rich cinematic world, mixing them carefully
to achieve the desired result. Sitting at the other end of the scale,
silence, in all its cinematic flavours, remains one of the most
powerful paints in the sound design palette.


_[OceanofPDF.com](https://oceanofpdf.com/)_


# **12** **AESTHETIC**
## **_EXPLORING THE OVERALL FEEL_** **_OF A SOUND TRACK_**

I do not always know what I want, but I do know what I don’t want.


_(Stanley Kubrick)_


How do we know when a sound is right? Top-down design
frameworks ( _see_ Chapters 5 and 6) help to steer our choices, but the
emotional sense of whether something _feels_ right within a scene is
the real test. The assessment of how well a sound works in any
given moment is governed by a complex set of factors, from
individual taste and genre conventions, to the particular sonic
aesthetics that the chosen tools and methods bring along with them.


Wadsworth, E., _Dazzle-ships in Drydock at Liverpool_ (1919). NATIONAL GALLERY OF CANADA

### **STYLISTIC APPROACHES**


As you begin to discuss a project with a director, it is a good idea to
get a feel for what they have in mind. It can be incredibly difficult to


describe sound directly, but finding reference points to other work
can provide a pivot for discussion. These reference points might
relate to other films, but radio pieces or musical works might also
prove to be useful – anything that helps you to establish a sense of
an overall sonic aesthetic. If a director comes with references, be
sure to chat them through to ensure you understand what it is they
like about each piece. In most projects, the director’s ideas about
sound follow the image to a greater or lesser extent, but at times the
reverse is true. When director Ian Pons Jewell and I worked together
on an experimental project, I created a sound mood sketch for each
scene ahead of writing and storyboarding. Then, as he recalls, ‘I
would listen to the sound design mood you created, then write the
scenes as I listened to the soundscape.’ Similarly, David Lynch finds
that sometimes ‘sounds will conjure image...so sound is what came
first’.

More often, though, it is the image that the director has in their
head before they consider sound in any detail. It can sometimes be
beneficial for the sound designer to suggest their own sound
references (scenes or shorts may be more useful than feature films),
to allow the director to test their mental image against some concrete
sound examples, to see how they play out. Of course, directors are
usually trying to accomplish something that they feel is unique in
terms of its precise aesthetic make-up – references are better seen
as points from which pivot, rather than ready-made ideas to be
selected. If schedules allow, a cross-department research screening
can also be a valuable process, opening up interesting avenues of
discussion. Having all of the key creative perspectives in dialogue
with each other can enrich the narrative process immensely.

Once the initial research process is complete, experiments and
sketches can be helpful to further develop a film’s sonic aesthetic by
making the ideas more tangible. As explored in Chapter 5, these
could take the form of environmental sketches or sound world
collages, which can be discussed both narratively and in terms of
overall style. This process is useful even when a director ends up
rejecting ideas, as it helps point to other potential dead ends in our
creative explorations.


Through development, and on until the end of the creative
process, there is a continual evolution of the sense of what is right
for the film. ‘Each film is a universe unto itself,’ reflects Walter Murch.
‘Everyone who works on it, director included, is trying to discover the
laws by which this universe works: what kind of character, action,
colour, tempo, sense of reality, sense of humour suits the film.
They’re particular to each film – what works on one film won’t
necessarily work on another. And as they evolve, you absorb these
laws so completely that they become unconscious reflexes, almost
like being infected with a benign virus.’

### **NATURALISM AND BEYOND**


On a more general scale, the stylistic approach relates to how
faithfully the sound should stick to the basic sense of shared reality,
from realism and hyperrealism to surrealism. Which of these are
potential ingredients for any given film can usually be determined at
an early stage and will vary across different scenes; for example, a
broadly realist drama might contain dreamier sequences that allow
for surrealist explorations, as in _Happy-Go-Lucky_, directed by Mike
Leigh.

#### **Realism**


One phrase I have often heard from the mouth of an inexperienced
director is that a ‘scene should sound _normal_ ’. This is no less absurd
than requesting a ‘normal’ frame composition, or a ‘normal’
performance from an actor. Every scene needs to have narrative and
emotional intent, and every creative decision adds to the overall
effect. When a director uses the term ‘normal’, they usually mean a
naturalistic and observational feel. This sets certain limitations on the
sound team’s options – they will probably have to work with diegetic
sounds only and resist more pronounced subjective techniques – but
it does allow a huge amount of leeway. The off-screen space offers
near endless creative potential, and the aesthetic quality of


significant sounds allows us to speak to how a character is feeling,
or to say something about the story. If you find that a director is
struggling to give an overall brief for a scene, ask how they would
like the audience to _feel_, and what information they should be
receiving. You can then respond with sound-related ideas.

A technically realist approach to sound can even come across as
highly experimental at times, as in the _Dogme 95_ films or in many of
the films of the Nouvelle Vague. In those works, a very limited
amount of post-production resulted in a very raw, direct effect, which
can be quite a challenging listen compared with the massaged
aesthetics of Hollywood and similar styles of filmmaking. These
minimalistic cinematic movements were about foregrounding
authenticity, with the idea that a more immediate, less worked-up
process would remain more faithful to the truth of the moment.

#### **Hyperrealism**


Hyperrealism ( _see_ also Chapter 9) is a sense of exaggeration to
create a punchier and more focused result. This is partly about
making things sound closer and richer, and foley plays a big part in
selling this aesthetic, as does close-miced dialogue, whether from
production sound or ADR.

This approach also requires some lateral thinking when it comes
to sound effects. ‘How do you produce sounds that have “amplified
reality”?’ asks Randy Thom. ‘You begin by trying to forget for a while
what the Nazi tank in an Indiana Jones film would “really” sound like,
and start thinking about what it would _feel_ like in a nightmare. The
treads would be like spinning samurai blades. The engine would be
like the growl of an angry beast. You then go out and find sounds
that have those qualities, or alter sounds to make them have those
qualities.’

Another aspect of hyperrealism is the adding of sound elements
to actions that would in fact make no noise in reality. Perhaps
bleeping and swishing interface sounds might be added to a police
computer system, or whooshes to a camera _whip pan_ or _crash_
_zoom_ . This form of hyperrealism can come across as somewhat


cartoonish, adding rhythm and energy to action or comedy, but often
feeling out of place in other styles of film.

#### **Surrealism**


Beyond exaggeration, impressionistic or dream-like sound worlds
can be explored to evoke strange atmospheres and channel
information to the subconscious mind. The style of sound design
may bear very little relationship to reality, or the on-screen image,
and so requires a particularly lateral design process. There is a
humorous anecdote of surrealism going awry in David Lynch and
Alan Splet’s reflections on _The Elephant Man_ post-production
process. In support of the picture edit, the two had worked together
on a surreal sound edit, with unusual choices and transitions that
were intended to take the audience deep into the mind of the
protagonist. Once the locked edit was turned over to the British
studio post-production team, however, the design work was
assumed to be a mistake. The British team worked diligently to ‘fix’
the sound edit, restoring a sense of realism throughout. When Lynch
and Splet returned to the movie, the tracks had to be changed back
again, to reflect their original intent. The style is more commonplace
in cinema now, but at the time it was something of a radical
departure from the norm. Similar setbacks can still occur today if
different aesthetic perspectives control different stages of the
workflow. On a 2017 episode of _Black Mirror_, editor Tom Lindsay and
director John Hillcoat had to fight to restore the sense of sonic
impressionism that had been created during the offline edit, and had
somehow been lost along the way through to the mix.

### **GENRE CONVENTIONS**


When talking about the kind of film that is being made, genre is
another helpful signifier. What might read as a straightforward drama
on the page might be envisaged by the director to include elements
of comedy or horror, for example. Each genre label carries a set of


aesthetic traditions or conventions, or _tropes_ ; where there is a hybrid
of genres, the creative team must determine the balance between
the elements, and in which scenes to foreground each component.


A film that combines the genres of horror and animation must find the right balance of the
sonic aesthetics associated with each stylistic component.


These conventions can be considered in terms of _memetics_  - the
virus-like spread of ideas through a population, now happening


increasingly quickly online, with associations forming in a culture’s
collective unconscious. Why, when we see a full moon in a horror
film, do sound effect editors often lay up a howling wolf? This
presumably has its roots in European werewolf lore, which itself
harks back to earlier mythologies. These kinds of associations
combine to define a particular genre, leading to a hierarchy of default
options, which help to ground the experience of the audience. This
might be seen as creatively limiting, but the best genre work is shot
through with vivid originality, whilst still participating in the evolution
of convention.

Horror contains many other conventions: the creaking off-screen
door, the unsettling drone, a very quiet moment dashed by the huge
impact of a _jump scare_ . Science fiction, on the other hand, allows for
synthesized and heavily processed sounds, which might feel out of
place elsewhere, while animations often take a more minimal
approach to the sound edit, dispensing with detailed layers such as
foley moves and realistic atmos, in favour of a more artful collage.

Whilst acknowledging and working with conventions, it is
important not to fall squarely into the realm of cliché. As David
Sonnescein puts it, ‘Don’t settle for the expected, common sounds. It
will bore you and the audience.’


**AESTHETIC ACTIVITY A**


Choose a particular genre, which you feel uses sound in an evocative, but perhaps
somewhat conventional way, such as horror, fantasy or sci-fi.

Watch a number of examples of the genre and see if you can spot similar
devices, or tropes, happening in different films.

You might also want to expand this exercise beyond sound design and see if you
can spot other genre tropes happening in the writing, cinematography, production
design, editing and music.

### **DOCUMENTARY**


As well as providing entertainment and escapism, most
screenwriters attempt to explore some kind of underlying truth in


their work. When it comes to documentary, this aspect of storytelling
comes into sharper focus, with audiences usually expecting a more
authentic representation of reality, with an honest approach to
storytelling that stays true to the spirit of the subject matter. However,
in every creative decision, across direction, framing, cutting, mixing
and so on, filmmakers are necessarily making a string of subjective
judgement calls in order to tell the story.

We must then acknowledge that there is no such thing as
objective documentation, and that we are unavoidably making
aesthetic decisions however we choose to approach a film.
Equipment must be selected, microphones placed and edits made,
all of which add their particular flavours. As a result, with
observational documentary, it is often less useful to think about
things in terms of achieving realism, and more appropriate to think
about controlling narrative towards an engaging story that takes the
viewer into the heart of the matter.

Another key concept here is _manipulation_  - controlling or altering
aspects within the narrative to make a point, or to affect the
perception of an audience, often without them realizing. It is very
common for documentary sound to be constructed from layers, not
necessarily captured at the time of production, in the same way that
we tracklay sound in fiction projects. Arguably, every choice we
make is a form of manipulation, but, with each decision, we should
ask ourselves, ‘Are we being authentic to the subject matter? Are we
revealing inner truth?’

Films allow a creative team to uncover the underlying meaning of
things, which at times may be best done through non-literal means.
‘It’s in watching the documentary that you understand the truth of
what you were experiencing,’ filmmaker JF Martell suggests. ‘If it’s a
good documentary, it honours and actually allows you to discover the
experience.’

### **CULTURAL TRADITIONS**


Different cultures, and their associated film industries, have not
always taken the same approach to film sound, forming distinct
aesthetic identities in the process. In the early days of sound film, the
Italian industry took the position that _post-syncing_ - recording all
sound in post-production – would achieve the best result, whereas
French cinema placed much more importance on _direct sound_,
gaining as much usable sonic material as possible from the shoot.
These differing approaches have affected the ways in which
audiences from different cultures view film. For example, early postsyncing efforts were often only vaguely in sync with the image, and
audiences in those countries became accustomed to this effect,
whereas viewers from elsewhere tend to have far less tolerance for
sync issues.

There are other, more ancient cultural traditions on display within
cinema. For example, the deep-rooted Japanese use of negative
space and Hollywood’s more bombastic approach can be compared
by flicking between the original mix and the US dubs of Studio Ghibli
films. The poetic space in the Japanese mix often contrasts sharply
with the Hollywood sound track, where every gap seems to have
been filled in with crowd sounds and extra layers of sfx. Industries in
other countries have also developed distinctive aesthetics, with
India’s Bollywood and Nigeria’s Nollywood both having their own
particular brand of hyperrealism.

Subcultures also bring their own aesthetics to cinema. The feel of
the UK’s reggae sound system scene in the 1970s was authentically
captured in dramas such as _Babylon_, while the 1980s New Wave
scene of New York was documented in the fictional _Liquid Sky_ .
These films attempt to communicate the flavour of being involved in
a particular cultural movement and stand as a testament to a time
and place. Particular perspectives and identities can also be put
across in film. Queer cinema is a key example, whether through
early expressions of camp, the transgressive films of John Waters, or
the disturbing tone of _Stranger by the Lake_ . Use of particular
aesthetics can help define a cinematic movement, in opposition to
mainstream culture.

With these things in mind, during initial discussions on a film, the
sound team should make sure they fully understand the cultural


perspectives that the director and producer are trying to
communicate. Their design plans should be formulated, with
sensitivity, to support that intent.

### **TECHNOLOGICAL AESTHETICS**


As a form of technology in itself, cinema’s aesthetics are also
inherently shaped by the underlying processes used to capture and
reproduce it. There has been a gradual evolution towards higherfidelity sound – from optical and magnetic media, to digital – but this
has arguably brought with it a loss of character. Many modern
designers seek a hybrid approach, combining some of the rounded
character of analogue with the crispness of digital.

Whilst some projects may still be shot on analogue film stock,
production sound recording is now almost exclusively digital. The
challenges of getting good location audio are only amplified by the
operation of tape-based recorders, alongside the issue of additional
costs. However, tape remains a useful tool in post, as there is a
richness and warmth that can be achieved by recording, or
transferring, sounds to analogue media. Additionally, many sound
libraries still in use were recorded before the advent of digital,
bringing a flavour of the analogue domain along with them. There
has also been a big trend within software to emulate the quality of
analogue processes, in an attempt to bring back some of the
character and artifacts of older technology into modern workflows.

There are similar design decisions to be made in the choice of
equipment throughout the recording and mixing chain. Transparent
preamps are commonly used for dialogue and foley recording, but
you may choose instead to use warmer preamps for capturing any
elements to which you wish to bring a certain character. It is difficult
to put into words the difference in sound between these two
categories of amplifiers; if you are able to try them out for yourself,
the differences can be very obvious to the ear.

Mic choice also has a big influence on sound aesthetics, as does
the choice of mix processors and reverb technology ( _see_ Chapter


14). These matters often come down to individual taste but should
also be judged within the context of the film, and the kind of stylistic
result that is being sought.

#### **Futzing**


During the mix phase, the sound team must also choose between
different aesthetic options for _futzing_ - the degradation of recordings
to emulate a particular kind of technology. This could be dialogue
coming over an intercom, the bleed of sound through headphones,
or the distorted signal of a detuned radio.


Tape tends to reproduce high frequencies less well due to the nature of the medium, and
also introduces hiss. However, these are qualities that can sometimes work well as part of a
film’s overall aesthetic.


Futzing can be done for real, by feeding the clean signal through
a playback device that will change the quality in the desired way.
Recording this played-back signal results in a _wet_ layer alongside
the original _dry_ layer, which can be mixed together to achieve the
right balance. When running both signals together, you may need to
correct for phase alignment ( _see_ Chapter 8).


Alternatively, you can use a digital emulation of a futzing effect,
which has the benefit of efficiency and tweakability, but may not
generate as interesting a result as doing it for real.

Similar decisions are required when designing something to
sound ‘bad’. Usually, this means _cinematically_ bad – that is to say,
the audience can enjoy the creatively or aesthetically low quality of
the material being presented. It might be an electronic toy running
out of batteries, or a scene where hokey sound effects underscore a
school play, but the viewer is safe in the understanding that it is
intentionally poor. There is a delicate balance at work in this kind of
process, and it is often best to iteratively degrade something of high
quality, rather than beginning with a low-quality source.

In the simulated reality of _The Matrix_, the sound team
experimented with the idea of building limitations and glitches into
the system, giving clues about the counterfeit nature of the
characters’ world. ‘The footsteps would all sound exactly the same,
sounds looping in the background, or going out of sync,’ designer
Dane Davis explains, ‘but we ran into this dangerous line about what
would be perceived as limitations of the Matrix and what would be
perceived as limitations of the filmmakers – like bad filmmaking.’
These kinds of approaches can be a lot of fun to work with, and very
effective when pulled off, but the sound designer must always be
conscious of where the dangerous lines lie.

#### **Period**


When looking back at films from previous eras, there are often
aesthetic qualities that jump out as being ‘of their time’, reflecting the
fashions and technology. Sometimes, it will be necessary to hark
back to these in retrospect, for effect.

As part of the development of an 1980s period film containing
many _metafilms_ (films within films) supposedly made at the time, I
worked with a team that created a series of short fictional sound
scenarios, using vintage technology, in an attempt to get closer to
the mindset of designers working at the time. This helped us to key
into exactly how things such as VHS and early digital effects units


helped define the sound of the era. It was then possible to build a
library of sounds with reference to this aesthetic world before starting
the sound edit. We had provided ourselves with a palette of paints to
help us achieve a sense of period authenticity.

Using such techniques, sound is often called upon to provide
historical context for period pieces. This can create practical
difficulties for location shoots – any tell-late contemporary sounds
must be judiciously worked around – but the use of older library
recordings, and studio recreations of previous eras, can help sell the
illusion of a particular moment in history.

### **TRIAL AND ERROR**


Each of the approaches discussed here can help to ensure the audio
team are on the same aesthetic page before the practical phase
begins. However, every environment, spot effect and non-diegetic
technique will modulate the overall effect, and ideas may develop
dramatically during the practical work itself.

Creativity is a process of discovery and some trial and error is
inevitable. Just as a sound designer might experiment with different
sounds within a scene, a director must experiment with the scene’s
overall effect. Try to be patient throughout the process of co-creating
the aesthetic of the film and be prepared to collaborate with directors
who cannot articulate exactly what they want. Work together to try
out original ideas; sometimes these will work out beautifully on the
first attempt, but just as often they will serve as a pivot towards other
discoveries.

‘It’s like making a stew,’ suggests Alan Splet. ‘You have a few
potatoes, a few peas, some carrots, a little beef. I put it in the pot
and taste it every so often to see how it’s coming along. Then maybe
you need a little more of this or that, and you blend it all together
until you get your sound. You know when it’s finally right by instinct. It
just begins to feel right.’

This layering process is sometimes best experimented with
before you start cutting things into the timeline. One of my favourite


design techniques is to open a set of sounds in an audio player or
sampler, set to loop mode, and try throwing in layers, removing
others, changing volumes and pitches, in an ever-evolving collage.
When I find results I like, I make notes of settings and copy the
source files to a folder to be laid up against the scene when the time
comes. This free-flowing process opens the door for happy
accidents. As Brian Eno’s _Oblique Strategies_ suggests, ‘Honour thy
mistake as a hidden intention.’

There is one caveat with the layered approach: do not keep piling
sounds on top of each other if things are not quite working. It is an
easy trap to fall into, to think that you can fix a slight sense of
‘wrongness’ with the first sound by adding in a second, then a third,
then a fourth. Every layer should feel like it is getting you significantly
closer to something interesting, otherwise it should be muted. If you
feel yourself becoming frustrated at not finding the right combination,
take a break, or move on to something else. Creativity works better
when you allow your experience to flow. Going for a walk is one way
to turn off the conscious creative process; you may find on your
return that the subconscious has thrown up a lateral idea that you
might never have thought of directly. If that does not work, try turning
off the image and dialogue, and respond purely intuitively to the
moment. ‘We should let a certain amount of freedom and
randomness happen in sound tracks,’ suggests Gary Rhydstrom.
‘Don’t be afraid to have interesting sounds relate to each other and
let natural sounds have a rhythm and pitch that relates even to the
music in the movie.’


No matter which approach you take to developing a film’s aesthetic,
you are ultimately searching for those ideas whose ‘rightness’ calls
out to you. This subconscious metric will be a combination of your
tastes, the film’s style and genre, and the perspectives and identities
that you must tap into to tell a particular story. Ideally, there is a
strong cohesion between these strands – it is very difficult to do your
best work when your own tastes differ too much from those of your
closest collaborators. It is vital always to ensure that you have keyed
into the director’s overall aesthetic plans for a film before committing
to the project.


_[OceanofPDF.com](https://oceanofpdf.com/)_


# **13** **COLLABORATION**
## **_ROLES AND WORKFLOW WITHIN_** **_THE INDUSTRY_**

There’s definitely, definitely, definitely no logic to human behaviour. But yet so, yet so
irresistible.


_(Björk Guðmundsdóttir)_


While we are building narrative worlds for an audience to inhabit, we
are also co-creating the filmmaking environment and shaping the
industry that fuels it. The complexity of the medium requires a large
number of people to come together in collaboration. This demands
careful management, to ensure good communication, efficiency and
crew morale through an often unpredictable and high-intensity
process. Within the sound department, the _supervising sound editor_
( _sound supervisor_ ) is the person responsible for putting together the
team and ensuring everything runs smoothly.


S.W. Fores, _Hieroglyphics_ (ca. 1800). THE WELLCOME COLLECTION

### **SOUND PERSONNEL**


Most of our explorations so far have centred around the creative,
storytelling aspects of film sound. These are usually the primary


concern of the sound designer, but there are many other practical
aspects involved in delivering a film’s sound track. The sound
supervisor is the person who handles the more managerial tasks of
budgeting, scheduling, coordinating reviews with the director and
liaising with other departments. The supervisor role is usually
doubled by the sound designer, although there are a small number of
administrative supervision specialists who work alongside designers,
often taking on multiple projects at once.

On some productions, the role of sound designer may be applied
to someone who creates specific novel sound effects – spaceships,
alien voices and so on. A sci-fi film, for example, may employ
multiple sound designers, working underneath a supervisor. Industry
conventions and individual taste often end up dictating the details of
credits, but ‘sound designer’ as an all-encompassing term, along the
lines of a production designer, usually feels like the best label for the
job.

Working in support of the designer/supervisor are a number of
sub-departments:

#### **Editorial**


**Dialogue supervisor/editor/ADR supervisor:** responsible for all
things dialogue through post-production, these roles are amongst the
most important and sensitive in the entire process. A single person
may handle all the dialogue tracks on their own, or there may be a
separate dialogue editor, who is responsible for the sync sound edit,
and ADR supervisor, who handles the post dialogue recording
process.

**ADR editor:** this role is often handled by the ADR supervisor,
who has been in the recording sessions and can bring an existing
knowledge of the material to the edit. The ADR editor works with the
selected takes from the recording session, fitting them tightly to the
image, smoothing over joins, and making suggestions of alternative
ADR takes to the director where things are not quite sitting.

**Sound effects editor:** if there is an sfx editor involved in a
project, the sound designer will often delegate the majority of sound


effects to them, whilst handling a particular category personally. On a
bigger production there may be multiple sfx editors, either split down
the line of spots and atmos, or in films with a large quantity of a
specific element – vehicles or similar – a specialist sfx editor may be
brought in for that category of spots alone.

**Foley editor:** foley artists are very good at matching on-screen
action, but the captured sounds usually need a bit of attention after
the session. The foley editor refines the recorded timings through the
_foley fitting_ process, ensuring that everything is working well with the
image. Where multiple options have been recorded, the foley editor
makes choices on which layers to use, and sets a rough balance
between them.

There may also be assistant sound editors who carry out session
admin, re-edit previously tracklayed material to new picture versions,
maintain sound libraries, and provide general support through the
editorial process.

#### **Post-Recording**


**Foley artist:** performing along to the image, the foley artist brings
characters and objects to life using sound props. Combining physical
precision and emotional expression, foley artistry often suits those
with a background in dance. It also requires a high degree of
creative problem-solving, working with the available materials to find
the right sound for each visual element.

**Foley mixer:** the mixer selects and places mics, working closely
with the foley artist to achieve the desired result. They also operate
the recording system and organize tracks during the session.

**ADR mixer/recordist:** running studio operations for ADR, the
mixer configures mic set-ups, ensures the performers are
comfortable and carries out temp edits and mixes of ADR takes for
review.

**Sound effects recordist:** a third party may also be
commissioned to make recordings of particular environments, or
capture spot effects that are not practical to perform in the foley
studio.


#### **Re-Recording**

**Re-recording mixer:** shouldering a large amount of responsibility
during the closing stages, the re-recording mixer combines all of the
edited sound layers into a single mixdown. This process begins with
the _premix_, where elements are gelled, balanced and given a sense
of perspective. In the _final mix_, the director then works with the
designer and mixer to refine the premix and sculpt the flow of
intensity, emotion and aesthetic through the timeline.

#### **Recruitment**


The sound supervisor chooses suitable candidates for each role,
using existing contacts, or seeking out new collaborators. ‘I’m quite
open to working with people I haven’t worked with before, as long as
I have “proof” of skill, experience and reputation,’ remarks designer
and supervisor Anna Bertmark ( _God’s Own Country_, _Lilting_ ). ‘It’s vital
that I can trust in the abilities of the professionals I recommend or
employ on a project, so I feel confident I can deliver the sound as
intended. But within those remits, I’m open to recommendations to
new people, and will always do interviews with them before bringing
them on the team, to also make sure they have a positive and team
supporting attitude, which I think is more important than lots of
experience.’

The sound supervisor then guides the overall process to ensure
that the team can deliver a result with which both director and
producer are happy. This is achieved by working to create a sense of
rapport within the team and ensuring everyone feels valued and
supported through the process.

This process usually involves a lot of remote communication, with
the post sound team rarely working within a single building. Sound
editing can be more economical when done from home, freeing up
more of the facility budget for specialist recording and mixing
studios. These facilities usually provide their own personnel, staffed
by studio managers, technicians, assistants and runners.


Within the facility model, the runner is the entry-level position,
responsible for basic practical tasks, whilst learning the ropes and
shadowing more senior colleagues. Once they have gained sufficient
experience, runners can move up to an assistant or technical
position, if an opening becomes available. These roles can then
serve as the stepping stones to becoming a post-recordist or mixer.

### **SOUND SUPERVISION**

#### **Budgeting**


The sound supervisor is involved in running the post sound
department’s budget, sometimes being entirely responsible for
arranging all fees, or at other times passing along names to the _post-_
_production supervisor_ (PPS) to handle negotiations. Budgeting
methods in films can vary hugely – sound post might be allocated
anything between 1.5 and 5 per cent of the overall production
budget, depending on the requirements of the project. The level of
financial resources plays a large part in determining the supervisor’s
crewing approach. A bigger budget will tend to bring a bigger team,
meaning that more ground can be covered at a quicker rate. Where
budgets are lower, a supervisor may negotiate a more flexible
schedule, so that the project can be worked around other full-rate
jobs. The expectations of the producer and director may also need to
be managed to suit the level of the budget. High production values
cannot be assured without the money to back them up, and it is
always better to under-promise and over-deliver.

#### **Scheduling**


The PPS draws up the overall post schedule, liaising with each
department – edit, sound, visual effects and colour grading – to find
the best plan for hitting the film’s delivery deadline. A more detailed
sound-specific schedule is then maintained by the sound supervisor,
determining the tasks to be done each day.


Example section of a feature film schedule, showing the weeks from picture lock to the start
of the premix.


There is always a certain amount of guesswork involved in
figuring out how long things will take, but there are some useful rules
of thumb that can be used when working with experienced
professionals:













Dialogue and sfx editors can usually cover around ten minutes of
material per day, depending on its complexity.
In ADR, somewhere in the region of ten to twelve cues can be
recorded per hour, with the ADR edit taking around twice as long
as it takes to record.
Foley teams can record ten to twenty minutes of material per day,
depending on the complexity of the scenes, and the nature of the
props. The foley edit takes about as long as the recording, or
potentially a little longer, depending on how tight the performance
timings are.
Within the mix phase, a combined dialogue and foley premix
usually takes around ten to twenty minutes of material per day,


with the same true of a joint sfx and music premix. For the final
mix, around one day per ten minutes is ideal, but projects with a
lower budget may need to make do with half of that.


It is best to allow some contingency on top of this where possible,
and time must also be scheduled in for the editors to revise their cuts
based on the director’s notes. This may happen during the editorial
phase itself, or they may attend the mix to provide updates as
needed.

#### **Workflow**


The supervisor works with their team to devise a plan for how each
of the sound post processes should interact. There are a significant
number of interdependencies between the responsibilities of each
role, so careful consideration is always needed. This method of
ordering and configuring each of the separate tasks into a connected
whole is known as ‘defining the workflow’.

At times, the team can work autonomously, using standard
approaches designed to dovetail elegantly. However, during more
sensitive moments the supervisor may need to intervene to make
specific recommendations. Workflow issues can cost a project a lot
of wasted time, so any non-standard plans should be tested in
advance. Starting with a straightforward section of the film can help
iron out any issues.

One crucial aspect of workflow is _tracklaying_, where audio layers
are organized by category and placed on appropriately named
tracks. The post-recordists usually check with the sound editors how
they would like things laid out, with the editors in turn checking in
with the re-recording mixer, who may even supply a template for
them to cut directly into.

Ensuring everyone’s creative work is maintained in a flexible way
throughout the workflow is another key concern for the supervisor.
For example, a sound editor might do temporary mix work to help
make decisions about which sounds to choose – shaping volume,
panning elements within the space, applying reverb and so on. If the


director then gives notes on this sound edit, and falls in love with any
of the temp work, ideally it would be possible to transfer it in a
flexible way that allows the re-recording mixer to pick it up from the
same starting point.

If sound collaborators are using the same editing software, it is
usually simple to transfer tracks between different sessions. They
can either send over a project file with all the associated media, or, if
changes have been made after an initial transfer, simply send the
new project file and any newly added or updated audio files. Any
new media can be identified by sorting the project’s audio folder by
‘Date modified’ and picking up all of the files where the date is more
recent than the time of the first transfer.

Is it usually worth avoiding using different software within a
workflow, as this can create inefficiency, but at times this situation
might crop up. In these cases, tracks can be transferred using an
interchange format such as OMF or AAF. This maintains editing
flexibility, but either throws away or burns in any temp mix work.
Tracks can also be exported as plain audio, with a _timecode offset_ in
the filename that indicates where the file is to be placed. This is the
way that composers normally deliver their musical cues to be added
into the mix.

Whole sound edits can also be _stemmed_ out in this way, and
turned over for mix, but this is usually a bad idea as it removes the
_handles_ from the audio clips. These are the edges of a cut region,
which allow them to be pulled out in time, allowing a mixer to
reshape a scene transition, or find a different moment of atmos from
the same recording.

As a project progresses through the workflow, the supervisor
helps to control the flow of information, materials, facilities and kit
around the team, ensuring everyone has everything they need at
each stage of the process. If the schedule starts to fall behind, the
supervisor may also step in to help, with delegation of tasks to more
junior team members to speed things along.

When multiple sound editors are working within the same subdepartment, work may be split by sections of the timeline, or reel.
However, it is often better to split by category of element, to help


maintain consistency across the film. For example, the style of films I
tend to design call for a large amount of non-diegetic sound effects –
surreal, psychological and so on. Usually I tackle these, whilst my
sound effects editor handles the diegetic sfx. These two categories
are then brought together at some point during the process for
review and tweaks, ahead of the mix process.

#### **Workload**


Whilst keeping a watchful eye over the process, the supervisor
should take care not to micromanage their team. Each member
knows their own process best, and should be left to manage their
own workload unless they come for advice, or if it becomes clear that
they are in need of assistance. Overwork and burn-out can be a real
concern, particularly on long-form projects, and sometimes time off is
needed to recuperate and come back with a fresh perspective and a
sharper mind. Editing and mixing are also iterative tasks, involving
many gradual improvements across multiple passes. The supervisor
has an important role in encouraging everyone involved to trust the
process and know that the film will get there in the end.

When it is appropriate, a creative professional might do well to
consider which of their outstanding tasks best suit their current
mood. We often do our best creative work when we do not feel like
we are working, so keeping a sense of levity in the process can be
important, with a balance of playfulness and pushing on through.
This can be especially challenging during time-pressured situations.
People will respond to stress in different ways, but a good supervisor
will work hard to maintain a positive and productive atmosphere
throughout the process. Of course, collaborations do not always run
smoothly, and choosing your collaborators carefully is important.
When the chemistry is right, connections tend to stick, and longrunning creative relationships are a mainstay in the industry, often
evolving into friendships. Having said that, some of the more fruitful
collaborations in cinematic history have been shot through with a
passion that can spill over into antagonism. The relationship
between director Werner Herzog and actor Klaus Kinski is one


notorious example. A certain amount of creative friction can be what
makes for the positive result – the grit in the oyster that becomes the
pearl.

#### **Creative Reviews**


The first detailed creative meeting with the director is usually the
spotting session ( _see_ Chapter 9). The notes from the spotting
session then help guide the team through until the next review point.
The supervisor usually tries to hold off reviews until the team is
happy to present. It is a little pointless to receive feedback on
elements you already know need significant work, or do not
represent your ideas. You may also create anxiety and mistrust in
the relationship with the director if you present too early. However,
leaving things too late can also create its own problems. It is
important to get sign-off of each stage of the process in good time,
with an _approve and move on_ attitude helping maintain project
momentum.

Sound edit reviews may be done remotely, with the director
feeding notes back. These are often best organized using a cloudbased spreadsheet, allowing columns for different kinds of notes and
enabling each sound editor to add their own comments, and tick
things off the shared list as they go. Cloud services can also be
useful for ensuring projects are automatically backed up, whilst also
allowing others in the team to access shared files, although care
must be taken around security.

Mix reviews are ideally done in person, to ensure that everyone is
listening to the same thing. This is particularly important for theatrical
mixes. A 5.1 mix room with a large subwoofer will sound completely
different from the speakers of a laptop.

### **COMMUNICATING WITH OTHER** **DEPARTMENTS**


As well as managing communication within the sound team, the
supervisor must also liaise with other departments throughout the
process. Working with others who do not always understand what
the sound team does can be challenging, but maintaining a good
bedside manner will help to ensure a positive and creative
atmosphere throughout the process. As editor Ed Hanbury suggests,
‘If I’m working in an environment where I’m happy and can bring the
best out of myself creatively, the rest generally falls into place.’

#### **Director**


The most important line of communication is with the director. There
are various ways to communicate design ideas from script ( _see_
Chapters 5 and 6), but if the sound supervisor/designer is brought on
to a project at a later stage, the first meaningful engagement with the
director may not happen until the spotting session. This process will
then double as a great opportunity to forge creative bonds over the
project, and for first-time collaborators to get to know each other a
little better.

The kind of relationship a supervisor/designer has with the
director very much depends on the personalities involved. One way
to look at the collaboration is this: the designer is there as the expert
in film sound, and the director is the expert in this particular film that
they are making together. They will end up guiding each other, in a
