continual two-way process of question and answer. The director may
have the final say on creative matters, but as the sound designer it is
important to keep hold of your own creative instincts, allowing you to
guide the process towards a result that all can agree on. A director
may come to a review session with certain things in mind, but
hopefully they will always be on the look-out for the ideas that work
best for the film.

Whilst the film is being cut, the director will become used to the
layers of audio added by the editor, watching the film over and over
with those particular sounds attached. It can become slightly difficult
at times to move beyond these temporary sound effects. This is why
it is always a good idea to provide a custom-made sound library to


the editor ahead of the cut, where possible, and to work on key
scenes from the rough-cut stage, to allow time to reflect and
experiment with the less clear-cut elements. However, even with the
early involvement of the sound designer, sometimes elements the
picture editor lays up as placeholders may end up sticking through
the whole process. If an element works well, and has no licensing
issues, there is no need for the sound designer to replace it simply to
satisfy their professional pride.

The director nearly always attends ADR sessions and the final
mix, but their level of involvement during other phases varies. At one
extreme, a director may give all their initial sound thoughts during the
picture edit and spotting session, and then leave it until the final mix
to pick up the process. However, it is more common that there are a
series of review checkpoints along the way.

‘I know there’s a dialogue between the director and the sound
designer. There has to be,’ suggests director David Lynch. ‘But how
much of a dialogue and how much do you go into that with
them...They can be the greatest, but if they don’t tune in to what the
film needs, then it’s going to be a hotch-potch of great stuff that
doesn’t come together. So, it seems to me that the whole thing is to
get people on the same track and just keep going and going so that
everything that comes together is fitting into this world. Al [Splet] and
I had this way of working together. There are no rules. You can just
feel when it’s right.’

There will often be conflicting ideas, and it is difficult to remove
ego from the process, but what matters is not who is right, or who
gets their way, but what is right for the film. If you believe in
something passionately then you should fight for it and give it the
best chance of living within the film. But you also need to know when
it is time to back down. One of a director’s key tasks is to keep things
aesthetically coherent, with everything tested against their own
feelings. It is a common problem that the film the sound designer is
making in their imagination is not the same as the one in the
director’s, or the producer’s. Conflict can often come down simply to
matters of taste. For a rewarding experience, choose your
collaborators carefully. If you do not like any of a director’s previous


films, you are unlikely to be able to bring yourself authentically to
their latest creation.

#### **Picture Editor**


During the earlier stages of post-production, the editor takes the
leading role in controlling the flow of information and emotional
shape through the film; sculpting the sequence, rhythm and cadence
of dialogue, image, sound and music. On a well-organized project,
the sound designer and composer play a supporting role during this
time, providing the editor with elements that help solve story issues
and enhance narrative, through shifts of meaning and tone. This
creative phase of the edit is known as the _offline edit_, as it is usually
done using proxy versions of the image, rather than the original
rushes. Once picture is locked, the rushes are conformed to the
agreed cut in the _online edit_ process.

Speaking about the role of sound during the cut, picture editor
Tom Lindsay ( _Catch Me Daddy_, _Black Mirror_ ) suggests, ‘If a project
is really driven by sound, it’s helpful to involve sound design early in
the process. Same would go for music. Often sound will steer a
project one way or another. I like sound to ultimately lead the picture,
and I often respond to music and find that it can provide a very
distinct framework for a scene.’

The supervisor should also develop a good relationship with the
assistant picture editor, relaying precise specifications for _turnover_ the process of handing over the picture editor’s tracklay – and
receiving updated vfx shots as they become available.

#### **Composer**


The sound designer and composer on a film can be seen as artists
painting within the same frame. Their created layers need to live
together within the same frequency spectrum. The relationship
between Skip Lievsay and Carter Burwell ( _see_ Chapter 4) is one
good example of a sensible approach to this kind of collaboration,


but reality often falls short of the ideal. ‘The music department
typically has no idea what the sound effects department is doing,
and vice versa,’ reflects designer Randy Thom. ‘Producers and
directors should see to it that there is some coordination of the two,
but they virtually never do. Lack of coordination of this kind is rare
during production. But most producers and directors are so
befuddled about sound that the best they can usually manage is to
tell both the music and sound effects departments to throw as much
crap as possible at the canvas, trusting that some sense can be
made of it in the final mix.’

One thing that can help hugely with these less planned workflows
is for the composer to deliver the score as different layers, known as
_stems_, to the final mix. This allows far more flexibility in making the
two blend. For scenes where loud music is playing under dialogue, it
can be particularly useful to have any layers that might interfere with
voice intelligibility on their own stems. This allows for the offending
music layers to be dipped under the dialogue, with others left at a
good volume to help maintain the scene’s energy.

It is also quite common for the composer to work purely in stereo,
meaning their result need to be _upmixed_ to a surround format in the
final mix. Whilst this can be achieved in an automated way using
special software (usually the only option when commercial music is
used and no stems are available), it is better for the re-recording
mixer to be able to work from a set of stemmed layers to spread the
music around the space. Music stems can be fed to the rear
speakers, either using panning or reverb, but are often kept out of
the centre channel, to help keep the score out of the way of the
dialogue, foley and spot effects. To help with this surround mix
process, it is often a good idea to ask the composer to ensure that
percussive and sustained layers are split on to different stems. A
mixer will tend to avoid having percussive sounds coming from
behind the audience, as this can trigger an instinct in people to turn
around to see what the sound was, bringing them out of the film. If
they have access to stems containing only the sustained material,
these will usually sit much better in the surrounds.


#### **Post-Production Supervisor**

The post-production supervisor (PPS) is ultimately responsible for
keeping the overall post process running smoothly. ‘A good PPS is a
good communicator and as a result should have a good network of
contacts and should be able to see relationships and solutions where
others can’t and have several “plan Bs” in case plan A doesn’t work
out,’ observes sound supervisor and designer Anna Bertmark. ‘They
should be a support to the other post department heads and
understand the benefits of having their best interests at heart – like
being aware of the going rates relative to the total budget. A PPS
should start communicating with the sound supervisor early on to
help consult about the budget and schedule, even if it’s just to check
their negotiated plan will work for the way the sound supervisor
wants to approach the film. They should be aware of what it takes to
make a project run well and support the sound supervisor along the
way.’

The PPS also keeps us in the loop about any visual changes in
post that may affect sound timings. Even after a cut is locked, the
visual effects team may add new elements to shots, which call for
associated spot effects, or alter the rhythms of movement.
Additionally, the PPS manages agent liaison, scheduling and on-theday practicalities for actors during the ADR session ( _see_ Chapter 8).

#### **Producer**


Producers often have a limited understanding of sound and may
make requests that are unrealistic. The sound supervisor needs to
try to understand their issues – commercial viability of the end
product, financier politics, potential legal troubles – and help them
understand theirs. It may best for these two parties to see
themselves as co-solvers of the same problem – getting the film
finished to the highest standard possible.

‘As my relationship with the director is creative, my relationship
with the producer is more practical and communication is usually
based around money, time and resources. I like keeping the


producer up to date with the progress of the project but wouldn’t run
to them every time there’s a problem,’ remarks Anna Bertmark.
‘Producers I work with rarely get involved creatively. This is only
really prevalent with inexperienced producers who don’t trust the
director. Most producers understand their role. You often get the best
results when the producer and director collaborate well and respect
and trust each other.’

### **BECOMING A BETTER COLLABORATOR**


A sound designer’s creative reputation is a crucial part of their
professional identity, but it is not enough on its own to ensure a
fruitful career, and people will only tolerate a troublesome
collaborator for so long. Directors in particular may seem difficult to
work with at times, but everyone needs to think about how their own
behaviour feeds into these relationships. If conflict occurs, reflecting
on what it says about your own psychology can be a useful way to
gain perspective, and when things are not working, being honest
with yourself and your colleagues can be the best way to diffuse
tension. Time is also a powerful agent of change, and sometimes
allowing things to settle down can go a long way to remedying a
problem. Designer Tim Prebble ( _Boy_, _30 Days of Night_ ) offers some
useful insight with reference to an African proverb: ‘“Smooth seas do
not make skilful sailors.” Sometimes art makes us suffer, and it is
important to appreciate there is a reason for the suffering.’

It is also important to learn to engage with criticism; there will
always be moments when your most precious ideas are bluntly shot
down, and avoiding a defensive reaction is crucial. Developing a
thicker skin comes with experience, but everyone will benefit from
trying to stay self-aware during moments of creative conflict, and
trying to receive criticism constructively, whilst keeping the overall
needs of the film in mind. ‘There is a phenomenon in movie making
that my friends and I sometimes call the “100 per cent theory”,’
observes Randy Thom. ‘Each department head on a film, unless
otherwise instructed, tends to assume that it is 100 per cent his or


her job to make the movie work. The result is often a logjam of
uncoordinated visual and aural product, each craft competing for
attention, and often adding up to little more than noise unless the
director and editor do their jobs extremely well.’ It is vital always to
work hard, in collaboration with your fellow filmmakers, to determine
exactly what it is that each film needs from you in order to become
the best version of itself.


_[OceanofPDF.com](https://oceanofpdf.com/)_


# **14** **TRANSFORMATION**
## **_OTHERWORLDLY CAPTURE AND_** **_MANIPULATION_**

A different way of hearing and experiencing the sounds of our world.


_(Laurie Spiegel)_


One of the many hats a sound designer must wear is that of the
sonic alchemist, performing mysterious operations to radically
transform a sound from one thing into another. This mode of design
is particularly useful for stories that reach beyond the world of
everyday reality, creating strange new zones for audiences to
inhabit. Transformative audio technologies often yield unpredictable
results, so the designer follows an experimental path of discovery
towards a set of meaningful materials that resonate with the story
and aesthetic of the film.


Trouvelot, E.L., _The Great Comet of 1881_ (1882). CRYSTAL BRIDGES MUSEUM OF AMERICAN ART

### **ALIEN SOUNDSCAPES**


As discussed in Chapter 12, each piece of equipment brings its own
flavour to proceedings. Different mics add their own sonic character,
and different recording media – analogue vs digital – offer a
distinctive feel. This allows for design choices to be made during the
recording process, with certain sound capture technologies instantly
moving the result away from the world of everyday perception.

For example, whilst conventional mics mimic the way the human
ear captures sound waves travelling through the air, _contact mics_
pick up the physical vibrations of objects and surfaces. A contact mic
can be taped on to various items, wedged into a gap, or clamped to
a structure, sympathetically resonating with the oscillations of
whatever it is coupled to. The aesthetic that this technique achieves
is difficult to describe, but it is something like listening in on the
internal world of objects. A similar technique is at work when a doctor
uses a stethoscope on a patient’s chest – the pulsing of the heart
causes the chest to resonate, which is in turn picked up by the
listening device.


For _Censor_, we created a transformative installation from 50 metres of loosely coiled piano
wire, spun into an amorphous shape. A transducer was attached to one end of the wire,
allowing us to send audio through the sculpture, causing it to resonate. Contact mics and
geophones were placed at different points along the wire’s path, recapturing the sounds at
different stages of the transformative journey. The results were then used as atmospheres
and textures to evoke the surreality of dream and memory.


There is an example of surface technology at work in _Possessor_
(2020), in which designer Martin Pavey used loudspeakers known as
_transducers_ to translate dialogue recordings into vibrations that
cause objects to resonate. Contact mics coupled to the same
surfaces then picked up the results, with the transmitted speech
taking on the characteristics of the resonant medium, lending a
strange internal quality to the voice to match the narrative scenario.

Contact mics are a relatively cheap form of technology compared
with high-end studio microphones. It is even possible to make them
from basic electrical components without too much difficulty. They
provide a means to explore the world in a completely different way,
seeking out interesting sources of vibrations – industrial facilities are
particularly rich in possibilities – and translating these resonances
into otherworldly sound recordings. This technique tends to give


fairly organic-sounding results, inhabiting a space that does not feel
overly synthetic, yet is still quite different from the everyday
experience.

For a more synthetic effect, electromagnetic frequency (EMF)
pick-ups capture electromagnetic fields as sound. Electrical devices,
such as computers or printers, can produce EMF radiation with
interesting oscillation patterns that are often quite unexpected. This
offers another a playful way to explore a world that lies hidden within
our own.

A third capture-based method of creating sounds beyond ordinary
perception is to use mics designed to capture _ultrasonics_, which are
frequencies above the threshold of human hearing. In tandem with
_high sample rate_ recording systems, it is then possible to pitch down
the results to bring a world of unheard tones into the audible range.
This is particularly useful for generating interesting material from
everyday sound props and creating otherworldly atmospheres.

### **CHANGING THE RECORD**


Alongside unusual capture devices, many processing technologies
exist to manipulate sound recordings, giving the sound designer a
near-infinite range of possibilities to create novel sounds. The results
of these processes can serve as new stand-alone layers, or they
may be blended back in with the original source material for a more
subtle effect.

#### **Reverb and Echo**


Reverb is a naturally occurring phenomenon ( _see_ Chapter 11), which
can now be simulated digitally to a fairly accurate degree. However,
earlier analogue attempts to recreate the effects are still widely used,
particularly in music studios, and their particular aesthetics are
sometimes exactly what is needed for a particular story context.


Early innovations include _plate reverb_ units, comprising a large
metal sheet encased in a wooden box, which uses transducers and
pick-ups to resonate the metal surface and capture the results. The
metal plates were designed to produce a lush, smooth result when
driven by sound. Whilst they are not particularly realistic, they can
still be very useful to spatialize sound in a dreamy, warm and
analogue way.

A _spring reverb_ is a much smaller device that sends sound
through a tightly coiled spring and picks up the results. The sound is
less lush than that produced by a plate reverb, but it can introduce
some strange _harmonics_ (frequencies other than the fundamental
tone of a sound), which are useful for transforming the feeling of a
source. Odd impact sounds can also be generated by striking the
unit physically; such sounds were used to great effect to represent
weapons in early sci-fi films.

Music studios have also traditionally housed an _echo chamber_  a room designed to facilitate worldizing processes ( _see_ Chapter 4).
This technique produces very realistic reverb, of course, with sounds
being sent into an actual space. The resulting acoustic phenomena
are then captured by conventional mics. However, it is still possible
to work in transformative processes to achieve more interesting
results. One method is to pitch up the sound material, worldize it,
and then bring the resulting pitches back down to match the original.
This has the net effect of leaving the source material sounding
similar, but slowing down just the reverb, creating the feeling of a
space that is much larger and darker than the real room. The inverse
process of pitching down the source, worldizing, and then pitching up
the results by the same amount leads to the sense of a tighter,
brighter space.

Echo effects can also be found out in the real world; projecting
sounds into the side of a mountain, for example, and recording the
bounce-back can give a rich and convincing result. However, most
electrical echo processors offer transformations that would be
impossible to recreate in everyday reality.

_Tape echo_ works by capturing a source sound on magnetic tape
using a _record head_ and then playing back the captured sound using


a _play head._ These devices use small loops of tape that go round
and round the tape heads, recording whatever is coming in and
playing back whatever has been captured soon after. The physical
distance between the two heads determines the length of the delay.
The recorded sound has to travel along the running spool of tape
from the record head to the play head to be heard again. On some
set-ups, this can be manually adjusted, whilst other units offer a set
delay length, or a small number of options.

One of the most obvious ways the resulting sounds can differ
from reality is through the phenomenon of _feedback_ . The output of
the play head can be mixed back in with the source sound to be
picked up again by the record head, which can create an
exponentially growing signal. The sound is repeated, and then each
repeat is also repeated; the runaway effects of this recursive process
can sound very unusual indeed. One example can be found in
_Berberian Sound Studio_ (2012), a film about a British sound
engineer who travels to Italy to work on a ‘giallo’ horror project. The
film is something of a love letter to 1970s sound equipment, and in
one scene the protagonist can be heard transforming a voice using
fed-back tape echo.

After tape echo came the development of analogue _delay units_,
which instead used electronic circuitry to delay the incoming signal.
Digital equivalents followed before long, and today there are many
options to choose from, which range from very warm and analogue
in character to very clean, crisp and digital.

#### **Modulation**


_Modulation_ refers to processes that change the properties of sound
within the time domain. These techniques have their origins in music
studio experiments, where engineers discovered tape effects
through play, or by accident, and also in equipment developed for
early avant-garde electronic composers. There is now a huge array
of options to choose from, with units and plugins often working as
multi-purpose devices, allowing different effects to be chained
together.


The following list details the most commonly found modulation
effects:

















**Flanging:** a technique involving a sound feeding back on itself
with a very short delay, giving a swooshing, psychedelic result.
Originally achieved by lightly pressing on a tape reel’s outer edge
(flange) to create a slight slowdown, whilst a second tape
machine played at the original speed. One of the earliest
examples can be heard in _Itchycoo Park_ by The Small Faces,
during a drum fill that bridges between two sections of the song.
The effect on the drums is somewhat reminiscent of a passing jet
plane, or sound heard through a drainpipe, yet somehow not
quite of this world. This heady feel leant itself particularly well to
the emerging genre of psychedelic rock, and by the mid 1970s,
hardware _flangers_ were available that could reproduce the effect
electronically.
**Phaser:** similar to the flanger, but instead of a delay it uses a
phase-shifting technique, which creates a different kind of
swirling effect. It became particularly popular with guitar players.
**Chorus:** this adds a slightly detuned copy to the original source,
creating a sense of multiple voices. The effect has its roots in a
system developed by Abbey Road Studios, at the request of John
Lennon, to find a way to automate the process of double-tracked
vocals without actually having to sing a melody twice.
**Tremolo:** a form of volume processor that rhythmically changes
the level of an input to a set pattern and speed.
**Harmonizer:** a digital device invented by US company Eventide,
which is able to change pitch without altering time, and can
create in-sync copies of a sound at multiple pitches.
**Pitch shift and time stretch:** these are modern algorithmic
incarnations of the idea at the heart of the harmonizer, in which
pitch and time can be controlled separately, as opposed to the
classic effect of slowing down or speeding up a tape or record,
which alters both pitch and time proportionately.



While these effects tended to be innovations of the music industry, it
was not long before they began to crop up in films. In particular, the


Eventide range of modulators became invaluable for sci-fi films.
Speaking of his work on _Dune_, Alan Splet describes how he used the
technology: ‘The sound of the shields is made up of several different
elements that I put together on the eight track to make a single
effect. On track one, there was a shrill hum. It’s a sound I’m not sure
of what the original base was, but I put it through the harmonizer,
which changes the pitch without changing the time base. I raised the
pitch slightly. On track two is a heavy power leakage, an arcing and
sparking sound. Then on another track is what I call a “shield
proximity sound”, a low roaring that came from an ultra-sound
machine, a cleaning mechanism, and I put that through the
harmonizer. Then there’s a rapid buzzing, an industrial sound that I
put through the flanger, which alters its harmonic structure.’

#### **Reversing**


As tape recording technology developed, it also became very easy
for sounds to be played backwards, creating strange time-mirrored
effects that were soon put to use in avant-garde classical
movements such as _Musique Concrète_, which counted film sound
theorist Michel Chion as one of its primary composers.

When reversing sounds, you may want to avoid anything with
very hard attacks, as these can stick out as very obviously reversed
when heard by modern ears. If a source contains hard transient
sounds, you could first try to tame those with a _transient shaper_ or
_de-clicker,_ and then reverse the result for a smoother effect.

The idea of time in reverse is also quite common as a cinematic
visual device. When adapting _I Woke Up Before I Went To Sleep_, a
poem by Pip Ridley, which explores an experience of the arrow of
time in reverse, I needed to devise both visual and sonic
representations of the idea of things running backwards. Sometimes
this would be a case of reversing photographed time lapses and
captured sound recordings. In other moments, running sounds
through reverb and modulation was enough to take the moment
beyond an everyday feel.


Reversing can also be used in tandem with reverb, to create an
effect that was made famous by the ghostly voices of _Poltergeist_ . For
_reverse reverb_, the source is played backwards through a reverb
device, and then both source and reverberated result are rereversed. This leads to an effect in which the reverb comes in before
the source material, in a bizarre pre-echo that breaks the laws of
everyday acoustics.

#### **EQ and Dynamics**


Whilst EQ and dynamics processors are particularly useful tools for
balancing a mix ( _see_ Chapter 15), they can also be used to
creatively transform an input signal. For example, whilst underwater
recordings can be made using a _hydrophone_ - a waterproof contact
mic – it often sounds better, and is more practical, to use
conventional recordings as the source, but radically change the
sound with EQ. By removing most of the high frequencies, a suitably
submerged effect can be achieved, mirroring the effects of water as
a sonic carrier medium. Similarly, in other scenarios certain parts of
the spectrum might be pulled out to create a particular effect – for
example, an explosion with all of the bass removed takes on a
completely different quality compared with the original sound.


Paul O’Brien’s _Solution of Continuity_ . As a transformative tool, tape can be used to bring a
layer of ‘sound dust’, especially when the levels are pushed so hard that the tape is
overloaded, or saturated, with signal.


EQ changes tone, but dynamics processors alter the volume of
an incoming sound, usually without affecting the overall tonal
balance. Conventionally used to tame wild input signals, these units
can also be put into service to do the opposite, creating unusual
patterns within a sound. _Sidechain compression_ is one example,
where one sound’s volume shape can be modulated by that of
another, opening up interesting transformative possibilities.

#### **Distortion**


Distortion is usually used to denote some kind of overloading of a
particular sound process, which then creates artifacts that may or
may not be desirable, depending on the context. Production mixers
avoid distortion at all costs – digital clipping nearly always sounds
horrendous – but certain kinds of analogue-style distortion can
transform a sound in interesting ways, beefing it up and providing a
more aggressive effect. If an audio signal is pushed too hard through


an analogue mixing desk it will _overdrive_, which can create some
interesting harmonics. A related effect involves _saturating_ a recording
media with signal. This is commonly used with magnetic tape, giving
it a thicker and fuller sound.


Although most of these transformative processes have their roots in
the analogue domain, they are all also available as software plugins,
with digital technology often looking to emulate the original effects as
closely as possible. Digital effects provide a great degree of flexibility
throughout the post-production process, but sometimes there is no
replacement for the sonic richness and complexity of analogue.
Modern workflows are able to look to a hybrid approach of the two,
taking the best of each to suit a film’s aesthetic. As digital technology
is still evolving very rapidly, it can be easy to get lost in the available
options. One way to navigate a way through is to seek out interviews
with contemporary sound designers and mixers, who often speak
about their preferred tools.

Whilst this transformative aspect of sound design is fun to work
with, and is capable of creating an endless stream of novel effects, it
is important not to put too much emphasis on processing within the
context of the sound designer’s role. According to Skip Lievsay
( _Gravity_, _No Country for Old Men_ ), ‘The key to being really good is
the ability to do abstract thinking. Often the best sound design
comes not from something you created; it comes from knowing the
right sound to use at all times. Some of the best sound designers I
have known had no idea how to use a synthesizer or manipulate a
sampler...but they did have an infallible sense of what was right for a
scene.’


_[OceanofPDF.com](https://oceanofpdf.com/)_


# **15** **FINALE**
## **_MIXING AND DELIVERING A FILM_**

A work is never completed...but merely abandoned.


_(Paul Valéry)_


This book so far has covered a broad and varied set of approaches
for thinking about and creating film sound. On any given project, all
the individuals in the team will bring a similarly varied set of
perspectives, owing to their differing experiences and tastes, and
depending on the responsibility of their role. It is the job of the rerecording mixer to combine all of these creative viewpoints, and the
sonic layers they have supplied, into a coherent whole, also known
as the _final mix_ .


Bosch, H., _Ascent of the Blessed_ (ca. 1505–15). PALAZZO GRIMANI DI SANTA MARIA FORMOSA


This diversity of perspectives will inevitably cause clashes at
times – both in terms of sound and of personality – and a good mixer
is also an exceptional mediator. They will have to navigate tricky


creative, political and technical territory in order to reach a
destination that best serves the film and its story. The mix is usually
the single most expensive activity in the sound post process. Ideally,
it happens in a large mix theatre, modelled after a cinema, with many
of the key stakeholders in attendance, so time becomes a very
precious commodity. The team must try to make their final decisions
collaboratively and efficiently, under intense time pressure, with the
mixer and sound supervisor doing their utmost to keep things on
track.

The technicalities of mixing are well beyond the scope of this
book – a weighty tome could easily be dedicated to that subject
alone. Film sound professionals do not usually become re-recording
mixers in their own right until many years into their career and it is
very much a case of learning by watching, listening and doing. The
focus here is instead to give a broad-strokes account of my own
experiences of the process, whilst giving a few pointers to help get
you started if you would like to give it a go or are unable to hire a
mixer for your own project. This do-it-yourself path may initially be
largely overshadowed by clouds of frustration, but a slow and steady
stream of discovery runs by it – walk at a gentle pace and be sure to
keep your ears wide open.

### **WHAT MAKES A GOOD MIX?**

#### **Quality Ingredients**


The merits of the finished sound mix depend significantly on the
quality of the source material. The best mixer in the world will
struggle to make something decent from a collection of bad
recordings and poorly constructed sound edits. Every sound team
member’s work plays a part in getting the project ready for the mix,
and a good crew works hard to give the mixer the best possible
starting point.

‘We’ll fix it in the mix’ is a dreaded phrase, generally heard on set
(and often by those unqualified to know the full implications), in the


interests of moving forwards with the shooting schedule. ADR ( _see_
Chapter 8) is one way to work around issues with production audio,
but at other times the re-recording mixer may be left to resolve the
problems as best they can. Whilst mix technology has come a long
way over the years, some things simply cannot be made to sound
polished, and a good sound supervisor will work hard to ensure the
mixer is not asked to perform miracles.

Of course, some issues remain entirely out of the control of the
sound department. The word ‘turkey’ is affectionately used by some
to describe a film whose overarching quality is so low that it has no
chance of taking flight in the unforgiving company of audiences and
critics. A ‘turkey in the room’ is usually perceived by everyone
present at the mix, but in case the director and producer have
tethered their hopelessly soaring dreams to the poor flightless
creature, the mixer must remain diplomatic throughout. In such
cases, propelling the film to the finish line with the minimum amount
of fuss is the name of the game – good-humouredly known as
‘booting it out of the door’. Sadly, the gravitational forces of critics
and box-office numbers will probably bring the whole thing crashing
down to earth at a later date.

Luckily, pure-bred turkeys are a relatively rare species once you
get to a decent level of the industry, so you will usually have the
sense that your work has contributed something meaningful, be it
through the creation of poignant experiences or simply those of pure
entertainment. That said, if you spot a glaring turkey sufficiently well
in advance, attempt a graceful sidestep – the project is unlikely to do
you any favours.

#### **A Skilled Chef**


In the wrong hands, even the finest of ingredients can result in a
disappointing dish. A successful drama tends to grab the audience
instantly, carry them through and leave them wanting more, and
much of the mixer’s job is about controlling the dramatic shape of the
sound in search of this effect. This may largely be a case of refining
the narrative arcs developed through the offline edit and sound

design process, but at times the mixer’s fresh perspective may also
introduce radically different ideas, opening up new avenues of
emotion and impact.

Some designers prefer to mix their own films. This allows them to
maintain more creative control, but will also require them to achieve
the sometimes tricky manoeuvre of regaining the head space of a
first-time viewer or listener after a long and winding post process. A
third party not only brings fresh ears, but also helps the designer and
director to be more brutal about cutting out anything that is not quite
working. As re-recording mixer Leslie Gaston-Bird points out, ‘Mixers
often spend lots of money on special tools, so you’re getting access
to those when you team up.’


Listening has many subjective elements, making a mixer’s job remarkably complex. Being
able to trust their own ears is crucial to their development in the role. As mixer Richard
Portman ( _The Godfather_, _The Deer Hunter_ ) explains, ‘You literally train your ear to shut out
everything else but the sound you’re listening to. We call it “putting your ear on it”.
Somebody will say, “There’s a tone over there,” and I’ll say, “I’ll get to it in a minute, I have
my ear on this.”’


At the more basic end of the kit spectrum are perhaps the most
important tools of all: volume and mute. Deciding on relative levels
between layers, and choosing which sounds to subtract (always
muted, rather than deleted, to keep options open), is a big part of
lending a sense of clarity to a mix, and focusing the attention of the
audience on the most important, or emotive, elements in any given
moment. As an audience does not listen actively, meaning they are
not focusing on the sound itself but instead taking in the total
experience, it is helpful to try to maintain a broad perspective as the
default position. You should zoom in to focus on audible details only
where you feel the overall effect needs to be pushed this way or that.
It is then possible properly to judge any changes you have made
during the next time the film is played back, or heard _in a run_ . This
can be subjective territory – every person listening in the mix may
have different feelings about what is and is not working – so it is
often a good idea to focus on one sonic element at a time. The
dialogue supervisor’s notes might be handled on one day, and the
composer’s on another. As a general approach, keeping the
director’s perspective at the forefront of the process is critical, to
avoid too many cooks spoiling the broth.

‘A sound mix is never your mix, it is the client’s,’ suggests Emma
Butt ( _Game of Thrones_, _White Riot_ ). ‘What I mean is that if you are
working on a project you need to remember that your client [the
director] has most likely been working on this for more than a year.
It’s their baby. The sound mix should always be the fun part where
everything they have worked hard to achieve finally starts to come
together, and they see their vision come to life. If they decide that
they don’t like something you’ve done, you cannot take it personally.
I’ve seen so many engineers fail at this and tell clients they are
wrong or argue with clients over how something should sound. By all
means, you should always give your opinion but at the end of the
day this is the client’s project and you need to respect their choices.’

#### **A Clean Kitchen**


Just as every listener in the mix room will inevitably have a different
sonic experience due to differing expectations, inclinations and
states of mind, every mix room is also unique. The advent of digital
sound may have enabled exact copies of sound material to be made,
but each time a digital audio file is played back into the world, the
complexities of reality come into play. Different models of speakers
will reproduce the same source material differently to some degree
or other, and the ramifications of room acoustics ( _see_ Chapter 11)
have a big impact on the sound that reaches our ears, effects that
are also modulated by our physical listening position within the
space.

To tackle this problem, mix rooms are designed to be as clean
and as faithful to the source material as possible – a quality that is
known as _transparency_ . The room and playback system will
inevitably add some colouration, but acoustic engineers work hard to
keep this to a degree that will not hinder judgement calls. Good
cinema systems are also designed with this in mind and, whilst there
is a certain degree of standardization, the film will inevitably sound
slightly different in each venue where it plays. A mixer can work to
become familiar with how a particular mix room sounds, and develop
instincts around balancing different aspects to give the possible
result for the film’s release journey. However, ultimately they can
have no control over the quality of the playback systems of all the
cinemas, film festival venues and domestic spaces where the film
will live, and have to accept that often the film will not sound exactly
as they intended.

### **PREMIXING**


Cinema-sized mix rooms (known as _dubbing theatres_ or _mix_
_theatres_ ) are expensive facilities to hire, so much of the initial mix
work is usually done in a smaller, more affordable space. This stage
takes care of the more fiddly, technical processes, allowing most of
the final mix to be spent on creative matters. The re-recording mixer
usually premixes the dialogues and ADR but may bring in a


colleague to handle the foley, sfx and music. The dialogue should be
tackled first, and this can then be used as a reference for the other
premixes. There is no gold standard for where dialogue should sit in
a mix, but there are a number of helpful guidelines when starting out.
Meter reads of around −12db for an average level of talking is a
good rule of thumb, with shouting perhaps jumping up to 6-db or
higher, and whispering being far quieter. Once a mixer has enough
experience, they will not usually watch the level read-outs. A studio
will be calibrated to play sound back at a certain level, and this
allows the mixer to learn how they like things to sound in a particular
room through feeling alone. ‘I think that in mixing, sound must
always progress,’ suggests mixer Owe Svensson ( _The Sacrifice_, _A_
_Pigeon Sat on a Branch Reflecting on Existence_ ). ‘Levels should
never be pre-determined. A voice, if it’s talking one way, should be
heard one particular way. If it’s louder, then you hear it louder, and so
on. In my way of working, the sound must always live. Sound should
keep changing.’

In addition to controlling levels – usually using a combination of
volume automation and dynamics processors such as _compressors_
and _expanders_ - the mixer uses EQ to sculpt the tonal balance of
sounds. For example, a common mix issue is that both composer
and sound designer may have used a large amount of bass in a
scene. The individual layers may sound fine on their own but there
are problems when they are played back together. To resolve this,
EQ can be used to remove bass from one of the elements. EQ can
also be used along with other effects to create futzes, simulating the
effect of audio emerging from the speakers of phones, TVs, radios
and the like. Panning ( _see_ Chapter 3) is another useful technique, to
place each element within the space.

Another big mix consideration is reverb ( _see_ Chapters 11 and
14). The diegetic sound sources that make up the sound edit will
probably have been recorded in a large variety of different places,
and it is usually necessary to make them feel like they are part of a
coherent world. This can be done by adding the same kind of reverb
to a collection of separately recorded elements. Perspective changes

- which affect how near or far away things feel – can also be
achieved by controlling the amount of reverb added, in combination


with EQ to model the real-world effects of how sound waves change
as they travel.

Non-diegetic reverbs can also be used to diminish a sense of
reality, or increase the feeling of subjectivity, in a moment. A very
effective example of non-diegetic reverb for storytelling can be found
midway through _Room_, where designer/mixer Steve Fanagan
applies shifting, dream-like reverbs on the dialogue to bring the
audience into the disorientation of a protagonist encountering the
outside world for the first time.

### **THE FINAL MIX**


Once the project moves into the theatre for the final mix, the cinemalike environment often brings a new sense of enthusiasm for all
involved, allowing them to re-encounter the film in a way that is much
closer to the first-time experience of the eventual audience. The
graded picture is often available to the sound team for the first time
during this phase, freshening up everyone’s visual experience in the
closing stages.


The mix theatre at Point1Post.


Beyond technical and political considerations, the mixer’s main
creative concern is overall dynamic shape, with loud and soft used
as light and shade. However, there are many other key narrative
elements to be considered in the theatre. One is audience immersion

- how much width and depth is used to ‘bathe’ the audience in
sound. Another is the low-frequency effects (LFE) track. This is run
to a large subwoofer in the theatre, which is capable of producing
extremely loud sub-bass _._ Alongside a more powerful sound system,
a larger room also has a big impact on how these phenomena are
perceived, and there is a crucial difference between the design of the
playback systems used in premix rooms and theatres. A premix room
usually has one speaker for each of the chosen mix format’s
channels. This is called a _point source_ set-up; for example, a 5.1
premix room would have five speakers to handle the front and rear
channels, and a single subwoofer for the LFE. Cinemas and
theatres, on the other hand, usually use an _arrayed_ system, where
the format’s channels are spread over a larger number of speakers
to fill the space better. For a more detailed discussion of immersion,
including more modern formats such as Dolby Atmos, _see_ Chapter 3.


Whilst the team will try to ensure that the pre-mix translates as
successfully as possible to the theatre, the fundamental design
differences mean that the film will inevitably sound quite different in
the two spaces. This jump can be slightly disconcerting for those
who are not used to it, but it can also encourage all concerned to
approach the film with fresh ears, injecting more of the perspective of
the first-time viewer/listener back into proceedings. The final mix
may also be the first time a director hears all the elements of the
sound track together. This can be quite an intense experience for
them, and part of the mixer’s role is to help them to navigate through
the available options.

### **DELIVERY**


Reflecting on studio mixing in general, Richard Formby points out
that ‘owing to the sheer number of possible processing applications
available, the decision has to be made when to stop’. In films, this is
usually when the mix budget runs out – going over schedule can
become very expensive, and another film may be waiting at the mix
room’s door. Representatives of the film’s financiers usually attend
the later stages and if they and the producer decide that the mix
schedule needs to be extended, then they may be willing to allocate
additional funds.

Once the time has finally expired, the mixer runs off the exports
for the main theatrical mix. This entails the master mix, plus the _mix_
_stems_ (also known as _mix splits_ ), which are usually in the form of a
_DME_ (dialogue, music and effects). This means that a separate
export is made for each of the dialogue, music and sound effects.
These can be helpful for making trailers, or for any potential changes
to the main film down the line, without the need for access to the
original mix set-up.

Alongside the theatrical mix, there is usually a requirement for a
broadcast mix for TV and streaming, and an _M&E_ (music and
effects) mix for _internationalization_, otherwise known as _foreign_
_dubs_ . For an M&E, no discernible language should be heard on the


effects stem, so tracklays are often designed to allow easy muting of
any elements that do not belong in the M&E.

Once all of the deliverables have been handed over, the sound
department’s work is done. How much of a life the film has out in the
real world is dependent on a great many factors that are outside the
control of the sound team, but everyone keeps their fingers crossed
and hopes for a good reception. And whilst the process of getting it
out there may have been long and complex, hopefully it will have
proved to be a worthwhile endeavour, and you might even have
learned a thing or two along the way.

# **APPENDIX I** **ONE LAST DANCE SCRIPT**

```
               ONE LAST DANCE

                Written by

                Glen Laker

EXT. HERNE BAY, RUINED PIER - DAY

We OPEN on the derelict pier, part-destroyed by storms and the
sea. A TIME-LAPSE shot as dawn breaks, the sun rising behind
the pier, the turning sea sinking beneath it.

EXT. HERNE BAY, STEPS TO BEACH - DAY

ON JOHN, a gaunt man in his 60s, as he descends the wooden
steps onto the single beach. On his way somewhere...

EXT. HERNE BAY, BEACH - DAY

JOHN makes his way along the beach, past the groynes poking up
through the pebble beach like weathered bones. Eyes fixed
ahead. Preoccupied. Deep in thought.

EXT. HERNE BAY, NEW PIER - DAY

JOHN passes between the pillars, beneath the struts of the
existing pier.

EXT. HERNE BAY, LOW TIDE - DAY

```

```
Along the beach, JOHN passes men working the exposed seabed,
using forks to dig up lugworms.
EXT. HERNE BAY, BEACH HUTS - DAY

JOHN passes the stretch of colourful beach huts. Ahead, two
teenage lovers are locked in an embrace before one of the huts.
JOHN's eyes flick to them as he passes.

EXT. HERNE BAY, PATH FROM BEACH - DAY

JOHN heads away from the beach, moving up the steps, inland.

EXT. HERNE BAY, BENCH - DAY

Further along the hill path, John comes to a bench. He sits and
now we see him for the first time. A far-away look in his eyes.
Looking out at the coast. Spinning his wedding ring on his
finger.

And over his POV of this seaside town, we...

ROLL TITLES

IN ON:

INT. KEITH'S VAN - DAY

KEITH, 44-year-old, drives along the coastal road. Beside him
is his 14-year-old son, RYAN. On the radio plays an up-tempo
number (RYAN's ballroom dancing tune).

A beat, then KEITH reaches over and turns it off.

                  RYAN
          Dad! I was listening to that.

                  KEITH
         Keep an eye out for your granddad.

INT. CAFE - DAY

In the window to a cafe, John sips his tea, looking out to sea,
wearing a sad, crooked grin.

INT. KEITH'S VAN - DAY

```

```
Ahead, the coastal road. KEITH and RYAN looking at the
arcades...

                  KEITH
             By the pier, he said.

RYAN spies JOHN in the window to a cafe and points.

                  RYAN
               There he is.

EXT. CAFE - DAY

The van pulls up. KEITH sounds the horn. BEEP! JOHN gathers his
belongings and heads over.

INT. KEITH'S VAN - DAY

KEITH watches his father approaching. Eyes flick to his watch.

                  KEITH
                 (sotto)
             In your own time...

RYAN opens the door to let JOHN in.

                  RYAN
               Hi, Granddad.

                  KEITH
               By the pier?

                  JOHN
                 (shrugs)
           Whole front's by the pier.

A weary smile from KEITH. This is going to be a long night.
JOHN sits. KEITH pulls the van away from the kerb.

EXT. SEAFRONT - DAY

A MONTAGE of a rundown seaside town, its glory long since faded
into a palette of blues and greys. A ghost town looking out to
sea.

                JOHN (V.O.)
      This whole stretch used to be ablaze. Alive.

```
INT. CAR - SAME

JOHN looks out the passenger-side window, the reflections of

  I'd bring your mum to the old
pavilion here, when it was still on

the pier, for tango lessons –

every Tuesday, we came...

                  KEITH
          “I remember when all this was
          fields – before the council put
            the bloody sea here...”

```
RYAN laughs.

JOHN doesn't react. Just watches the dead shops roll by.

EXT. KINGS HALL CAR PARK - EVENING

```

```
CUT TO:

```

```
KEITH pulls into an empty space before the grand Kings Hall
building. They all climb out. JOHN groans as he straightens his
stiff back.

RYAN walks on ahead.

                  RYAN
            I'd better get changed.

                  KEITH
            We'll see you in there.

RYAN runs toward the entrance, greeting some friends on his

 This... thing, this dance thing. Do we have to stay for the

```

```
                  KEITH
    Yes. Him and the girl have worked their socks off.

                  JOHN
     Only the Blues are playing Whitstable tonight.

                  KEITH
 That's what this is about? You're missing a football match?

                  JOHN
    Maybe I can sneak off later. Catch the second half.

KEITH stares, shakes his head, walks off, through the
chattering kids, to the entrance of the Kings Hall.

ON JOHN, hesitating, watching KEITH. He takes in the building,
the crowds. Something stopping him, rooting him to the spot.

EXT. KINGS HALL, ENTRANCE - DAY

Moments later, KEITH turns to look for his dad. But JOHN has
gone.

KEITH looks disappointed. A touch of anger. He continues on his
way.

EXT. KINGS HALL, STEPS - DAY

JOHN is taking a breather, halfway up the steps leading to
Kings Hall. The kids jog up the stairs beside him. For a beat,
JOHN looks out to sea. Sadness in there.

Then something clinks in his gaze.

He realises he can't stay here all night. He lifts his chin,
then shuffles on, to the hall.

                                  CUT TO:

INT. KINGS HALL - DAY

Following JOHN as he passes into the main hall. An under-16s
dance competition is under way. The hall has been decked out
cheaply in drapes, a few disco lights and a glitter-ball
hanging over the dance floor.

```

```
JOHN finds KEITH and RYAN. JOHN claps his hands.

                  JOHN
           Right then! Where's the bar?

He looks around. Panics.

               JOHN (CONT'D)
            There's no bloody bar.

                  KEITH
         Of course there's no bloody bar.

                  JOHN
           Why is there no bloody bar?

                  RYAN
      It's an under-sixteens' dance competition.

DEBRA, a pretty teenage girl with too much make-up on, sees
Ryan and rushes over.

                  DEBRA
     Ry! For a moment, I thought you'd baled on me.

Her annoyance at his tardiness quickly dissipates. She hugs
RYAN. Smiles at KEITH and JOHN.

```

```
DEBRA (CONT'D)

```

```
  (to Keith)
Hello, Mr Niccol!

```

```
(to John)

```

```
    Hello...
(off John's silence)..

```

```
Ryan's granddad!

```

```
JOHN nods, puffs out his cheeks. DEBRA holds his gaze for a
beat. She smiles at RYAN.

               DEBRA (CONT'D)
   You need to get changed. We're on in twenty minutes.

                  RYAN
                 OK, OK.

After they've left, JOHN looks around.

```

```
                  JOHN
         Used to be able to smoke in here.

                  KEITH
         Find a seat. I'll grab us a coffee.

KEITH moves off.

Alone, JOHN blows out his cheeks. He cranes his neck to take in
the hall. He emits a very loud yawn.

He wanders along the edge of the dance floor.

Finds a chair and sits. Takes a handkerchief from his pocket,
dabs sweat off his forehead.

He takes a deep breath. Studies his own skin covering his hands
as if he's never seen it before. The liver spots. The wrinkles.

He breathes out. Lifts his eyes, heavy like great weights.

He takes in the room. The high ceiling. The glitter-ball. The
dancers as they practise their steps.

JOHN closes his eyes, runs a hand over his face. Tired.

He blinks open his eyes. And stops. Frowning at the dance
floor.

JOHN'S POV

The dance floor is empty. The hall as quiet as a ghost ship.

And there on the dance floor, is a WOMAN - SARAH - in her late
60s, wearing a bright red floral dress. Watching us - JOHN with keen, bright eyes.

ON JOHN

Something new floods into JOHN's face. A jolt of fear followed
a split-second later by deep, deep childlike wonder.

Returning her gaze, he smiles.

```

```
SARAH smiles back. And with a quick stamp of her foot, she
strikes a pose and holds out her hand.

JOHN rises to his feet. He walks onto the dance floor, up to
Sarah...

JOHN takes SARAH's hand. Feeling her skin. Decades of memories
blossoming behind his eyes.

JOHN straightens his back. Pulls her to him.

TIME STANDS STILL, the lights fade.
A spotlight hits him and SARAH. MUSIC starts.

THE ARGENTINE TANGO.

JOHN and SARAH launch into a dance. It's not perfect, it's
real. They are in tune with each other. Moving in rhythm.

Circling each other. For a moment, transported back to their
tango lessons years before. We could be watching two young
dancers in their prime.

We focus on the details. Their touches. Their feet moving.

A hypnotism to the dance.

The dance of two lovers.

Eventually, the dance ends with JOHN and SARAH face-to-face.
John's hand fans across the small of Sarah's back, pressing her
to him.

JOHN gazes into her eyes. In a whisper:

                  JOHN
                I miss you.

He smiles.

BACK TO SCENE

```

```
Across the busy hall, by a table serving teas and coffees,
KEITH looks around the room for JOHN. He finds him sitting on
his own some distance away. As still as a statue. A faraway
look in his eyes.

KEITH approaches. Sits beside his father, hands him a beaker of
coffee. JOHN snaps out of his dream.

               JOHN (CONT'D)
                 Cheers.

                  KEITH
Listen, Dad, to be honest, watching these kids dance... it's a
                 bit...

He pulls a face.

               KEITH (CONT'D)
 If you want me to run you up to the game after Ry's dance...
                 (MORE)

               KEITH (CONT'D)
   He won't mind. You could still make the second half.

His mind miles away, JOHN shakes his head. A slight shrug.

Across the hall, RYAN appears with DEBRA. Both dressed in their
ballroom gear. Matching numbers on their backs.

RYAN eagerly searches the room, finds his dad and granddad.
Gives them a nervous smile.

KEITH smiles proudly.

JOHN gives a simple wave. A thumbs-up. RYAN's confidence grows
as he takes DEBRA in his arms and prepares for his dance.

KEITH looks back at JOHN. To his surprise, his father has a
tear in his eye.

A beat. KEITH knows his father's not going anywhere. He pats
his dad's knee. A smile flickers across JOHN's lips.

Music starts up. Ryan and Debra begin their dance. And as the
world before them becomes a blur of lights and colours, we MOVE

```

```
IN on KEITH and JOHN...

Father and son, side by side. Both remembering a mother, a
wife, a friend.

Both wearing the same crooked grin. Out on JOHN...

                               DISSOLVE TO:

EXT. HERNE BAY, RUINED PIER - NIGHT

The derelict pier. The sun setting behind it...

                                 FADE OUT.

The End.

```

# **APPENDIX II** **MAN VS SAND SCRIPT**

```
                MAN VS SAND

                Written by

              Anthony Fletcher

        Based on an idea by Prano Bailey-Bond

EXT. HOLE, BEACH - DAY

Wide landscape of a beach - a figure digs a hole in the sand.
The sound of his DIGGING and BREATHING can be heard lucidly.

TITLE ON BLACK - MAN vs SAND

PAOLO (30's), a construction worker, is digging a hole. He does
so with a real sense of vigour. Sweat drips from his brow. This
is manual labour and he goes about it with a real sense of
purpose. The hole he's digging seems to take an age to get any
bigger. After a while he pauses, takes a breath and then
continues to dig.

We hear the sound of a RADIO (OS).

EXT. OFFICE, BEACH - DAY

In another part of the same space THE BOSS (40+) is seated at a
desk wearing a suit. A radio plays in his office, full of

```

```
anecdotes of people talking about the economic crisis.

The Boss is meticulously moving individual grains of sand from
one set of scales to another, using a pair of tweezers. It's
painstaking work and he doesn't seem to be up to it; he's had a
long day already. When he tries and fails several times to pick
up the next grain, he throws the tweezers away in disgust and
sits at the desk, his head in his hands.

EXT. HOLE, BEACH - DAY

Paolo is still digging away at the hole. It doesn't look like
it's got any bigger. The Boss approaches.

The Boss watches Paolo dig from one edge of the hole. His foot
pushes some of the sand back into the hole. Paolo doesn't seem
to notice, he's so set on his mission.

EXT. HOME, BEACH - EVENING

Paolo sits at a dining table. To one side is his son, HARRY
(11). They don't speak. Instead they are watching TV.

                                   ON TV:

INT. TV STUDIO

A female TV JOURNALIST is interviewing a male POLITICIAN. We
don't hear what they are saying initially. A title comes up
below the Politician reading 'SIMEONE DI GORGONZOLA - MP'.

At first the discussion seems normal, with the Interviewer
simpering a little. As the Politician answers her question he
gradually becomes more animated, until the scene transforms
into something more bizarre. Strange graphs appear on screen
showing CRISIS against TIME.

The Politician pulls out an onion and, chopping it in half,
seems to be using it to explain the economy. The graphics
feature more lines such as MONEY and the lines jet off in
nonsensical directions.

```

```
EXT. HOME, BEACH - EVENING

Paolo and his son watching the scene on the TV stare bewildered.

Paolo's wife, JULIA (30's), enters carrying two plates, which
she places in front of her husband and son. The plates are
filled with sand.

INT. TV STUDIO

The Politician has a plate of spaghetti; he eats it ravenously
whilst the Journalist unbuttons her blouse.

As if another channel were interfering, images of SEXY WOMEN
writhing in front the camera are spliced in as the Politician
throws spaghetti over the Journalist and begins to eat it off
her belly.

EXT. HOME, BEACH - EVENING

Paolo, Julia and Harry attempt to eat the sand on their plates
using knives and forks. Most of the sand falls off the cutlery
back onto the plate. The whole process of something as simple
as eating becomes incredibly complex. Meanwhile the TV keeps
playing.

INT. TV STUDIO

The Politician and TV Journalist suck on the same piece of
spaghetti. Rapid images of women pouting and posing flash
across the screen, intercut with the same graphs seen earlier
in the show.

EXT. HOLE, BEACH - DAY

Paolo is once again digging his hole in the sand. He digs with
a similar energy to the previous time, albeit perhaps he seems
slightly more tired.

```

```
EXT. OFFICE, BEACH - DAY

The Boss is back at the table. He's continuing with his absurd
task but it seems obvious that he's lost his passion. He stares
at the sand, almost hypnotised.

EXT. HOLE, BEACH - EVENING

It's the end of the day. Paolo has gone. The spade sits in the
middle of the hole. The Boss walks over, he gets into the hole
and sits in the middle, staring. The sand intimidates him. The
edges of the hole seem to be closing in on him.

He picks up the spade and tries to balance it on one finger.
Then all of a sudden he starts wielding the spade like a
weapon, hacking at the walls of the hole.

EXT. HOME, BEACH - EVENING

Paolo's family are eating again. It's more sand for supper.
Harry looks disappointed - he clearly doesn't want to eat
anymore sand. In the background the TV is on again.

INT. TV STUDIO

It's the same absurd TV show as the night before. The
Politician is writhing around with some sexy ladies,
pornostyle, having the time of his life as if at a “Bunga
Bunga” party. This is intercut with more graphs.

EXT. HOLE, BEACH - EVENING

The Boss appears to be fighting the sand, and losing. The sand
is falling in on him.

EXT. HOME, BEACH - EVENING

The plates remain unfinished. Julia picks them up and takes
them away.

```

```
INT. TV STUDIO

The scene in the studio has descended into complete farce, with
the sexy girls and the politician rolling around on the floor.
The Politician's head pops up in between a girl's legs,
grinning insanely.

EXT. HOME, BEACH - EVENING

Julia comes back and joins the others watching TV. The family
stare at the TV uncomprehendingly. Only Harry seems to find it
amusing. He laughs. Both parents look at him as though he's
done something wrong. Harry clams up.

INT. TV STUDIO

In his hand the Politician has a bottle of champagne. He pops
the cork, which goes flying - fizz bubbling over in the classic
money shot.

EXT. HOME, BEACH - EVENING

Julia stands up. Walking over to the TV set, she switches it
off. Julia and Paolo look at one another. There's nothing to
eat, and nothing to say.

EXT. HOLE, BEACH - DAY

Paolo arrives for work again. He takes off his jacket and lets
it fall to the floor.

The hole is full.

The Boss is lying on the sand, next to where the hole was. The
spade is missing.

EXT. HOLE, BEACH - DAY, LATER

```

```
Paolo is digging for a third day. Only this time, he's using
his hands.

Paolo keeps digging and digging. The Boss watches on, looking
gloomy, guilty. Julia and Harry appear beside the hole, also
watching him. Harry picks up a handful of sand and playfully
throws it in the hole.

Paolo unearths the spade. He picks it up. The discovery seems
to enthuse him. He starts to dig even more rapidly.

It's as though Paolo has forgotten there's any other way of
living apart from shovelling sand.

Paolo hits something hard with his spade - the bottom. It makes
a noise. He looks up; Julia, Harry and The Boss are watching
him. All of them have shovels in their hands. They start to
fill in the hole.

The sand falls at Paolo's feet. Gradually more and more sand
covering him, up to his knees, his waist, his chest. Paolo
seems only gradually to realise what is actually happening. He
is about to be submerged. As the realisation dawns more and
more strongly his face starts to panic. But he's trapped.
There's no getting out now.

The sand has reached up to Paolo's neck. He has become a
character from a Beckett play. He opens his mouth to speak, but
the sand keeps coming. Soon there will be no more Paolo.

EXT. BEACH - DAY, LATER

Julia and The Boss stand to one side, staring sadly at a mound
of sand on the ground, with a spade sticking out of it. The
hole is gone, and so is Paolo. Julia is crying. She turns to
The Boss, who comforts her. He too looks as though he's on the
point of tears.

EXT. BEACH - DAY - LATER

Harry plays on the mound of sand, which is marked by nothing
more than a spade. He has a kite. But Harry becomes fascinated

```

```
by the spade and lets the kite go.

Harry picks up the spade and starts to dig.

```

# **APPENDIX III** **DYNAMIC QUALITIES OF** **SOUND**














|Quality|Example values|
|---|---|
|Acoustic|dry | wet | clear | resonant|
|Artificiality|organic | synthetic|
|Asynchrony (_sound’s connection to the image in_<br>_terms of causality and timing)_|synchronous | asynchronous |<br>connected | disconnected|
|Density|silent | spare | dense|
|Depth|shallow | deep|
|Energy|static | lively | low | high|
|Envelope|slow decay | soft attack | hard attack<br>| lingering release|
|Extension (_sense of scale compared to visual_<br>_framing)_|smaller | larger | tighter | wider |<br>flatter | deeper|
|Harmoniousness|dissonant | harmonious | minor |<br>major|
|Immersion|faraway | observational | immersive|
|Length|short | long|
|Level/volume|low | high | soft | loud|


|Musicality|noisy | tonal | broadband |<br>narrowband|
|---|---|
|Narrative style|realist | hyperreal | surreal|
|Narrative tone|light | dark | comedic|
|Pace|slow | fast|
|Perspective|far | close | observational | subjective|
|Pitch|low | high|
|Rhythm|regular | irregular | threes|
|Technological feel|analogue | digital|
|Timbre|dull | bright | cold | warm|
|Transition|smooth | sharp | soft | hard|
|Width|narrow | wide|

# **GLOSSARY**

**5.1** Surround mix format involving a front three (L C R), a rear pair (Ls Rs) and a subwoofer
(LFE).
**7.1** Surround sound format that adds an additional two surround channels to 5.1.
**1AS** The first assistant sound role in the production sound team.
**1st AD** The person responsible for timekeeping, morale and discipline on set.
**2-beep** A 1kHz sine wave that syncs with the ‘2’ of a film’s count-in clock to confirm correct
alignment.
**2-pop** _See_ 2-beep.
**2-track mix** A stereo (LR) mix that the production mixer creates live on set.
**2AS** The second assistant sound role in the production sound team.
**AAF/OMF** Interchange formats for transferring a tracklay from one software platform to
another.
**Acousmatic** A sound that is heard without its source being visible.
**Acousmêtre** An acousmatic entity, which derives a mysterious set of powers by being
heard but not seen.
**Active listening** The process of focusing on sound directly, rather than letting it wash over
us in the way audiences do.
**ADR** Automated dialogue replacement – a method for adding or replacing dialogue in postproduction.
**Alts** Alternative takes _,_ mainly used with reference to dialogue recordings.
**Ambience** _See_ atmos.
**Ambisonics** A 3D surround format, which provides a huge amount of flexibility in post.
**Amplitude** The amount of energy a sound wave has, resulting in how loudly or quietly it is
perceived.
**Animatic** A sequence of storyboard frames edited along a timeline, allowing us to
experiment with overlaying sound.
**Asynchrony** A disparity between what is seen and what is heard.
**Atmos** The sounds of an environment or setting, providing a sound bed within which the
other elements of the tracklay may sit.


**Binaural** A stereo capture technique that is able to reproduce 3D localization effects when
recordings are listened back to on headphones.
**Boom pole** A lightweight extendable pole, operated by the 1AS, which supports a wired or
wireless boom mic.
**Burnt-in timecode** Usually known as BITC (pronounced ‘bit-sea’), an overlay on to a
picture export, which stamps each frame with its timecode as a reference.


**Cam hop** A device that wirelessly ‘hops’ audio from recorder to camera, to combine sound
and image within the rushes.
**Cardioid** Mic pick-up pattern, so called for its resemblance to a heart, picking up more from
the front and less from the back.
**Clapperboard** _See_ Slate.
**Clip gain** A software method for increasing a sound’s volume without affecting the
automation settings of the underlying track.
**Clipping** A form of distortion in which the input signal is too loud to be contained within the
digital space of the recording format.
**Cocktail party effect** Psychoacoustic phenomenon which allows us to focus in on single
elements within a sound mass, such as a conversation at a crowded party.
**Codec** An encoding/decoding algorithm for storing and playing back digital audio or video.
**Comb-filter effect** An acoustic phenomenon in which certain frequencies build up, or
cancel each other out.
**Comp** Composite edit made from two or more takes, commonly used when building an edit
of a line by joining together sections of different takes.
**Compression** A compressor is a device that provides an automated volume control.
Whenever an input signal runs above a specified threshold, the level is reduced by a certain
amount, known as gain reduction.
**Conform** The process of replacing the lower-quality proxy files used in edit with the fullquality versions. This commonly applies to the picture edit, but a dialogue editor may follow
a similar process to rebuild the iso tracks from a set of edited 2-track mixes.
**Consolidated** When transferring tracklays using an interchange format, the media are
consolidated by being chopped down to only the parts used in the edit. This makes for
smaller file sizes, but it is usually a good idea to include handles of two to ten seconds to
allow edit tweaks down the line.
**Constructive interference** _See_ Wave interference.
**Contact mic** A microphone that picks up the physical vibrations of objects and surfaces.
**Count-in clock** On the working copy of a film, the editor will usually include a visual
countdown, with a 2-beep timed with the ‘2’ as a sync check fail-safe.
**Crossfade** A fade between two overlapping audio clips on the same track.
**Cue** An element that must be created for a film – may apply to ADR, sfx or music.
**Cue sheet** A table containing all the given cues that must be made across each category of
sound.


**DAW** Digital audio workstation – a general name for multi-track sound-editing applications.
**Destructive interference** _See_ Wave interference.
**Diegetic sound** Sound waves that exist within the world of the film, that the characters are
able to hear.
**DME** _See_ Mix stems.


**Echo chamber** A room designed to facilitate easy worldizing, often found in music studios.
**EDL** Edit decision list, detailing all the cuts that make up a particular version of the edit.
**Equalization (EQ)** A process for changing the tonal balance of an input signal.
**Expansion** Whilst a compressor makes louder sounds quieter, an expander makes softer
sounds even quieter. Particularly useful as part of a noise-reduction processing chain.


**Extension** The ability of a sound to expand the sense of space created by the frame,
creating an off-screen world of any desired size.


**Fill** Clean moments of background that can be used to plug holes in a dialogue edit.
**First** _See_ 1st AD.
**Fitting** The editing of ADR or foley performances into tight sync with the image.
**Foley** Technique of recording sound effects to picture.
**Frame rate** The speed at which the still images move from one to the next to create the
illusion of continuous movement, specified in frames per second (FPS).
**Futzing** The degradation of recordings to emulate a particular kind of technology.


**Geophone** A type of contact mic designed to pick up the low frequencies of the Earth’s
seismic activity.


**Hertz** Basic unit of frequency.
**Hydrophone** A waterproof contact mic for underwater recording.


**Iso tracks** The production mixer’s channels, laid down as individual channels on the
recorder.


**Jam sync** A basic way of syncing up camera and recorder by briefly connecting a cable
between them to align their internal clocks.
**Limiting** A more extreme form of compression, with many limiters preventing an input signal
from ever going above a threshold, termed as ‘brick wall limiting’.


**Looping** _See_ ADR.
**Low-frequency effects (LFE)** Also known as the rumble track, this channel is fed directly to
the cinema’s subwoofer.


**M-S (mid-side)** Mic technique that captures recordings in a way that is both mono and
stereo compatible.
**M&E** A music and effects mix, used for dubbing a film into additional languages.
**Mix splits** _See_ Mix stems.
**Mix stems** An export of the final mix as layers, or stems, usually split between dialogue,
music and effects (DME).
**Mood film** A sequence of moving or still images, often made from a film’s mood boards or
camera tests, to which a designer can add thematic sound ideas.
**MOS** An indication on a shot list that a setup is to be captured without sound. The origins of
the acronym are disputed.
**Motif** A recurring element that first establishes a specific meaning, which can later be
recalled in the audience’s emotional memory.


**Non-diegetic sound** Sounds that the characters in the scene are unable to hear, which
have been added for the benefit of the audience only.


**Panning** Spreading sound layers around the speakers in a stereo or surround mix.
**Picture lock** The final version of a film’s cut, crystallizing the precise sequence of frames.
**Plant mics** Mics hidden in a set to get closer to certain elements of action that the boom
mic cannot reach.


**Post-syncing** The replacing of a film’s sound in post-production.
**Prelap** A sound edit that comes in before the associated image. Postlap could be used to
describe the opposite effect, although this phrase is in less common usage.


**Radio mic** Usually refers to a wireless lavalier mic, although radio boom mics are also
commonly used.
**Re-recording mix** The bringing together of all the elements created for a film, to produce
the final mix.
**Recce** A scout of a location to explore areas of interest, and flag any technical issues.
**Reconform** If the picture edit is updated once the sound edit is already under way, the
audio team must resync their tracks to reflect any changes in the image.
**Resonance** The effect of certain frequencies, or pitches, building up due to reflections that
add together ( _see_ Wave interference).
**Reverb** An acoustic effect that happens when individual echoes bounce back and forth
between surfaces, forming a ‘tail’ of decaying sound, which continues after the original
sound stops.


**Sample rate** The number of times per second a sound wave’s state is captured in digital
recordings.
**Slate** A physical device that produces an audio-visual ‘clap’ at the start or end of a take to
enable syncing in post. Information about each camera setup and take number is also
written on to the slate before each shot.
**Sound field** The spread of sound within a space.
**Spectral masking** Where two elements play at similar pitches, they often obscure each
other to some extent.
**Spot effect** The sonic equivalent of spotlights, picking out a visual element on screen.
**Spotting session** A meeting with the director in which each scene is broken down in detail.
**Streamers** _See_ Wipes.
**Subwoofer** A loudspeaker designed to produce very low sub-bass frequencies.
**Sweetening** Using foley to beef up a production sound element.
**Sync plop** _See_ 2-beep.
**Syncitis** A psychological affliction that can happen when we start looking at the relationship
between sounds and images closely for extended periods, resulting in everything looking
out of sync.


**Temporal masking** The effect of a bigger change in what is heard tending to obscure a
smaller one, where two changes happen simultaneously.
**Timecode** A unique timestamp for every frame in a film.
**Tracklay** The process by which audio layers are organized by category and placed on
appropriately labelled tracks.
**Transducer** A loudspeaker that converts an incoming signal into vibrations, which can
cause objects and surfaces to resonate.


**Walla** Indistinct background voices used to provide texture in crowd scenes.
**Wave interference** The acoustic phenomenon of certain frequencies building up
(constructive interference) or cancelling out (destructive interference) within a sound wave.
**Wild tracks** Any sounds captured on set whilst the cameras are not rolling.


**Wipes** Visual indicators overlaid on to the image to cue a foley artist or ADR performer.
**Workflow** The ordering and configuring of post-production tasks into a connected whole.
**Worldizing** A technique in which sound is played back into the world, and re-recorded, to
achieve realistic acoustic effects.
